### 3.p115 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p115-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On the word "having taken" (gahetvā). The meditator, having approached a good friend as described, should ask for the meditation subject with a well-formed intention and resolve, first surrendering himself (attā niyyātetabbo) to the Buddha or to his teacher. To the Buddha: "Blessed One, I give up this self of mine to you." If he has not done so and lives in remote lodgings, he may be unable to stand fast when a terrifying object comes within range; he may go back to the village, mix with householders, take wrong ways of seeking, and meet misfortune. For one who has surrendered himself, fear does not arise when a terrifying object appears.

> gahetvā ti imassa pana padassa ayam atthaparidīpanā4:  
tena yoginā kammaṭṭhānadāyakaṃ kalyāṇamittaṃ upasan-  
kamitvā ti ettha vuttanayen'; eva vuttappakāraṃ kalyāṇa-  
mittaṃ upasankamitvā, buddhassa vā bhagavato ācari-  
yassa vā, attānaṃ niyyātetvā sampannajjhāsayena sampan-  
nādhimuttinā ca hutvā kammaṭṭhānaṃ yācitabbaṃ|  
     tattha:5 imāhaṃ bhagavā attabhāvaṃ tumhākaṃ paric-  
cajāmī ti evaṃ buddhassa bhagavato attā niyyātetabbo|  
evaṃ hi aniyyātetvā pantesu senāsanesu viharanto, bhera-  
vārammaṇe āpātham āgate santhambhituṃ asakkonto gāman-  
taṃ osaritvā gihīhi saṃsaṭṭho hutvā anesanaṃ āpajjitvā  
anayavyasanaṃ āpajjeyya|6 niyyātitattabhāvassa pan'  
assa bheravārammaṇe āpātham āgate pi bhayaṃ na uppaj-

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, teacher-transmission_

practices: [Good friendship (kalyāṇamittatā), the teacher who gives the subject](../practices/kalyanamittata.md)

### 3.p115 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p115-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On the beneficial result of that surrender. When the meditator considers "I have already given this self to the Buddhas", gladness alone arises, like a man who has fine Benares cloth eaten by mice or insects and feels displeasure, but if he gives it to a monk with no robe and sees the monk cutting it up, feels only gladness. So too for the teacher: one says "Venerable sir, I give up this self to you". One who has not surrendered himself is hard to admonish, unyielding, self-willed, goes where he pleases without asking his teacher; the teacher does not help such a one with material things or Dhamma nor teach him the secret texts, and lacking both kinds of help he finds no footing in the dispensation and soon comes to bad conduct or lay life. (The passage continues on p. 116.)

### 3.p116 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p116"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high · _restricted: summary only_

But one who has surrendered himself is not hard to admonish, does not go where he likes, is easy to speak to and lives dependent on the teacher; receiving both kinds of help from the teacher he attains growth, increase and abundance in the dispensation, like the pupils of the elder Cūḷapiṇḍapātika-Tissa. Three monks came to that elder. One said, when told "for your sake," that he would fall from a precipice a hundred men deep; the second, that he would wear this body away entirely by rubbing it on a rock from the heels up; the third, that he would stop his in-breaths and out-breaths and die. The elder said these monks were capable and gave them a subject; keeping his advice all three reached arahatship. This is the benefit of surrender.

### 3.p116 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p116-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On "with a fulfilled disposition and fulfilled resolve". The meditator should have a disposition (ajjhāsaya) fulfilled in six ways, beginning with non-greed. A saying is quoted: six dispositions ripen the bodhisattvas' awakening: disposition of non-greed (seeing fault in greed), of non-hate (fault in hate), of non-delusion (fault in delusion), of renunciation (fault in household life), of seclusion (fault in company), and of escape (fault in every kind of existence). All the stream-enterers, once-returners, non-returners, taint-destroyers, paccekabuddhas and fully awakened Buddhas, past, future and present, have reached what each was to reach by these same six ways; so the meditator should have a disposition fulfilled by them.

> sampannajjhāsayena sampannādhimuttinā ca hutvā ti ettha  
pana tena yoginā alobhādīnaṃ vasena chahi ākārehi sam-  
pannajjhāsayena bhavitabbaṃ|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

### 3.p116 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p116-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

And by resolve (adhimutti) the meditator should be one whose resolve is fulfilled, resolved on concentration, honouring concentration, sloping toward concentration (continued on p. 117: toward nibbāna).

> tad-adhimuttatāya pana adhimuttisampannena bhavitab-  
baṃ| samādhādhimuttena samādhigarukena samādhipab-

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

### 3.p117 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p117"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Resolve completed: the meditator should be sloping toward nibbāna, honouring and resolved on nibbāna. To one so disposed who asks for a subject, the teacher who has the knowledge of others' minds looks at the mind's behaviour and knows the temperament; another teacher should ask: "What is your temperament? Which states occur to you most? What is comfortable for you when you attend? To which subject does your mind incline?" and learn it so. Having known it, he teaches the subject that suits the temperament.

> bhārena, nibbānādhimuttena nibbānagarukena nibbānapab-

dhimuttino pan'; assa kammaṭṭhānaṃ yācato cetopariyāya-

tabbā| itarena: kiṃ carito 'si? ke vā te dhammā bahulaṃ  
samudācaranti? kiṃ vā te manasikaroto phāsu hoti? kata-  
rasmiṃ vā te kammaṭṭhāne cittaṃ namatī ti evam ādīhi  
nayehi pucchitvā jānitabbā| evaṃ ñatvā cariyānukulaṃ  
kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetabbaṃ|

### 3.p117 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p117-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The teacher should teach in three ways: to one who has learned the subject before, give it in one or two sittings for recitation; to one living near, teach it whenever he comes; to one who has learned and wishes to go elsewhere, teach neither too briefly nor too fully. For the earth kasiṇa, nine matters are taught: the four faults of the kasiṇa, making it, the method of developing what is made, the twofold sign, twofold concentration, sevenfold suitability and unsuitability, tenfold skill in absorption, balancing energy, and the way of absorption. For other subjects too what fits each is taught; all this will appear in the method of development for each.

kathetabbaṃ| pakatiyā uggahitakammaṭṭhānassa ekaṃ

tabbaṃ| tattha pathavīkasiṇaṃ tāva kathentena cattāro

nimittaṃ, duvidho samādhi, sattavidhaṃ sappāyāsappāyaṃ,

ti ime nava ākārā kathetabbā| sesakammaṭṭhānesu pi tassa  
tassa anurūpaṃ kathetabbaṃ| taṃ sabbaṃ tesaṃ bhāva-

### 3.p117 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p117-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Concluding the chapter. The meditator, while the subject is being taught, should take the sign and listen: "this is the earlier word, this the later, this is its meaning, this its intention, this its simile", binding it in each aspect; one who listens carefully in this way gets the subject well grasped, and through it attains distinction, not otherwise. The words "having approached a good friend, taking among the forty subjects one suited to his temperament" are thus set out fully in every respect. The colophon names this the third chapter, "the description of taking a meditation subject", of the section on developing concentration in the Visuddhimagga, made for the joy of good people.

### 3.p84 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p84"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter III opens. Concentration (samādhi) was named, under the heading of consciousness (citta), in the verse "established in virtue, a wise man develops consciousness and understanding"; so it is to be developed. But stated so briefly it is hard even to understand, let alone develop. To show its detail and the method of developing it, eight questions are set: what is concentration; in what sense is it so called; what are its mark, function, manifestation and proximate cause; how many kinds; what is its defilement; what is its cleansing; how is it developed; what is the benefit of developing it.

> tasmā tassa vitthārañ ca bhāvanānayañ ca das-  
setuṃ idaṃ pañhākammaṃ hoti:-  
     (1) ko samādhi?  
     (2) ken'; aṭṭhena samādhi?  
     (3) kān'; assa lakkhaṇa-rasa-paccupaṭṭhāna-padaṭṭhānāni?  
     (4) katividho samādhi?  
     (5) ko c'; assa sankileso?  
     (6) kiṃ vodānaṃ?  
     (7) kathaṃ bhāvetabbo?  
     (8) samādhibhāvanāya ko ānisaṃso ti?

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind, practice_

terms: [samādhi](../terms/samadhi.md)

### 3.p84 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p84-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Answering the first two questions. Concentration is of many kinds, and to set out all of them would not serve the purpose and would only lead to distraction; so only the kind meant here is stated: wholesome one-pointedness of consciousness (kusalacittekaggatā) is concentration. It is so called in the sense of concentrating (samādhāna), which is the even and right placing (ādhāna) and steadying of consciousness and its concomitants on one object.

> (1) tatr'; idaṃ vissajjanaṃ:- ko samādhī ti? samādhi  
bahuvidho nānappakārako| taṃ sabbaṃ vibhāvayituṃ  
ārabhamānaṃ vissajjanaṃ adhippetañ c'; eva atthaṃ na  
sādheyya uttariñ ca vikkhepāya saṃvatteyya, tasmā idhā-  
dhippetam eva sandhāya vadāma:- kusalacittekaggatā sa-  
mādhi|  
     (2) ken'; aṭṭhena samādhī ti? samādhānaṭṭhena samādhi|  
kim idaṃ samādhānaṃ nāma? ekārammaṇe cittacetasi-  
kānaṃ samaṃ sammā ca ādhānaṃ ṭhapanan ti vuttaṃ hoti;

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind_

terms: [samādhi](../terms/samadhi.md)

### 3.p85 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p85"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Concentration is that state by whose power consciousness and its concomitants remain evenly and rightly on one object, undistracted and unscattered. Its mark (lakkhaṇa) is non-distraction; its function (rasa) is to eliminate distraction; its manifestation (paccupaṭṭhāna) is non-wavering; and, since "the mind of one who is happy is concentrated", its proximate cause (padaṭṭhāna) is happiness (sukha).

> tasmā yassa dhammassānubhāvena ekārammaṇe cittacetasikā  
samaṃ sammā ca avikkhipamānā1 avippakiṇṇā ca hutvā  
tiṭṭhanti, idaṃ samādhānan ti veditabbaṃ|  
     (3) kān'; assa lakkhaṇa-rasa-paccupaṭṭhāna-padaṭṭhānānī ti?  
ettha pana avikkhepalakkhaṇo samādhi, vikkhepaviddhaṃsa-  
na-raso, avikampana-paccupaṭṭhāno, sukhino cittaṃ samā-  
dhiyati ti2 vacanato pana sukham assa padaṭṭhānaṃ|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind_

terms: [samādhi](../terms/samadhi.md)

### 3.p85 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p85-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Fourth question, how many kinds. By its mark concentration is one. By access and absorption (upacāra, appanā) it is two; likewise by worldly and supramundane, with rapture and without rapture, accompanied by happiness and by equanimity. It is three by inferior, middling and superior, and likewise by with/without applied and sustained thought, accompanied by rapture, and limited/exalted/measureless. It is four by painful or pleasant practice with slow or quick direct-knowledge, and in other ways; five by the five jhāna factors in the fivefold reckoning.

> (4) katividho samādhī ti avikkhepalakkhaṇena tāva eka-  
vidho, upacāra-appanāvasena duvidho; tathā lokiyā-lokut-  
taravasena, sappītikanippītikavasena, sukhasahagata-upek-  
khāsahagatavasena ca| tividho hīna-majjhima-paṇītavase-  
na; tathā savitakka-savicārādivasena, pītisahagatādivasena,  
paritta-mahaggatappamāṇavasena ca| catubbidho dukkhā-  
paṭipadā-dandhābhiññādivasena; tathā paritta-parittāram-  
maṇādivasena, catujhānangavasena,3 hānabhāgiyādivasena,  
kāmāvacarādivasena, adhipativasena ca| pañcavidho pañca-  
kanaye pañcajhānangavasenāti4|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind_

terms: [samādhi](../terms/samadhi.md), [upacāra-samādhi](../terms/upacara-samadhi.md), [appanā-samādhi](../terms/appana-samadhi.md)

### 3.p85 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p85-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Explaining the twofold division. Access concentration (upacāra-samādhi) is the one-pointedness obtained by way of the six grounds of recollection, mindfulness of death, the recollection of peace, the perception of repulsiveness in nutriment and the defining of the four elements, together with the one-pointedness in the stage preceding the absorption concentrations. Because of the saying "the preparation for the first jhāna is a condition, as proximity condition, for the first jhāna", the one-pointedness that immediately follows the preparation is absorption concentration (appanā-samādhi). Thus concentration is twofold as access and absorption.

> tattha ekavidha-koṭṭhāso uttānattho yeva|  
     duvidha-koṭṭhāse: channaṃ anussatiṭṭhānānaṃ maraṇa-  
satiyā,5 upasamānussatiyā, āhāre paṭikūlasaññāya6 catu-  
dhātuvavatthānassā ti imesaṃ vasena laddhacittekaggatā,  
yā ca appanā-samādhīnaṃ pubbabhāge ekaggatā:- ayaṃ  
upacārasamādhi| paṭhamassa jhānassa parikammaṃ paṭha-  
massa jhānassa anantarapaccayena paccayo ti7 ādi-vacanato  
pana yā parikammānantarā ekaggatā:- ayaṃ appanā-samā-  
dhī ti evaṃ upacārappanā8 vasena duvidho|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind_

terms: [upacāra-samādhi](../terms/upacara-samadhi.md), [appanā-samādhi](../terms/appana-samadhi.md)

### 3.p86 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p86"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Continuing the kinds. With and without rapture: in the fourfold reckoning the one-pointedness in the first two jhānas, and in the fivefold reckoning in the first three, is with rapture; in the remaining two jhānas it is without rapture; access concentration may be either. Accompanied by happiness or by equanimity: in the fourfold reckoning the one-pointedness in three jhānas, and in the fivefold in four, is accompanied by happiness, in the remaining one by equanimity; access may be either. Threefold: merely obtained is inferior, not very well developed is middling, well developed and mastered is superior. With applied and sustained thought (the first-jhāna concentration together with access); with sustained thought only (the second jhāna in the fivefold reckoning, reached by one who sees danger only in applied thought and seeks only its abandoning); without either (the one-pointedness in the second and later jhānas in the fourfold, the third and later in the fivefold reckoning). Accompanied by rapture (in the first two jhānas of the fourfold and the first three of the fivefold reckoning), by happiness (in the third and fourth), by equanimity (in the last); access may be accompanied by rapture and happiness or by equanimity. Limited (one-pointedness in the plane of access), exalted (in wholesome fine-material and immaterial consciousness), measureless (associated with the noble path).

> seseu dvīsu jhānesu ekaggatā nippītiko samādhi; upacāra-  
samādhi pana siyā sappītiko, siyā nippītiko ti evaṃ sappīti-  
ka-nippītikavasena duvidho| catutthaduke catukkanaye tīsu

samādhi pana siyā sukhasahagato, siyā upekkhāsahagato  
ti evaṃ sukhasahagata-upekkhāsahagatavasena duvidho|

majjhimo, subhāvito vasippatto paṇīto ti evaṃ hīna-majjhi-

ādhi saddhiṃ upacārasamādhinā savitakkasavicāro; pañca-

vitakkamatte yeva ādīnavaṃ disvā, vicāre adisvā, kevalaṃ  
vitakkappahānamattaṃ ākankhamāno paṭhamajjhānaṃ atik-

upekkhāsahagato vā ti evaṃ pītisahagatādivasena tividho|  
catutthattike upacārabhūmiyaṃ ekaggatā paritto samādhi,  
rupāvacarārūpāvacarakusale ekaggatā1 mahaggato samādhi,  
ariyamaggasampayuttā ekaggatā appamāṇo samādhī ti evaṃ  
paritta-mahaggatappamāṇavasena tividho|

### 3.p86 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p86-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Fourfold. Concentration may be by painful practice with slow direct-knowledge, painful practice with quick direct-knowledge, pleasant practice with slow, or pleasant practice with quick. From the first attention up to the access of a given jhāna, the development of concentration is called the practice (paṭipadā); from access up to absorption the understanding is called direct-knowledge (abhiññā). The practice is painful for one for whom the hindrances and other opposed states arise and are hard to overcome; it is pleasant for one for whom they do not.

> catukkesu: paṭhamacatukke atthi samādhi dukkhāpaṭi-  
pado dandhābhiñño, atthi dukkhāpaṭipado khippābhiñño, atthi  
sukhāpaṭipado dandhābhiñño, atthi sukhāpaṭipado khippā-  
bhiñño ti|2 tattha paṭhamasamannāhārato paṭṭhāya yāva  
tassa tassa jhānassa upacāraṃ uppajjati, tāva pavattā samā-  
dhibhāvanā paṭipadā ti vuccati| upacārato pana paṭṭhāya  
yāva appanā, tāva pavattā paññā abhiññā ti vuccati| sā  
pan'; esā paṭipadā ekaccassa dukkhā hoti nīvaraṇādi-paccanī-  
kadhammasamudācāragahaṇatāya kicchā; asukhāsevanā ti

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind_

terms: [samādhi](../terms/samadhi.md)

### 3.p87 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p87"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

What makes practice painful or pleasant, direct-knowledge slow or quick. One who uses what is unsuitable (asappāya) has painful practice and slow direct-knowledge; one who uses what is suitable has pleasant practice and quick direct-knowledge; mixed use gives mixed results. One who has not cut the impediments and done the preliminary work has painful practice; one who has not perfected the skills in absorption has slow direct-knowledge. The division may also be seen by craving and ignorance (craving-overpowered: painful; ignorance-overpowered: slow), by earlier work in calm and in insight, and by strong or weak defilements and sharp or dull faculties.

> tattha yāni parato sappāyāsappāyāni ca paḷibodhupacche-  
dādīni1 pubbakiccāni ca appanā-kosallāni ca vaṇṇayissāma2|  
tesu yo asappāyasevī hoti, tassa dukkhāpaṭipadā dandhā ca  
abhiññā hoti, sappāyasevino sukhāpaṭipadā khippā ca  
abhiññā| yo pana pubbabhāge asappāyaṃ sevitvā, apara-  
bhāge sappāyasevī hoti, pubbabhāge vā sappāyaṃ sevitvā,  
aparabhāge asappāyasevī, tassa vomissakatā veditabbā|  
tathā paḷibodhupacchedādikaṃ1 pubbakiccaṃ asampādetvā  
bhāvanam anuyuttassa dukkhāpaṭipadā hoti, vipariyāyena

abhiññā hoti, sampādentassa khippā| api ca  
taṇhā-avijjāvasena, samatha-vipassanādhikāravasena cāpi

padā hoti, anabhibhūtassa sukhā| avijjābhibhūtassa ca  
dandhābhiññā hoti, anabhibhūtassa khippā|  
yo ca samathe akatādhikāro, tassa dukkhāpaṭipadā hoti, ka-  
tādhikārassa sukhā| yo pana vipassanāya akatādhikāro  
hoti, tassa dandhā abhiññā hoti, katādhikārassa khippā|  
     kilesindriyavasena cāpi etāsaṃ pabhedo ve-  
ditabbo| tibbakilesassa hi mudindriyassa dukkhāpaṭipadā  
hoti dandhā ca abhiññā, tikkhindriyassa pana khippā abhiñ-  
ñā| mandakilesassa ca mudindriyassa sukhā paṭipadā hoti  
dandhā ca abhiññā, tikkhindriyassa pana khippā abhiññā ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: causal · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind, practice_

### 3.p87 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p87-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

A person who reaches concentration by painful practice and slow direct-knowledge has concentration called "of painful practice and slow direct-knowledge"; the same applies to the other three. Then the next fourfold division begins: concentration may be limited with a limited object, limited with a measureless object, measureless with a limited object, or measureless with a measureless object.

> iti imāsu paṭipadā-abhiññāsu yo puggalo  
dukkhāya paṭipadāya dandhāya ca abhiññāya samādhiṃ  
pāpuṇāti, tassa so samādhi dukkhāpaṭipado dandhābhiñño  
ti vuccati| esa nayo sesattaye pī ti evaṃ dukkhāpaṭipadā-  
dandhābhiññādivasena catubbidho| dutiya-  
catukke atthi samādhi paritto parittārammaṇo, atthi paritto  
appamāṇārammaṇo, atthi appamāṇo parittārammaṇo, atthi  
appamāṇo appamāṇārammaṇo ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind_

### 3.p88 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p88"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Limited and measureless. Concentration that is not well practised and cannot be a condition for a higher jhāna is limited (paritta); one occurring on an object not enlarged is "with a limited object"; concentration well practised, well developed and able to be a condition for a higher jhāna is measureless (appamāṇa), and one occurring on an enlarged object has a measureless object; the mixed cases are understood by combining these marks. Then fourfold by the jhāna factors: the first jhāna has five factors (applied thought, sustained thought, rapture, happiness, concentration) once the hindrances are suppressed; the second has three (applied and sustained thought stilled); the third two (rapture faded away); the fourth two, by way of concentration accompanied by equanimous feeling, with happiness abandoned. The four concentrations that are factors of these four jhānas make four.

> paritto| yo pana avaḍḍhite ārammaṇe pavatto, ayam  
parittārammaṇo| yo paguṇo subhāvito upari jhānassa  
paccayo bhavituṃ sakkoti, ayaṃ appamāṇo| yo ca vaḍḍhite  
ārammaṇe pavatto, ayaṃ appamāṇārammaṇo| vuttalak-  
khaṇavomissatāya pana vomissakanayo veditabbo| evaṃ  
paritta-parittārammaṇādivasena catubbidho|  
tatiyacatukke vikkhambhitanīvaraṇānaṃ vitakka-vicāra-  
pīti-sukhasamādhīnaṃ vasena pañcangikaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhā-  
naṃ,1 tato vūpasantavitakkavicāraṃ tivangikaṃ dutiyaṃ,  
tato virattappītikaṃ duvangikaṃ tatiyaṃ, tato pahīnasukha-  
upekkhāvedanā-sahitassa samādhino vasena duvangikaṃ  
catutthaṃ:- iti imesaṃ catunnaṃ jhānānaṃ angabhūtā  
cattāro samādhī honti| evaṃ catujhānangavasena catub-

concepts: [The four jhānas](../concepts/four-jhanas.md)

### 3.p88 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p88-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Fourfold as leading to decline, to stability, to distinction and to penetration. The text quotes: for one who has gained the first jhāna, perceptions and attentions accompanied by sensual desire arising is understanding that shares in decline; when mindfulness that accords with it stands firm, it shares in stability; when perceptions and attentions unaccompanied by applied thought arise, it shares in distinction; when perceptions and attentions accompanied by disenchantment and associated with dispassion arise, it shares in penetration. The concentration associated with each such understanding is likewise of four kinds.

> catutthacatukke: atthi samādhi hā-  
nabhāgiyo, atthi ṭhitibhāgiyo, atthi visesabhāgiyo, atthi nibbe-  
dhabhāgiyo| tattha paccanīkasamudācāravasena hānabhā-  
giyatā, tad-anudhammatāya satiyā saṇṭhānavasena ṭhiti-  
bhāgiyatā, upari visesādhigamavasena visesabhāgiyatā, nib-  
bidāsahagatasaññāmanasikāra-samudācāravasena nibbedha-  
bhāgiyatā ca veditabbā| yath'; āha:2 paṭhamassa jhānassa  
lābhiṃ kāmasahagatā saññā manasikārā-samudācaranti hāna-  
bhāginī paññā| tad-anudhammatā-sati santiṭṭhati ṭhitibhā-  
ginī paññā| avitakkasahagatā saññā manasikārā samudāca-  
ranti visesabhāginī paññā| nibbidāsahagatā saññā manasi-  
kārā samudācaranti virāgupasaṃhitāni nibbedhabhāginīpaññā  
ti| tāya pana paññāya sampayuttā samādhī pi cattāro hon-  
tīti| evaṃ hānabhāgiyādivasena catubbidho|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: intermediate · types: consciousness-mind_

### 3.p88 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p88-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Fourfold by plane: concentration of the sense sphere (all access one-pointedness), of the fine-material sphere, of the immaterial sphere, and unincluded (apariyāpanna, the supramundane); the second, third and fourth are wholesome one-pointedness of the fine-material, immaterial and supramundane.

> pañcamacatukke kāmāvacaro samādhi, rūpāvacaro sa-  
mādhi, arūpāvacaro samādhi, apariyāpanno samādhī ti  
evaṃ cattāro samādhī| tattha sabbāpi upacārekaggatā  
kāmāvacaro samādhi| tathā rūpāvacarādi kusalacitt'; ekag-  
gatā itare tayo ti evaṃ kāmāvacarādivasena catubbidho|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: unmarked · types: consciousness-mind_

### 3.p89 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p89"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Fourfold by predominance: concentration gained by making zeal (chanda), energy (viriya), consciousness (citta) or inquiry (vīmaṃsā) the predominant is called zeal-concentration, energy-concentration, consciousness-concentration or inquiry-concentration. In the fivefold count the second jhāna of the fourfold count is divided into two, as second (beyond applied thought only) and third (beyond applied and sustained thought), giving five jhānas.

> ayaṃ vuccati chandasamādhi | | | viriyaṃ ce bhikkhu | | |  
pe||| cittaṃ ce bhikkhu |||pe||| vīmaṃsaṃ ce bhik-  
khu adhipatiṃ karitvā labhati samādhiṃ, labhati cittassa ekag-  
gataṃ:- ayaṃ vuccati vīmaṃsāsamādhī ti1 evaṃ adhipativa-  
sena catubbidho|

vitakkamattātikkamena dutiyaṃ; vitakkavicārātikkamena

### 3.p89 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p89-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Fifth and sixth questions, the defilement and the cleansing of concentration, are answered in the Vibhaṅga: the defilement is the state that shares in decline, the cleansing the state that shares in distinction. For one who has gained the first jhāna, perceptions and attentions accompanied by sensual desire arising is understanding sharing in decline; perceptions and attentions unaccompanied by applied thought arising is understanding sharing in distinction.

> (5) ko c'; assa sankileso? (6) kiṃ vodānan ti ettha pana vissaj-  
janaṃ vibhange vuttam eva| vuttaṃ hi tattha sankilesan  
ti hānabhāgiyo dhammo| vodānan ti visesabhāgiyo dhammo ti3|

nayena hānabhāgiyo dhammo veditabbo| avitakkasahagatā

### 3.p89 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p89-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Seventh question, how concentration is to be developed. The supramundane concentration associated with the noble path is developed by the method of developing understanding, so nothing separate is said. The worldly is developed in this order: having purified virtue and being established in it, cut the impediments that apply, approach a good friend who gives a meditation subject, take from the forty subjects one that suits one's temperament (cariyā), leave a dwelling unsuited to development of concentration and live in a suitable one, cut the minor impediments, and not neglect any of the details of development.

> 7| kathaṃ bhāvetabbo ti ettha pana yo tāva ayaṃ lokiya-  
lokuttara-vasena duvidho ti ādisu ariyamaggasampayutto  
samādhi vutto, tassa bhāvanānayo paññābhāvanānayen'  
eva sangahīto|4 paññāya hi bhāvitāya so bhāvito hoti,  
tasmā taṃ sandhāya evaṃ bhāvetabbo ti na kiñci visuṃ  
vadāma| yo panāyaṃ lokiyo, so vuttanayena sīlāni visodhe-  
tvā, suparisuddhe sīle patiṭṭhitena, yvāssa dasasu paḷibodhesu5  
paḷibodho atthi, taṃ upacchinditvā, kammaṭṭhanadāyakaṃ  
kalyāṇamittaṃ upasankamitvā, attano cariyānukulaṃ cattā-  
ḷīsāya kammaṭṭhānesu aññataraṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā,  
samādhibhāvanāya ananurūpaṃ vihāraṃ pahāya, anurūpe

sabbaṃ bhāvanāvidhānaṃ aparihāpentena bhāvetabbo ti|  
ayam ettha sankhepo|

### 3.p90 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p90"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The ten impediments (palibodha) are given in a verse: dwelling, family, gain, class, and fifth building work; travel, relatives, illness, books, and supernormal power. Each impediment is named by its object.

> āvāso ca kulaṃ lābho, gaṇo kammañ ca pañcamaṃ;  
          addhānaṃ, ñāti, ābādho, gantho, iddhī ti te dasā ti  
ime dasa paḷibodhā nāma|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

terms: [paḷibodha](../terms/palibodha.md) · obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p90 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p90-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

(i) Dwelling. "Dwelling" may be a single room, a single cell-block or a whole monastery, and it is not an impediment for everyone. It is an impediment only for one who is zealous over new building work and the like, or hoards many belongings, or is bound in mind by some concern for it; not for another.

> tattha (i|) āvāso ti eko pi ovarako vuccati, ekam pi parive-  
ṇaṃ, sakalo pi sanghārāmo| svāyaṃ na sabbass'; eva paḷi-  
bodho hoti|  
     yo pan'; ettha navakammādisu ussukkaṃ vā āpajjati, ba-  
hubhaṇḍasannicayo vā hoti, yena kenaci vā ṇena apek-  
khavā1 paṭibaddhacitto,2 tass'; eva paḷibodho hoti, na itarassa|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p90 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p90-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

A story of two clansmen who left Anurādhapura and went forth at Thūpārāma. One, having learned two summaries (mātikā) and kept five rains, went to the eastern district; after long residence there he became an elder and, thinking the place fit for seclusion, went back to invite his friend. The friend, greeting him, looked after him in the proper way. The visitor, on the first night, expected gifts of ghee, sugar-syrup or a drink, and then breakfast; getting none, and after a poor alms round, asked whether the elder always lived like this; on being told yes, he said the eastern district was pleasant and they should go there; the elder left the town by the south gate.

> tatridaṃ vatthu:- dve kira kulaputtā anurādhapurā nik-  
khamitvā anupubbena3 thūpārāme pabbajiṃsu| tesu eko  
dve mātikā paguṇā4 katvā pañcavassiko hutvā pavāretvā

cintesi: paṭisallānasāruppam idaṃ ṭhānaṃ, handa naṃ sa-  
hāyakassāpi ārocemī ti| tato nikkhamitvā anupubbena  
thūpārāmaṃ pāvisi| pavisantaṃ yeva naṃ disvā samāna-  
vassikatthero paccuggantvā pattacīvaraṃ paṭiggahetvā vat-  
taṃ akāsi| āgantukatthero senāsanaṃ pavisitvā cintesi:  
idāni me sahāyo sappiṃ vā phāṇitaṃ vā pānakaṃ vā pesissati,  
ayaṃ hi imasmiṃ nagare ciraṃ nivāsī ti| so rattiṃ aladdhā  
pāto cintesi: idāni upaṭṭhākehi gahitaṃ yāgukhajjakaṃ pesis-  
satī ti| tam pi adisvā: pahiṇantā n'; atthi, paviṭṭhassa

dve ekaṃ vīthiṃ caritvā uḷunkamattaṃ yāguṃ labhitvā  
āsanasālāyaṃ nisīditvā piviṃsu| tato āgantuko cintesi:  
nibaddhayāgu maññe n'; atthi, bhattakāle idāni manussā  
paṇītaṃ bhattaṃ dassantī ti| tato bhattakāle pi piṇḍāya  
caritvā laddham eva bhuñjitvā āha: kiṃ, bhante, sabbakā-  
laṃ evaṃ yāpethā ti? ām', āvuso ti| bhante, pācīna-  
khaṇḍarāji phāsukā, tattha gacchāmā ti| thero nagarato

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, narrative_

### 3.p91 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p91"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The story ends: on the road the visitor found the elder heading for the potter's village and asked why; the elder said the visitor had praised the eastern district. The visitor asked whether in all that time he had no extra belongings; the elder said there was only the Community's bed and stool, put away, and nothing else. The visitor said his own staff, oil-tube and sandal-bag were still there. The elder replied that he (the visitor) had stayed only one day and had already put that much aside. Moved, the visitor praised the elder as one for whom the forest is the abode everywhere, saying "stay right here", and on the second day took his own bowl and robe and went off himself. "For such a one a dwelling is not an impediment."

> dakkhiṇadvārena nikkhamaṇto kumbhakāragāmamaggaṃ  
paṭipajji| itaro āha: kiṃ pana,1 bhante, imaṃ maggaṃ  
paṭipannatthā ti? nanu tvaṃ, āvuso, pācīnakhaṇḍarājiyā  
vaṇṇaṃ abhāsī ti| kiṃ pana, bhante, tumhākaṃ ettakaṃ  
kālaṃ vasitaṭṭhāne na koci atirekaparikkhāro atthī ti|  
ām', āvuso, mañcapīṭhaṃ sanghikaṃ, taṃ paṭisāmitam eva;  
aññaṃ kiñci n'; atthī ti| mayhaṃ pana, bhante, kattara-  
daṇḍo telanāḷi upāhanatthavikā ca tatth'; evā ti| tayā, āvuso,  
ekadivasaṃ vasitvā ettakaṃ ṭhapitan ti| āma, bhante ti|  
so pasannacitto theraṃ vanditvā:- tumhādisānaṃ, bhante,  
sabbattha araññavāso yeva| thūpārāmo catunnaṃ buddhā-  
naṃ dhātunidhānaṭṭhānaṃ, lohapāsāde sappāyaṃ dhamma-  
savanaṃ, mahācetiyadassanaṃ theradassanañ ca labbhati,  
buddhakālo viya pavattati|2 idh'; eva tumhe vasathā ti  
dutiyadivase pattacīvaraṃ gahetvā sayam eva agamāsī ti  
īdisassa āvāso na paḷibodho hoti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, narrative_

### 3.p91 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p91-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

(ii) Family means the family of relatives or of supporters. For some monk even a supporting family is an impediment, when he lives in close association ("they are happy when they are happy"); he does not go even to a nearby monastery to hear the Dhamma without the family's people. For another, even parents are not impediments, like the nephew of the elder at Koraṇḍaka monastery.

> (ii|) kulan ti ñātikulaṃ vā upaṭṭhākakulaṃ vā| ekaccassa  
hi upaṭṭhākakulam pi sukhite sukhito ti ādinā nayena saṃ-  
saṭṭhaviharato3 paḷibodho hoti, so kulamānussakehi vinā  
dhammasavanāya sāmantavihāram pi na gacchati| ekac-  
cassa mātāpitaro pi paḷibodhā na honti, koraṇḍakavihāra-

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p92 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p92"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Continuing the Koraṇḍaka story: the young monk arrived at the monastery on the day the rains-residence began and took the lodging his father had built. His father, hearing that a young visiting monk had it, came and asked whether there was a duty toward a resident in his lodging (eating in his house for three months); the monk stayed silent. The parents' family did not recognise him and he took his alms there for the whole three months without making himself known; at the end, given oil, a lump of sugar and cloth, he left. He met his preceptor on the road and gave him oil for his feet, sugar for a drink, and the cloth, saying Rohaṇa was suited to him. When the preceptor reached Koraṇḍaka the mother, seeing him alone, wept, thinking her son was dead, and the elder told her that the young monk, of few wishes, had not made himself known.

> so vassūpanāyikadivase yeva taṃ vihāraṃ patto| senāsa-  
nam pi 'ssa pitarā kāritam eva pattaṃ| ath'; assa pitā  
dutiyadivase āgantvā: kassa, bhante, amhākaṃ senāsanaṃ  
pattan ti pucchanto: āgantukassa1 daharassā ti sutvā taṃ  
upasankamitvā vanditvā āha: bhante, amhākaṃ senāsane  
vassaṃ upagatassa vattaṃ atthī ti| kiṃ, upāsakā ti?  
temāsaṃ amhākaṃ yeva ghare bhikkhaṃ gahetvā pavāretvā  
gamanakāle āpucchitabban ti| so tuṇhībhāve na adhivāsesi|  
upāsako pi gharaṃ gantvā: amhākaṃ āvāse eko āgantuko  
ayyo upagato sakkaccaṃ upaṭṭhātabbo ti āha| upāsikā:  
sādhū ti sampaṭicchitvā paṇītaṃ khādanīyaṃ bhojanīyaṃ  
paṭiyādesi| daharo pi bhattakāle ñātigharaṃ agamāsi; na  
naṃ koci sañjāni| so temāsam pi tattha piṇḍapātaṃ pari-  
bhuñjitvā, vassaṃ2 vuttho: ahaṃ gacchāmī ti āpucchi| ath'  
assa ñātakā: sve, bhante, gacchissathā ti3 dutiyadivase ghare  
yeva bhojetvā telanāḷiṃ pūretvā ekaṃ guḷapiṇḍaṃ navahat-  
thañ ca sāṭakaṃ datvā: gacchatha, bhante ti āhaṃsu| so  
anumodanaṃ katvā rohaṇābhimukho pāyāsi| upajjhāyo  
pi 'ssa pavāretvā paṭipathaṃ āgacchanto pubbe diṭṭhaṭṭhāne  
yeva taṃ addasa| so aññatarasmiṃ rukkhamūle therassa  
vattaṃ akāsi| atha naṃ thero pucchi: kiṃ, bhadramukha!4  
diṭṭhā te upāsikā ti? so: āma, bhante ti sabbaṃ pavattiṃ  
ārocetvā, tena telena therassa pāde makkhetvā guḷena pāna-  
kaṃ katvā pāyetvā5 tam pi sāṭakaṃ therass'; eva datvā the-  
raṃ vanditvā: mayhaṃ, bhante, rohaṇaṃ yeva sappāyan ti  
agamāsi| thero pi vihāraṃ āgantvā dutiyadivase koraṇḍa-  
kagāmaṃ pāvisi| upāsikā pi: mayhaṃ bhātā mama puttaṃ  
gahetvā, idāni āgacchissatī ti6 sadā maggaṃ olokayamānā  
va tiṭṭhati| sā taṃ ekakam eva āgacchantaṃ disvā: mato  
me maññe putto! ayaṃ7 thero ekako va āgacchatī ti therassa  
pādamūle nipatitvā8 paridevamānā rodi| thero: nanu da-  
haro9 appicchatāya attānaṃ ajānāpetvā va gato ti taṃ10 sam-

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, narrative_

### 3.p93 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p93"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The story ends: the elder consoled her, told her everything and showed the cloth from his bowl-bag. The mother, gladdened, lay face down toward the direction her son had gone and, paying homage, said that the Blessed One must have taught the Rathavinīta practice, the Nālaka practice, the Tuvaṭaka practice and the great Noble-lineage practice, which show contentment with the four requisites and delight in development, taking a monk like her son as a body-witness; she marvelled that one who ate for three months in the house of the mother who bore him never said "I am the son, you are the mother". Even the parents of such a monk are not impediments, much less another family of supporters.

> assāsetvā sabbaṃ pavattiṃ ārocetvā pattatthavikato taṃ  
sāṭakaṃ nīharitvā dassesi|1 upāsikā pasīditvā puttena  
gatadisābhimukhā urena nipajjitvā namassamānā āha:  
mayhaṃ puttasadisaṃ vata maññe bhikkhuṃ kāyasakkhiṃ  
katvā bhagavā rathavinītapaṭipadaṃ2 nāḷakapaṭipadaṃ3  
tuvaṭakapaṭipadaṃ4 catupaccayasantosa-bhāvanārāmatā-  
dīpakaṃ| mahā-ariyavaṃsapaṭipadañ5 ca desesi| vijāta-  
mātuyā nāma gehe temāsaṃ bhuñjamāno pi: 6ahaṃ putto,  
tvaṃ mātā ti na vakkhati! aho! acchariyamanusso ti|  
evarūpassa mātāpitaro pi paḷibodhā na honti

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, narrative_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p93 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p93-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

(iii) Gain means the four requisites. For a monk of merit, people give requisites in great number wherever he goes; he thanks them and teaches, and gets no time for the ascetic's work; from dawn people crowd him, and the alms-seekers come at first light to say that lay supporters wish to see him. He is always busy, and for him the requisites are an impediment. He should leave the company and wander alone where he is not known; so that impediment is cut.

> (iii|) lābho ti cattāro paccayā| te kathaṃ paḷibodhā  
honti? puññavantassa hi bhikkhuno gatagataṭṭhāne ma-  
nussā mahāparivāre paccaye denti| so tesaṃ anumodento,  
dhammaṃ desento, samaṇadhammaṃ kātuṃ na okāsaṃ  
labhati| aruṇuggamanato yāva paṭhamayāmo, tāva manus-  
sasaṃsaggo na upacchijjati| puna balavapaccūse yeva  
bāhulikapiṇḍapātikā7 āgantvā: bhante, asuko upāsako upā-  
sikā amacco amaccadhītā tumhākaṃ dassanakāmā8 ti vadanti|  
so: gaṇha, āvuso, pattacīvaran ti gamanasajjo va hotī ti nic-  
cavyāvaṭo, tass'; eva9 te paccayā paḷibodhā honti| tena  
gaṇaṃ pahāya, yattha naṃ na jānanti, tattha ekakena cari-  
tabbaṃ| evaṃ so paḷibodho upacchijjatī ti10|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p93 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p93-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

(iv) Class (gaṇa) means a group of pupils in the discourses or in the Abhidhamma. It is an impediment for the teacher who gives recitation or questioning and so gets no time for the ascetic's work. He should cut it thus: if the monks have learned much and little remains, he finishes it and goes into the forest.

> (iv|) gaṇo ti suttantikagaṇo vā ābhidhammikagaṇo vā,  
yo tassa uddesaṃ vā paripucchaṃ vā dento samaṇadhammassa  
okāsaṃ na labhati, tass'; eva11 gaṇo paḷibodho hoti; tena so  
evaṃ upacchinditabbo| sace tesaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ bahuṃ  
gahitaṃ12 hoti, appaṃ avasiṭṭhaṃ, taṃ niṭṭhapetvā araññaṃ  
pavisitabbaṃ| sace appaṃ gahitaṃ,13

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p94 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p94"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

If little has been taught and much remains, he should not go beyond a yojana but approach another teacher within that limit and ask that that venerable one take over the recitation; if he cannot get this, he tells them he has a task and they may go where they wish, and he leaves the class to do his own work. (v) Building work is an impediment because the builder must learn what is got and not got, and press on with what is done; it is to be cut by finishing a little that remains, or handing much to the Community or those who bear its burden, or, if his own, to those who bear his; if none is found, he gives it up to the Community and goes. (vi) Travel: if one is waiting for someone's ordination or some requisite, the wish to go is hard to dispel even in the forest, so he should go, finish the matter, and only then be zealous in the ascetic's work.

> yojanato paraṃ agantvā antoyojanaparicchede aññaṃ gaṇa-  
vācakaṃ upasankamitvā: ime āyasmā uddesādīhi sangaṇha-  
tū ti vattabbaṃ| evam pi1 alabhamānena: mayhaṃ, āvuso,  
ekaṃ kiccaṃ atthi; tumhe yathā-phāsukaṭṭhānāni gacchathā  
ti gaṇaṃ pahāya attano kammaṃ kātabban ti2|  
     (v|) kamman ti navakammaṃ| taṃ karontena vaḍḍhakī  
ādīhi laddhāladdhaṃ jānitabbaṃ, katakate ussukkaṃ āpajji-  
tabban ti sabbadā3 paḷibodho hoti| so pi evaṃ upacchindi-  
tabbo: sace appaṃ avasiṭṭhaṃ hoti, niṭṭhapetabbaṃ| sace  
bahuṃ4 sanghikaṃ ce5 navakammaṃ, sanghassa vā sangha-  
bhārahārakabhikkhūnaṃ vā niyyādetabbaṃ; attano santa-  
kaṃ ce, attano bhārahārakānaṃ niyyādetabbaṃ| tādise  
alabhantena sanghassa pariccajitvā gantabban ti|  
     (vi|) addhānan ti maggagamanaṃ| yassa hi katthaci  
pabbajjāpekkho vā hoti, paccayajātaṃ vā kiñci laddhabbaṃ  
hoti, sace taṃ alabhanto na sakkoti adhivāsetuṃ, araññaṃ  
pavisitvā samaṇadhammaṃ karontassa pi gamikacittaṃ  
nāma duppaṭivinodanīyaṃ6 hoti, tasmā gantvā taṃ kiccaṃ  
tīretvā va samaṇadhamme ussukkaṃ kātabban ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

### 3.p94 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p94-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

(vii) Relatives. In the monastery these are teacher, preceptor, pupils and others; at home mother, father, brother and so on. When they are ill they are an impediment to him, so he should cut it by attending them until they are restored to health.

> (vii|) ñātī ti vihāre ācariyupajjhāya-saddhivihārika-ante-  
vāsikasamānupajjhāyaka-samānācariyakā, ghare mātā pitā  
bhātā ti evam ādikā, te gilānā imassa paḷibodhā honti, tasmā  
so paḷibodho upaṭṭhahitvā tesaṃ pākatikakaraṇena upacchin-  
ditabbo|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, ethics_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p94 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p94-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

If the preceptor is ill and does not quickly recover, he should be looked after even for life; so too a teacher of going-forth or of full ordination, a pupil, and those ordained or gone forth by him or sharing his preceptor.

> tattha7 upajjhāyo tāva gilāno, sace lahuṃ na  
vuṭṭhāti, yāvajīvam pi paṭijaggitabbo| tathā pabbajjā-  
cariyo, upasampadācariyo, saddhivihāriko, upasampādita-  
pabbājita8-antevāsika-samānupajjhāyakā ca|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: ethics, practice_

### 3.p95 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p95"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Rules for the care of relatives: one's own medicine is given to parents if they have none, even by seeking it on alms round; to brothers and sisters only their own belongings are used for medicine, or one's own lent for a time; medicine is not prepared for a sister's husband who is not a relative, but is given to the sister to give him; likewise for a brother's wife; and for their children, who are one's relatives, it may be done.

> kātabbam eva| atha tesaṃ bhesajjaṃ n'; atthi, attano  
santakaṃ dātabbaṃ| asati bhikkhācariyāya pariyesitvā  
pi dātabbam eva| bhātubhāginīnaṃ pana tesaṃ santakam  
eva yojetvā dātabbaṃ| sace n'; atthi, attano santakaṃ tāva-  
kālikaṃ datvā,1 pacchā labhantena gaṇhitabbaṃ, alabhantena  
na codetabbā| aññātakassa bhaginiyā sāmikassa2 bhesajjaṃ  
neva kātuṃ na dātuṃ vaṭṭati: tuyhaṃ sāmikassa dehī ti  
vatvā pana bhāginiyā dātabbaṃ| bhātujāyāya pi es'; eva  
nayo| tesaṃ pana puttā3 imassa ñātakā yevā ti tesaṃ  
kātuṃ vaṭṭatī ti4|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: ethics_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p95 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p95-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

(viii) Illness means any disease: it torments and so is an impediment, and should be cut by treating it. If it does not subside after several days' treatment, one should reproach oneself ("I am not your slave or your hireling; by feeding you I have suffered in the beginningless round") and do the ascetic's work.

> (viii|) ābādho ti yo koci rogo, so bādhayamāno5 paḷibodho  
hoti, tasmā bhesajjakaraṇena upacchinditabbo| sace pana  
katipāhaṃ bhesajjaṃ karontassa pi na vūpasammati: nāhaṃ  
tuyhaṃ dāso, na bhaṭako, taṃ yeva hi6 posento anamatagge  
saṃsāravaṭṭe dukkhaṃ patto7 ti attabhāvaṃ garahitvā  
samaṇadhammo kātabbo ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

### 3.p95 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p95-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

(ix) Books (gantha) means keeping up the study of the scriptures. It is an impediment only for one constantly busy with reciting and the like, not for another. Stories are told; in the first, the Majjhima-reciter Revata asks Revata of Malaya for a meditation subject and is asked how well he knows the scriptures; he says the middle collection is fluent; the elder answers that the middle collection is hard to keep up, since when one recites the first fifty the middle fifty comes, and then the last.

> (ix|) gantho ti pariyatti-pariharaṇaṃ, taṃ sajjhāyādīhi  
niccavyāvaṭass'; eva8 paḷibodho hoti, na itarassa| tatr'  
imāni vatthūni:- majjhimabhāṇaka-revatthero9 kira mala-  
yavāsi-revattherassa10 santikaṃ gantvā kammaṭṭhānaṃ  
yāci| thero: kīdiso 'si, āvuso, pariyattiyan ti pucchi|  
majjhimo me, bhante, paguṇo ti| āvuso, majjhimo  
nām'; eso dupparihāro, mūlapaṇṇāsaṃ11 sajjhāyantassa  
majjhimapaṇṇāsako12 āgacchati

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, narrative_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p96 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p96"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The first story ends: he took the subject, did not recite for nineteen years, and in the twentieth year reached arahatship; when monks came to learn from him he said he had not looked at the scriptures for twenty years, yet he knew them so well that from beginning to end he had no doubt about a single syllable. Two others are told: Nāga of Kāraḷiya-giri gave up the scriptures for eighteen years and then, teaching the Dhātukathā, found no point that came out of order; and the Tipiṭaka Cūḷābhaya, at the Mahāvihāra, without having taken up the commentary, had a golden drum beaten to teach the three Piṭakas, and was told by the Community to state whom he had learned from; his preceptor tested him and showed he did not have the teachers' tradition from the teachers' mouth.

> anolokentassa, api ca kho kataparicayo aham ettha| ārabha-  
thā ti vatvā ādito paṭṭhāya yāva pariyosānā ekavyañjane pi  
'ssa kankhā nāhosi|  
     kāraḷiyagirivāsi-1nāgatthero pi aṭṭhārasa vassāni pari-  
yattiṃ chaḍḍetvā, bhikkhūnaṃ dhātukathaṃ uddisi| te-  
saṃ gāmavāsikattherehi saddhiṃ saṃsandentānaṃ ekapañho  
pi uppaṭipātiyā āgato nāhosi|  
     mahāvihāre pi tipiṭaka-cūḷābhayatthero nāma aṭṭhaka-  
thaṃ anuggahetvā va: pañcanikāyamaṇḍale tīṇi piṭakāni  
parivattessāmī ti suvaṇṇabheriṃ pahārāpesi| bhikkhusan-  
gho: katamācariyānaṃ uggaho? attano ācariyuggahaṃ  
yeva vadatu| itarathā vattuṃ na demā ti āha| upajjhāyo  
pi naṃ attano upaṭṭhānaṃ āgataṃ pucchi: tvaṃ āvuso  
bheriṃ pahārāpesī ti? āma, bhante| kiṅkāraṇā ti? pari-  
yattiṃ, bhante, parivattessāmī ti| āvuso abhaya, ācariyā  
idaṃ padaṃ kathaṃ vadantī ti? evaṃ vadanti, bhante ti|  
thero: hun ti paṭibāhi| puna so aññena aññena pariyā-  
yena: evaṃ vadanti, bhante ti tikkhattuṃ āha| thero  
sabbaṃ: hun ti paṭibāhitvā: āvuso, tayā paṭhamaṃ kathito  
yeva2 ācariyamaggo, ācariyamukhato pāna anuggahitattā

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, narrative_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p97 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p97"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The Cūḷābhaya story ends. Sent by his preceptor to the elder Mahā-Dhammarakkhita at Tulādhārapabbata, Abhaya recited the scriptures to him by night and the elder expounded them by day. When the exposition was finished, the elder sat on a mat before Abhaya and asked him for a meditation subject; Abhaya, then a stream-enterer, protested that he had heard it all from the elder himself; the elder answered: "the path of one who has gone there is a different thing." Abhaya gave him the subject and left; later, while expounding the Dhamma at the Lohapāsāda, he heard that the elder had attained final nibbāna, and said that the arahat path was fitting for his teacher, an upright thoroughbred who had sat on a mat before his own Dhamma-pupil to ask for a subject. For such as these, study is not an impediment.

> anupubbena desanaṃ kathayitvā1 niṭṭhapetvā abhayatthe-  
rassa santike taṭṭikāya nisīditvā: āvuso, mayhaṃ kammaṭṭhā-  
naṃ kathehī ti āha| bhante, kiṃ bhaṇatha? nanu mayā  
tumhākam eva santike sutaṃ? kim ahaṃ tumhehi aññā-  
taṃ kathessāmī ti| tato naṃ thero: añño esa, āvuso,  
gatakassa maggo2 nāmā ti āha| abhayatthero kira tadā  
sotāpanno hoti| ath'; assa so kammaṭṭhānaṃ datvā āgantvā,  
lohapāsāde dhammaṃ parivattento thero parinibbuto ti  
assosi| sutvā: āharath', āvuso, cīvaran ti cīvaraṃ pārupitvā:  
anucchaviko, āvuso, amhākaṃ ācariyassa arahattamaggo|  
ācariyo no, āvuso, uju ājānīyo|3 so attano dhammante-  
vāsikassa santike taṭṭikāya nisīditvā: mayhaṃ kammaṭṭhā-  
naṃ kathehī ti āha| anucchaviko, āvuso, therassa arahatta-  
maggo ti| evarūpānaṃ gantho paḷibodho na hotī ti4|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, narrative_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p97 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p97-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

(x) Supernormal power (iddhi) means the worldling's power. It is hard to keep up, like an infant lying on its back or young corn, and is broken by a little. It is an impediment to insight, not to concentration, since one obtains it having reached concentration. Therefore one who wants insight should cut the impediment of power; the remaining nine (avasesā) are for the other person (itarena).

> (x|) iddhī ti pothujjanikā iddhi| sā hi uttānaseyyaka-  
dārako viya, taruṇasassaṃ viya ca dupparihārā hoti, appa-  
mattaken'; eva bhijjati| sā pana vipassanāya paḷibodho  
hoti, na samādhissa; samādhiṃ patvā pattabbato; tasmā  
vipassanatthikena iddhipaḷibodho upacchinditabbo, itarena  
avasesā ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: intermediate · types: practice, powers-experiences_

obstacles: [The ten impediments (paḷibodha)](../obstacles/ten-palibodhas.md)

### 3.p97 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p97-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Turning to "having approached a good friend who gives a meditation subject": a meditation subject is of two kinds, the universal (sabbatthaka) and the special (pārihāriya). The universal subject is loving-kindness toward the monks and so on, and mindfulness of death, and, some say, the perception of foulness. The meditation-subject practiser first delimits the community of monks within the boundary and develops loving-kindness toward them ("may they be happy, free from hate"), then the deities of the boundary, then the influential people of the alms-village, then the people there, then all beings.

> kammaṭṭhānadāyakaṃ kalyāṇamittaṃ upasankamitvā ti  
ettha pana duvidhaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ:- sabbatthaka-kam-  
maṭṭhānaṃ, pārihāriya-kammaṭṭhānañ ca| tattha sabbat-  
thaka-kammaṭṭhānaṃ nāma bhikkhusanghādisu mettā mara-  
ṇasati5 ca, asubhasaññā ti pi eke| kammaṭṭhānikena hi  
bhikkhunā paṭhamaṃ tāva paricchinditvā sīmaṭṭhakabhik-  
khusanghe: sukhitā hontu6 avyāpajjā ti mettā bhāvetabbā,  
tato sīmaṭṭhakadevatāsu, tato gocaragāmamhi issarajane,7  
tato tattha manusse upādāya sabbasattesu| so hi bhikkhu-  
sanghe mettāya sahavāsīnaṃ muducittataṃ8 janeti| ath'  
assa te sukhasaṃvāsā honti| sīmaṭṭhakadevatāsu mettāya  
mudukatacittāhi devatāhi dhammikāya rakkhāya susaṃvihi-

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md), [Good friendship (kalyāṇamittatā), the teacher who gives the subject](../practices/kalyanamittata.md)

### 3.p98 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p98"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Reasons the universal subject is called universal. Loving-kindness toward the monks makes co-residents gentle-minded; toward the deities of the boundary it brings their righteous protection; toward the influential people of the village it brings well-guarded requisites; toward the people it brings freedom from contempt; toward all beings it lets one go about unobstructed anywhere. Mindfulness of death, thinking "I must die", makes one give up wrong seeking and grow in urgency and unsluggish living. For one whose mind is used to the perception of foulness even divine objects do not overpower the mind through greed. Being so helpful and to be desired everywhere, they are the "universal subject" and the ground of the yoga-work.

> tārakkho hoti|1 gocaragāmamhi issarajane mettāya mudu-  
katasantānehi issarehi dhammikāya rakkhāya surakkhita-  
parikkhāro hoti| tattha manussesu mettāya pasāditacittehi  
tehi aparibhūto hutvā vicarati| sabbasattesu mettāya  
sabbattha appaṭihatacāro hoti| maraṇasatiyā pana avassaṃ:  
mayā maritabban ti cintento anesanaṃ pahāya uparūpari  
vaḍḍhamānasaṃvego anolīnavuttiko2 hoti| asubhasaññā-  
paricitacittassa pan'; assa dibbāni pi ārammaṇāni lobhavasena  
cittaṃ na pariyādiyanti| evaṃ bahūpakārattā sabbattha  
atthayitabbaṃ icchitabban ti ca adhippetassa yogānuyoga-  
kammassa ṭhānañ cā ti sabbatthaka-kammaṭṭhānan ti vuccati|

### 3.p98 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p98-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The special subject (pārihāriya): among the forty, that which suits a person's temperament (carita) is to be kept up constantly and is the base for each higher stage of development, so it is called the special subject. The one who gives either kind is the giver of the subject, the "good friend" (kalyāṇamitta), described in a verse: dear, respected, esteemed (bhāvanīya), a speaker, patient with speech, one who makes profound talk, and one who does not urge to what is unfit; possessed of such qualities, wishing only good and standing for growth.

> cattāḷīsāya pana kammaṭṭhānesu yaṃ yassa caritānuku-  
laṃ,3 taṃ tassa niccaṃ pariharitabbattā, uparimassa upari-  
massa ca bhāvanākammassa padaṭṭhānattā, pārihāriya-  
kammaṭṭhānan ti vuccati| iti imaṃ duvidham pi kammaṭ-  
ṭhānaṃ yo deti, ayaṃ kammaṭṭhānadāyako nāma, taṃ  
kammaṭṭhānadāyakam kalyāṇamittan ti:-  
          piyo garu bhāvanīyo vattā ca vacanakkhamo,  
          gambhīrañ ca kathaṅkattā, no ca ṭhāne niyojaye ti|  
     evam ādiguṇasamannāgataṃ ekanta4-hitesiṃ vuḍḍhipak-

### 3.p98 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p98-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Quoting the Buddha ("by coming to me as a good friend beings subject to birth are freed from birth"), the text holds that the fully awakened Buddha alone is the good friend complete in every way; while he lived the subject was to be received from him. After his parinibbāna, from whichever of the eighty great disciples is living; failing that, from one who has attained the taints' ending (khīṇāsava) by developing jhānas of the fourfold or fivefold kind for the sake of the subject he wants to take and then insight founded on them.

> mamaṃ hi, ānanda, kalyāṇa-  
mittaṃ āgamma jātidhammā sattā jātiyā parimuccantī ti5  
ādivacanato pana sammāsambuddho yeva sabbākārasampan-  
no kalyāṇamitto, tasmā tasmiṃ sati tass'; eva bhagavato  
santike gahitakammaṭṭhānaṃ6 sugahitaṃ hoti| parinibbute  
pana tasmiṃ, asītiyā mahāsāvakesu yo dharati, tassa santike  
gahetuṃ vaṭṭati| tasmim pi asati, yaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ  
gahetukāmo hoti, tass'; eva vasena catukkapañcakajjhānāni  
nibbattetvā jhānapadaṭṭhānaṃ vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā āsa-  
vakkhayaṃ pattassa khīṇāsavassa santike gahetabbaṃ|  
     kiṃ pana khīṇāsavo:

### 3.p99 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p99"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

How to choose the giver of the subject. Even a taint-destroyer (khīṇāsava) is told to declare himself only when he knows the pupil is a doer of the subject (the story of Assaguttatthera, who sat cross-legged on a piece of hide in the air to teach). If no taint-destroyer is available, the subject is taken from a non-returner, once-returner, stream-enterer, one with jhāna (a worldling), or a holder of three, two or one Piṭaka, taking the earlier in that order; if there is not even a one-Piṭaka holder, from one who has one recitation with its commentary well in hand and is himself conscientious (lajjī), who is a keeper of the tradition and holds the teachers' view, not his own. The taint-destroyers and the like teach only the way they themselves reached; the learned, having approached different teachers, will teach the subject showing the great road as a great elephant does. So one approaches such a person and performs the duties toward him before taking the subject.

> kammaṭṭhānakārako1 ayan ti jānitvā ākāse cammakhaṇḍaṃ  
paññapetvā tattha pallankena nisinno kammaṭṭhānaṃ ka-  
thesī ti? tasmā sace khīṇāsavaṃ labhati, icc'; etaṃ kusalaṃ|  
no ce labhati, anāgāmi-sakadāgāmi-sotāpanna-jhānalābhī-  
puthujjanatipiṭakadhara - dvipiṭakadhara - ekapiṭakadharesu  
purimassa purimassa santike| ekapiṭakadhare pi asati, yassa  
ekasangīti pi aṭṭhakathāya saddhiṃ paguṇā, sayañ2 ca lajjī  
hoti, tassa santike gahetabbaṃ| evarūpo hi tantidharo  
vaṃsānurakkhako paveṇipālako ācariyo ācariyamatiko va  
hoti, na attano matiko hoti,3 ten'; eva porāṇakattherā: lajjī  
rakkhissati, lajjī rakkhissatī ti tikkhattuṃ āhaṃsu| pubbe  
vuttakhīṇāsavādayo c'; ettha attanā adhigatamaggam eva  
ācikkhanti| bahussuto pana taṃ taṃ ācariyaṃ upasankam-  
itvā uggahaparipucchānaṃ visodhitattā ito c'; ito ca suttañ  
ca kāraṇañ ca sallakkhetvā sappāyāsappāyaṃ yojetvā, gaha-  
naṭṭhāne gacchanto, mahāhatthī viya mahāmaggaṃ dassento,  
kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathessati|4 tasmā evarūpaṃ kammaṭ-  
ṭhānadāyakaṃ kalyāṇamittaṃ upasankamitvā tassa vatta-  
paṭivattaṃ5 katvā kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetabbaṃ|

### 3.p99 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-3-p99-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

If the giver is in the same monastery, well; if not, one goes where he lives. One should not set out with feet washed and anointed, wearing sandals, carrying an umbrella, having pupils carry oil-tubes, honey, molasses and the like, and surrounded by pupils; one goes having fulfilled the duties of a traveller, carrying one's own bowl and robe, performing the duties in each monastery entered on the way, with few belongings and the utmost frugality. Before entering that monastery one has a tooth-stick made allowable on the way and takes it in; one should not first go into another cell, thinking to rest a moment, wash and anoint the feet and then go to the teacher, because monks there unfriendly to the teacher might ask the reason for coming, speak against the teacher and say "you are lost if you go to him", causing regret so that one turns back.

> sace pan'; etaṃ ekavihāre yeva labhati, icc'; etaṃ kusalaṃ|  
no ce labhati, yattha so vasati, tattha gantabbaṃ| gac-

chattaṃ gahetvā, telanāḷi-madhuphāṇitādīni gāhāpetvā ante-  
vāsikaparivutena gantabbaṃ| gamikavattaṃ pana pūretvā  
attano pattacīvaram sayam eva gahetvā antarāmagge yaṃ  
yaṃ vihāraṃ pavisati, sabbattha vattapaṭivattaṃ kurumā-  
nena sallahukaparikkhārena paramasallekhavuttinā hutvā  
gantabbaṃ| taṃ vihāraṃ pavisantena antarāmagge yeva  
dantakaṭṭhaṃ kappiyaṃ kārāpetvā gahetvā pavisitabbaṃ,  
na ca muhuttaṃ vissametvā pādadhovanamakkhanādīni kat-  
vā: ācariyassa santikaṃ gamissāmī ti aññaṃ pariveṇaṃ pavi-  
sitabbaṃ| kasmā? sace hi 'ssa tatra ācariyassa visabhāgā  
bhikkhū bhaveyyuṃ, te āgamanakāraṇaṃ pucchitvā ācari-  
yassa avaṇṇaṃ pakāsetvā naṭṭho 'si| sace tassa santikaṃ

### 4 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Developing the earth kasiṇa: when the disk, adverted to with eyes shut, comes into focus as when the eyes were open, the learning sign has arisen; as he keeps striking at it with thought, the hindrances are suppressed, the defilements subside, the mind is concentrated with access concentration and the counterpart sign arises — unlike the learning sign it shows no kasiṇa fault, like a mirror disk drawn from its case.

> tass' evaṃ karontassa anukkamena nīvaraṇāni vikkhambhanti, kilesā sannisīdanti, upacārasamādhinā cittaṃ samādhiyati, paṭibhāganimittaṃ uppajjati.

To guard the counterpart sign one should avoid seven unsuitable things and resort to the seven suitable: dwelling, resort (alms village), speech, person, food, climate and posture.

> āvāso, gocaro, bhassaṃ, puggalo, bhojanaṃ, utu, iriyāpatho ti satt' ete asappāye vivajjaye. sappāye satta sevetha

### 4 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-3"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

For absorption, ten kinds of skill are to be applied (making the basis clean, balancing the faculties, skill in the sign, exerting, restraining and gladdening the mind when needed, looking on with equanimity, avoiding the unconcentrated, cultivating the concentrated, resolution upon concentration), with evenness of energy.

> dasavidhaṃ appanākosallaṃ, viriyasamatā, appanāvidhānan ti ime nava ākārā kathetabbā.

### 4 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-4"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Rapture is fivefold: minor rapture only raises the hairs; momentary rapture is like flashes of lightning; showering rapture breaks over the body again and again like waves on the shore; uplifting rapture is strong enough to lift the body into the air; pervading rapture suffuses the whole body.

> sā pan' esā khuddikā pīti, khaṇikā pīti, okkantikā pīti, ubbegā pīti, pharaṇā pītī ti pañcavidhā hoti. tattha khuddikā pīti sarīre lomahaṃsamattam eva kātuṃ sakkoti. khaṇikā pīti khaṇe khaṇe vijjuppātasadisā hoti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: powers-experiences, practice_

### 4 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-5"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Rapture, maturing, perfects bodily and mental bliss; bliss, maturing, perfects the threefold concentration — momentary, access and absorption.

> sukhaṃ gabbhaṃ gaṇhantaṃ paripākaṃ gacchantaṃ tividhaṃ samādhiṃ paripūreti, khaṇikasamādhiṃ, upacārasamādhiṃ, appanāsamādhin ti.

### 4 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-6"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Before going on, the meditator should master the first jhāna in five ways: mastery in adverting, in attaining, in resolving (its duration), in emerging and in reviewing.

> tatr' imā pañcavasiyo: -āvajjanavasī, samāpajjanavasī, adhiṭṭhānavasī, vuṭṭhānavasī, paccavekkhaṇavasī ti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: practice_

### 4.p125 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p125"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Earth-kasiṇa: taking the learning sign. The eyes are to be opened neither too wide (the eye tires, the disk becomes too obvious and the sign does not arise) nor too little (the disk is unclear and the mind becomes sluggish, and again the sign does not arise); one opens them evenly, like one looking at the reflection of one's face in a mirror, and takes the sign. The colour is not to be reviewed nor the characteristic attended to; but without letting go of the colour, treating it as belonging to its support, one sets the mind on the concept (paññatti) as the prominent thing and attends to that. Of the names for earth (pathavī, mahī, medinī, bhūmi, vasudhā, vasundharā) one uses whichever suits one's perception, though "earth" (pathavī) is the most evident, so one develops it as "earth, earth". One adverts to it, opening the eyes at times and closing them at times, a hundred or a thousand times or more, until the learning sign (uggahanimitta) arises: it has arisen when, adverting with the eyes closed, it comes into focus just as with them open. From then on one does not sit on that spot but goes to one's own dwelling and develops it sitting there; to avoid the bother of washing the feet one should have a single-soled sandal and a staff. If the young concentration is lost through some unsuitable cause, one puts on the sandals, takes the staff, goes to the place, takes the sign, returns, sits comfortably and develops it, adverting to it again and again and striking at it with thought and applied thought. As one does so, gradually the hindrances (nīvaraṇa) are suppressed, the defilements subside, the mind is concentrated by access concentration, and the counterpart sign (paṭibhāganimitta) arises. (The text continues on p. 126 with how the two signs differ.)

> cakkhūni ummīletvā nimittaṃ gaṇhantena bhāvetabbaṃ|  
ati-ummīlayato hi cakkhu kilamati, maṇḍalañ ca ativi-  
bhūtaṃ hoti, ten'; assa nimittaṃ nuppajjati| atimandaṃ  
ummīlayato maṇḍalaṃ avibhūtaṃ hoti, cittañ ca līnaṃ  
hoti, evam pi nimittaṃ nuppajjati, tasmā, ādāsatale mu-  
khanimittadassinā viya, samena ākārena cakkhūni ummīletvā1

khitabbo, na lakkhaṇaṃ manasikātabbaṃ| api ca vaṇṇaṃ  
amuñcitvā nissaya savaṇṇaṃ katvā ussadavasena paṇṇatti-  
dhamme cittaṃ ṭhapetvā manasikātabbaṃ| pathavī, mahī,  
medinī,2 bhūmi, vasudhā, vasundharā ti ādisu pathavī-  
nāmesu yam icchati, yad-assa saññānukulaṃ hoti, taṃ vat-  
tabbaṃ| api ca pathavī ti etad eva nāmaṃ pākaṭaṃ,  
tasmā pākaṭavasen'; eva: pathavī, pathavī3 ti bhāvetabbaṃ|  
kālena ummīletvā,4 kālena nimmīletvā4 āvajjitabbaṃ|  
yāva uggahanimittaṃ nuppajjati, tāva kālasatam pi kāla-  
sahassam pi tato bhiyyo pi eten'; eva nayena bhāvetabbaṃ|  
tass'; evaṃ bhāvayato yadā nimmīletvā4 āvajjantassa um-  
mīlitakāle viya āpātham āgacchati, tadā uggahanimittaṃ  
jātaṃ nāma hoti|

attano vasanaṭṭhānaṃ pavisitvā tattha nisinnena bhāvetab-

punappunaṃ samannāharitabbaṃ, takkāhataṃ vitakkā-

### 4.p139 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p139"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Absorption lasts a single moment of consciousness; then comes the life-continuum (bhavanga); then, after cutting off the life-continuum, adverting for the sake of reviewing the jhāna, then the reviewing. At this point, with the formula "quite secluded from sensual desires, secluded from unwholesome states, he enters and abides in the first jhāna, with applied and sustained thought, with rapture and happiness born of seclusion", the meditator has attained the first jhāna on the earth kasiṇa, abandoning five factors, possessed of five factors, good in three ways and endowed with ten marks.

> iti ekacittakkhaṇikā yeva  
appanā, tato bhavangapāto| atha bhavangaṃ vocchinditvā2  
jhānapaccavekkhaṇatthāya āvajjanaṃ, tato jhānapaccavek-  
khaṇan ti3|  
     ettāvatā ca pan'; esa vivicc'; eva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi  
dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭha-  
maṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati4 evam anena pañcan-

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, consciousness-mind_

concepts: [The first jhāna](../concepts/first-jhana.md), [The five hindrances (pañca nīvaraṇāni)](../concepts/five-hindrances.md) · practices: [The earth kasiṇa (pathavī-kasiṇa), taking the learning sign (Vism IV p. 125)](../practices/kasina-earth.md)

### 4.p139 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p139-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Commentary on "quite secluded from sensual desires" (vivicc' eva kāmehi): "having separated from sensual desires", having become without them, having withdrawn. The word "eva" is restrictive. Because it is restrictive, even though sensual desires are absent when one abides in that first jhāna, it shows that they are the opposite of the jhāna and that the jhāna is attained by giving up sensual desires alone: just as a lamp's light shows that darkness is the opposite and is present when light is absent, and reaching the far bank comes by leaving the near bank. So the "eva" makes the restriction.

> tattha vivicc'; eva kāmehī ti kāmehi viviccitvā, vinā hutvā,  
apakkamitvā| yo panāyam ettha eva kāro, so niyamat-  
tho ti veditabbo| yasmā ca niyamattho, tasmā, tasmiṃ  
paṭhamajjhānaṃ upasampajja viharaṇa-samaye, avijjamānā-  
nam pi kāmānaṃ, tassa paṭhamassa jhānassa6 paṭipakkhabhā-  
vaṃ kāmapariccāgen'; eva c'; assa adhigamaṃ dīpeti| ka-  
thaṃ? vivicc'; eva kāmehī ti evaṃ hi niyame kariyamāne  
idaṃ paññāyati, nūn'; imassa7 jhānassa kāmāpaṭipakkha-  
bhūtā, yesu sati idaṃ nappavattati, andhakāre sati dīpo-  
bhāso8 viya, tesaṃ pariccāgen'; eva c'; assa adhigamo hoti,  
orimatīrapariccāgena pārimatīrass'; eva, tasmā niyamaṃ  
karotī ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, consciousness-mind_

obstacles: [Sensual desire (kāmacchanda)](../obstacles/kamacchanda.md)

### 4.p139 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p139-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Why "eva" is said only in the first phrase. The commentator answers that it is said there because of the escape (nissaraṇa): this jhāna is the way out of the sense sphere and the opposite of sensual lust, and so it is the very escape from sensual desires; he quotes "this is the escape from sensual desires, namely renunciation (nekkhamma)". The question whether one could enter the jhāna without being separated from unwholesome states is rejected: it is not so to be seen. The explanation of the second phrase begins.

> tattha siyā, kasmā pan'; esa pubbapade yeva vutto, na  
uttarapade? kiṃ akusalehi dhammehi aviviccā pi jhānaṃ  
upasampajja vihareyyā ti? na kho pan'; etaṃ evaṃ daṭ-  
ṭhabbaṃ| taṃ nissaraṇato hi pubbapade esa vutto, kāma-  
dhātu-samatikkamanato hi kāmarāgapaṭipakkhato ca idaṃ  
jhānaṃ kāmānam eva nissaraṇaṃ| yath'; āha:- kāmānam  
etaṃ nissaraṇaṃ yadidaṃ nekkhamman ti|9 uttarapade pi

obstacles: [Sensual desire (kāmacchanda)](../obstacles/kamacchanda.md)

### 4.p140 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p140"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The "eva" should be understood as applying also to the second phrase ("quite secluded from unwholesome states"), as in "here alone, monks, is the first ascetic, here the second". One cannot enter the jhāna without being secluded also from the other unwholesome states called the hindrances (nīvaraṇa); so both phrases have the restriction. Although the general word "secluded" covers all seclusions (by substitution of opposite, of mind, and so on), here only three are to be understood: bodily seclusion (kāyaviveka), mental seclusion (cittaviveka) and seclusion by suppression (vikkhambhanaviveka).

> pana yathāḥ: idh'; eva bhikkhave paṭhamo samaṇo, idha dutiyo  
samaṇo ti1 ettha eva kāro ānetvā vuccati, evaṃ vattabbo|  
na hi sakkā ito aññehi pi nīvaraṇasankhātehi akusalehi dham-  
mehi aviviccajhānam upasampajja viharituṃ, tasmā vivicc'  
eva kāmehi vivicc'; eva akusalehi dhammehī ti evaṃ padadvaye  
pi esa daṭṭhabbo| padadvaye pi ca kiñcāpi viviccā ti iminā  
sādhāraṇavacanena tad-angavivekādayo cittavivekādayo2 ca  
sabbe pi vivekā sangahaṃ gacchanti, tathā pi kāyaviveko  
cittaviveko vikkhambhanaviveko ti tayo eva idha daṭṭhabbā|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, consciousness-mind_

terms: [nīvaraṇa](../terms/nivarana.md) · obstacles: [The five hindrances (nīvaraṇa)](../obstacles/five-hindrances.md)

### 4.p140 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p140-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On "sensual desires" (kāmehi): the word takes in both objective sensual desires (vatthukāma: agreeable, dear sights and the rest as the Niddesa gives them) and sensual desires as defilements (kilesakāma: zeal, lust, zealous lust, intention, lust for sense objects and so on, as the Vibhaṅga gives them). So "secluded from sensual desires" is right in the sense of objective desires too, giving bodily seclusion; "secluded from unwholesome states" is right in the sense of defilement-desires or of all unwholesome states, giving mental seclusion. The first gives up the pleasure of sensuality; the second takes up the happiness of renunciation. The first also gives up the ground of defilement and the cause of fickleness and purifies the undertaking; the second gives up defilement and the cause of foolishness and nurtures the disposition. This is the method for objective sensual desires; on the side of defilement-desire, "sensual desire" (kāmacchanda) of many kinds, such as "zeal" and "lust", is meant by the single word.

> kāmehī ti iminā pana padena yena ca niddese: katame  
vatthukāmā? manāpā piyārupā ti3 ādinā nayena vatthu-  
kāmā vuttā, ye ca tatth'; eva vibhange ca chando kāmo, rāgo  
kāmo, chandarāgo kāmo, sankappo kāmo, rāgo kāmo, sankap-  
parāgo kāmo: | | | ime vuccanti kāmā ti4 evaṃ kilesakāmā  
vuttā, te sabbe pi sangahītā icc'; eva daṭṭhabbā| evaṃ hi  
sati vivicc'; eva kāmehī ti vatthukāmehi pi vivicc'; evā ti attho  
yujjati| tena kāyaviveko vutto hoti| vivicca akusalehi  
dhammehī ti kilesakāmehi sabbākusalehi vā viviccā ti attho  
yujjati| tena cittaviveko vutto hoti| purimena c'; ettha  
vatthukāmehi vivekavacanato eva kāmasukhapariccāgo,  
dutiyena kilesakāmehi vivekavacanato nekkhammasukha-  
pariggaho vibhāvito hoti| evaṃ vatthukāma-kilesakāma-  
vivekavacanato yeva ca etesaṃ paṭhamena sankilesavatthup-  
pahānaṃ, dutiyena sankilesappahānaṃ, paṭhamena lola-  
bhāvassa hetupariccāgo, dutiyena bālabhāvassa, paṭhamena  
ca payogasuddhi, dutiyena āsayaposanaṃ vibhāvitaṃ hotī ti  
ñātabbaṃ5|  
     esa tāva nayo kāmehī ti ettha vuttakāmesu vatthukāma-  
pakkhe| kilesakāmapakkhe pana chando ti ca rāgo ti ca  
evam ādīhi anekabhedo kāmacchando yeva kāmo ti adhippeto|

terms: [nīvaraṇa](../terms/nivarana.md) · obstacles: [Sensual desire (kāmacchanda)](../obstacles/kamacchanda.md)

### 4.p141 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p141"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On the "unwholesome states" that the second phrase names: sensual desire, though included among the unwholesome, is stated separately in the Vibhaṅga as opposed to the jhāna (or because it is a defilement-desire in the first phrase and an unwholesome state in the second). Of the other unwholesome states, only the hindrances (nīvaraṇa) are named, since the Vibhaṅga, in its passage on the unwholesome states ("sensual desire" and so on), shows only the hindrances, because they are the adversaries and opposites of the jhāna factors. The hindrances are opponents of the jhāna factors: they are what strike at, harm and destroy them. So it is said in the Peṭaka: concentration is the opponent of sensual desire (kāmacchanda), rapture of ill will (vyāpāda), applied thought (vitakka) of sloth and torpor (thīnamiddha), happiness (sukha) of restlessness and remorse (uddhacca-kukkucca), and sustained thought (vicāra) of doubt (vicikicchā).

> so ca akusalapariyāpanno pi samāno: tattha katame kāmā?  
chando kāmo1 ti ādinā nayena vibhange jhānapaṭipakkhato  
visuṃ vutto| kilesakāmattā vā purimapade vutto, akusala-  
pariyāpannattā dutiyapade| anekabhedato c'; assa kāmato  
ti avatvā kāmehī ti vuttaṃ| aññesam pi ca dhammānaṃ  
akusalabhāve vijjamāne: tattha katame akusalā dhammā?  
kāmacchando ti2 ādinā nayena vibhange upari jhānangānaṃ  
paccanīkapaṭipakkhabhāvadassanato nīvaraṇān'; eva vut-  
tāni| nīvaraṇāni hi jhānangapaccanīkāni, tesaṃ jhānangān'  
eva paṭipakkhāni viddhaṃ sakāni vighātakānī ti vuttaṃ hoti|  
tathā hi samādhi kāmacchandassa paṭipakkho, pīti vyāpādas-  
sa, vitakko thīnamiddhassa, sukhaṃ uddhacca-kukkuccassa,  
vicāro vicikicchāyā ti peṭake3 vuttaṃ|

### 4.p141 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p141-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Reading the two phrases together. "Quite secluded from sensual desires" declares the seclusion of suppression (vikkhambhana) of sensual desire (kāmacchanda); "secluded from unwholesome states" refers to all five hindrances, with the first phrase (taking what was not yet taken) covering sensual desire and the second the remaining hindrances. Likewise the first covers greed (lobha, whose object is the five cords of sensual pleasure) among the three unwholesome roots, the second hate and delusion, whose objects are the grounds of resentment and the like. Among floods, yokes, taints, clingings, bodily ties and fetters, the first covers the sensual ones (flood of sensuality, yoke of sensuality, taint of sensuality, clinging to sensuality, the bodily tie of covetousness, the fetter of sensual lust), the second the remainder; the first covers craving and what is associated with it, the second ignorance and what is associated with it; and the first the eight thought-moments associated with greed, the second the other four unwholesome kinds.

> evam ettha vivicc'  
eva kāmehī ti iminā kāmacchandassa vikkhambhanaviveko  
vutto hoti| vivicca akusalehi dhammehī ti iminā pañcan-  
nam pi nīvaraṇānaṃ, agahitaggahaṇena4 pana paṭhamena  
kāmacchandassa, dutiyena sesanīvaraṇānaṃ, tathā paṭha-  
mena tīsu akusalamūlesu pañcakāmaguṇabhedavisayassa  
lobhassa, dutiyena āghātavatthubhedādivisayānaṃ dosamo-  
hānaṃ| oghādisu vā dhammesu paṭhamena kāmogha-kā-  
mayoga-kāmāsava-kāmupādāna-abhijjhākāyagantha-kāma  
rāgasaṃyojanānaṃ, dutiyena avasesa-oghayogāsava-upādā-  
naganthasaṃyojanānaṃ| paṭhamena ca taṇhāya taṃ-sam-  
payuttakānañ ca, dutiyena avijjāya taṃ-sampayuttakānañ ca|  
api ca paṭhamena lobhasampayuttānaṃ aṭṭhannaṃ cittup-  
pādānaṃ, dutiyena sesānaṃ catunnaṃ akusalacittuppādā-  
naṃ vikkhambhanaviveko vutto hotī ti veditabbo| ayaṃ  
tāva vivicc'; eva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehī ti ettha  
atthappakāsanā|
