terms: [nīvaraṇa](../terms/nivarana.md) · obstacles: [The five hindrances (nīvaraṇa)](../obstacles/five-hindrances.md), [Sensual desire (kāmacchanda)](../obstacles/kamacchanda.md)

### 4.p142 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p142"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On "with applied thought and sustained thought" (savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ). Applied thought (vitakka) is thinking, i.e. directing (ūhana). Its mark is the directing of consciousness onto an object; its function is striking and threshing (the meditator makes the object struck and threshed by applied thought); its manifestation is leading consciousness onto the object. Sustained thought (vicāra) is roving, i.e. continued moving about. Its mark is continued pondering (anumajjana) of the object; its function is to keep the conascent states on it; its manifestation is consciousness's continued binding to it. Though the two are not always separate, applied thought is coarser and comes first, like the striking of a bell; sustained thought is subtler, like the ringing that follows. Applied thought is a stirring of consciousness at first arising, like a bird about to fly spreading its wings, or a bee heading for a lotus; sustained thought is the calm, unagitated occurring, like the bird gliding on outspread wings or the bee hovering above the lotus.

> tattha vitakkanaṃ vitakko,1 ūhanan ti vuttaṃ hoti|  
svāyaṃ ārammaṇe cittassa abhiniropanalakkhaṇo; āhana-  
na-pariyāhananaraso| tathā hi tena yogāvacaro āramma-  
ṇaṃ vitakkāhataṃ vitakkapariyāhataṃ karotī ti vuccati|  
arammaṇe cittassa ānayanapaccupaṭṭhāno|  
     vicaraṇaṃ vicāro, anusañcaraṇan ti vuttaṃ hoti| svāyaṃ  
ārammaṇānumajjanalakkhaṇo; tattha sahajātānuyojanaraso;  
cittassa anuppabandhanapaccupaṭṭhāno|  
     sante pi ca nesaṃ katthaci avippayoge oḷārikaṭṭhena pub-  
bangamaṭṭhena ca ghaṇṭābhighāto viya cetaso paṭhamā-  
bhinipāto vitakko|2 sukhumaṭṭhena anumajjanasabhāvena  
ca ghaṇṭānuravo viya anuppabandho vicāro| vipphāravā  
c'; ettha vitakko paṭhamuppattikāle paripphandanabhūto  
cittassa, ākāse uppatitukāmassa pakkhino pakkhavikkhepo  
viya, padumābhimukhapāto viya ca gandhānubandhacetaso  
bhamarassa| santavutti vicāro nātiparipphandanabhāvo  
cittassa, ākāse uppatitassa pakkhino pakkhappasāraṇaṃ viya,  
paribbhamanaṃ viya ca padumābhimukhapatitassa bhama-  
rassa padumassa uparibhāge| dukanipātaṭṭhakathāyaṃ3  
pana ākāse gacchato mahāsakuṇassa ubhohi pakkhehi vātaṃ  
gahetvā4 pakkhe sannisīdāpetvā gamanaṃ viya ārammaṇe  
cetaso abhiniropanabhāvena pavatto vitakko; [so hi ekaggo  
hutvā appeti;]5 vātagahaṇatthaṃ6 pakkhe phandāpaya-  
mānassa gamanaṃ viya anumajjanasabhāvena pavatto  
vicāro ti vuttaṃ| taṃ anuppabandhena pavattiyaṃ yuj-  
jati| so pana nesaṃ viseso paṭhamadutiyajjhānesu pākaṭo  
hoti| api ca malaggahitaṃ kaṃsabhājanaṃ ekena hatthena  
daḷhaṃ gahetvā, itarena hatthena cuṇṇatelavālaṇḍupakena7  
parimajjantassa, daḷhagahaṇahattho viya vitakko, parimaj-  
janahattho viya vicāro| tathā kumbhakārassa daṇḍappa-  
hārena cakkaṃ bhamayitvā, bhājanaṃ karontassa uppīḷana-

terms: [vitakka](../terms/vitakka.md), [vicāra](../terms/vicara.md) · concepts: [The first jhāna](../concepts/first-jhana.md)

### 4.p143 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p143"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Further similes (the bronze-bowl simile ends p. 142: applied thought is like the hand that grips the dirty bowl firmly, sustained thought like the hand that rubs it). Applied thought is like the potter's hand that presses the clay on the spinning wheel, sustained thought like the hand that moves here and there; applied thought is like the compass-point fixed in the middle, sustained thought like the point that moves round outside. Since the jhāna occurs with this applied thought and this sustained thought, as a tree with flower and fruit, it is called "with applied thought and sustained thought".

> hattho viya vitakko, ito cito ca sañcaraṇahattho viya vicāro|  
tathā maṇḍalaṃ karontassa majjhe sannirujjhitvā ṭhitakaṇ-  
ṭako viya abhiniropano vitakko, bahi paribbhamanakaṇṭako  
viya anumajjanto1 vicāro| iti iminā ca vitakkena iminā  
ca vicārena saha vattati, rukkho viya pupphena phalena cāti  
idaṃ jhānaṃ savitakkaṃ savicāranti vuccati|

### 4.p143 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p143-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

"Born of seclusion" (vivekaja): seclusion (viveka) is the going away of the hindrances (nīvaraṇa-vigama); or seclusion is that which is secluded, the mass of states associated with jhāna that is secluded from the hindrances. So it is born of that seclusion or in that seclusion.

> vivekajan ti ettha vivitti viveko|3 nīvaraṇavigamo ti  
attho| vivitto ti vā viveko, nīvaraṇavivitto jhānasampayut-  
tadhammarāsī ti attho| tasmā vivekā, tasmiṃ vā viveke  
jātan ti vivekajaṃ|

terms: [nīvaraṇa](../terms/nivarana.md), [viveka](../terms/viveka.md) · obstacles: [The five hindrances (nīvaraṇa)](../obstacles/five-hindrances.md)

### 4.p143 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p143-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

"Rapture and happiness" (pītisukha). Rapture (pīti) is so called because it refreshes. Its mark is being endeared (sampiyāyana); its function is to refresh body and mind, or to pervade; its manifestation is elation. It is fivefold: minor rapture (khuddikā), momentary (khaṇikā), showering (okkantikā), uplifting (ubbegā) and pervading (pharaṇā).

> pītisukhan ti [ettha4] pīṇāyatī ti5 pīti| sā sampiyāyana-  
lakkhaṇā; kāyacittapīṇanarasā, pharaṇarasā vā; odagya-  
paccupaṭṭhānā| sā pan'; esā khuddikā6 pīti, khaṇikā pīti,  
okkantikā pīti, ubbegā pīti, pharaṇā pītī ti pañcavidhā hoti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: consciousness-mind, powers-experiences_

terms: [pīti](../terms/piti.md)

### 4.p143 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p143-4"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The five kinds described. Minor rapture can raise the hairs on the body only. Momentary rapture comes at moments like flashes of lightning. Showering rapture breaks over the body again and again like waves on a seashore. Uplifting rapture is strong, lifting the body up until it leaps into the air; the elder Mahā-Tissa of Puṇṇavallika, on a full-moon evening, seeing the moonlight and turning toward the Great Shrine, thought of the four assemblies paying homage there, produced uplifting rapture with the Buddha as object, and leapt like a ball into the air and landed in the shrine courtyard.

> tattha khuddikā pīti sarīre lomahaṃsamattam eva kātuṃ  
sakkoti| khaṇikā pīti khaṇe khaṇe vijjuppātasadisā7 hoti|  
okkantikā pīti samuddatīraṃ vīci viya kāyaṃ okkamitvā  
okkamitvā bhijjati| ubbegā pīti balavatī hoti, kāyaṃ ud-  
dhaggaṃ katvā ākāse langhāpanappamāṇappattā| tathā hi  
puṇṇavallikavāsī mahā-tissatthero puṇṇamadivase sāyaṃ  
cetiyangaṇaṃ gantvā candālokaṃ disvā mahācetiyābhimukho  
hutvā: imāya vata velāya catasso parisā mahācetiy  
vandantī ti pakatiyā diṭṭhārammaṇavasena buddhāramma-  
ṇaṃ ubbegāpītiṃ uppādetvā sudhātale pahaṭacitrageṇḍuko8  
viya ākāse uppatitvā mahācetiyangaṇe yeva patiṭṭhāsi|

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: intermediate · types: powers-experiences, consciousness-mind_

terms: [pīti](../terms/piti.md)

### 4.p144 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p144"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence moderate

The girl of Vattakālaka village at Girikaṇḍaka (continued from p. 143): her parents, going to hear the Dhamma, told her, being "heavy-burdened" (garubhārā), that she could not go about at the wrong time; from the house terrace she saw the shrine courtyard by moonlight, the lamp-offering and the four assemblies with the monks reciting, and thought them fortunate; uplifting rapture arose in her, she leapt through the air, and arrived at the shrine before her parents. When asked how she had come, she said she had come through the air, not by the road; when told that only taint-destroyers travel through the air, she answered that strong rapture with the Buddha as object had arisen in her while she looked at the shrine by moonlight, and she knew neither standing nor sitting.

> thāya1 vihāraṃ gacchantā: amma! tvaṃ garubhārā akāle  
carituṃ2 na sakkosi; mayaṃ tumhaṃ pattiṃ katvā dhammaṃ  
sossāmā ti agamaṃsu| sā gantukāmā pi tesaṃ vacanaṃ  
paṭibāhituṃ asakkontī ghare ohīyitvā gharājire ṭhatvā  
candālokena girikaṇḍake3 ākāse cetiyangaṇaṃ olokentī4  
cetiyassa dīpapūjaṃ addasa, catasso ca parisā mālāgandhā-  
dīhi cetiyapūjaṃ katvā padakkhiṇaṃ karontiyo bhikkhusan-  
ghassa ca gaṇasajjhāyasaddaṃ assosi| ath'; assā:- dhañ-  
ñā vat 'ime, ye vihāraṃ gantvā evarūpe cetiyangaṇe anusañ-  
carituṃ, evarūpañ ca madhuraṃ dhammakathaṃ sotuṃ  
labhantī ti muttārāsisadisaṃ cetiyaṃ passantiyā eva ubbe-  
gāpīti udapādi| sā ākāse langhitvā mātāpitunnaṃ5 purima-  
taraṃ yeva ākāsato cetiyangaṇe orūyha cetiyaṃ vanditvā  
dhammaṃ suṇamānā aṭṭhāsi| atha naṃ mātāpitaro āgant-  
vā: amma! tvaṃ katarena maggena āgatā'; sī ti pucchiṃsu|  
sā: ākāsena āgatā 'mhi, na maggenā ti vatvā: amma! ākāsena  
nāma khīṇāsavā sañcaranti; tvaṃ kathaṃ āgatā ti vuttā,  
āha: mayhaṃ candālokena

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: intermediate · types: powers-experiences, narrative_

### 4.p144 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p144-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Pervading rapture and the maturing of the factors. When pervading rapture arises the whole body is suffused like a full bladder or a mountain cave flooded by a great deluge. This fivefold rapture, as it grows and matures, fulfils twofold tranquillity (passaddhi), of body and of consciousness; tranquillity, growing and maturing, fulfils twofold happiness, bodily and mental; happiness, growing and maturing, fulfils threefold concentration: momentary, access and absorption. Of these, the pervading rapture that grows and becomes the root of absorption concentration and joins with concentration is the rapture meant in this sense.

> evaṃ ubbegāpīti ākāse langhāpanappamāṇā hoti|  
     pharaṇāpītiyā pana uppannāya sakalasarīraṃ dhamitvā  
pūritavatthi viya mahatā udakoghena pakkhandapabbata-  
kucchi7 viya ca anuparipphuṭaṃ hoti| sā pan'; esā pañ-  
cavidhā pīti gabbhaṃ gaṇhantī paripākaṃ gacchantī duvi-  
dhaṃ passaddhiṃ paripūreti kāyappassaddhiñ ca cittappas-  
saddhiñ ca| passaddhi gabbhaṃ gaṇhantī paripākaṃ gac-  
chantī duvidham pi sukhaṃ paripūreti, kāyikañ ca cetasikañ  
ca| sukhaṃ gabbhaṃ ganhantaṃ paripākaṃ gacchantaṃ  
tividhaṃ samādhiṃ paripūreti, khaṇikasamādhiṃ, upacāra-  
samādhiṃ, appanāsamādhin ti| tāsu yā appanā samā-  
dhissa mūlaṃ hutvā vaḍḍhamānā samādhisampayogaṃ gatā  
pharaṇāpīti| ayaṃ imasmiṃ atthe adhippetā pītī ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: intermediate · types: consciousness-mind, practice_

terms: [pīti](../terms/piti.md), [passaddhi](../terms/passaddhi.md), [samādhi](../terms/samadhi.md) · concepts: [The first jhāna](../concepts/first-jhana.md)

### 4.p145 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p145"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Happiness (sukha) is so called because it makes easy and gulps down (khādati) or digs out (khaṇati) bodily and mental affliction. Its mark is gratification (sāta); its function is to enhance the associated states; its manifestation is aiding. Though rapture and happiness are not always separate, rapture is the gladness at getting a desirable object, happiness the experiencing of what is got. Where there is rapture there is happiness; where there is happiness there is not necessarily rapture. Rapture belongs to the formations aggregate, happiness to the feeling aggregate. Rapture is like a traveller weary in the desert who hears or sees water in the woods; happiness is like his entering the shade and using the water. They are shown in different moments where each is prominent. So this jhāna is "with rapture and happiness" in that both belong to it, or is "rapture-and-happiness born of seclusion" as one compound, both born of seclusion.

ṇati1 ca kāyacittābādhan ti sukhaṃ| taṃ sātalakkhaṇaṃ,  
sampayuttānaṃ upabrūhaṇarasaṃ,2 anuggahapaccupaṭṭhā-

### 4.p145 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p145-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On "the first jhāna", which will be clarified later. "Enters" (upasampajja) means having reached, or having brought about. "Abides" (viharati) means that, possessed of the jhāna as described, he brings about the movement, the occurring, the maintenance, the going on of his person by a fitting posture (iriyāpatha) of abiding; the Vibhaṅga says he "moves, occurs, maintains, keeps going, behaves, abides".

upasampādayitvā vā, nipphādetvā ti vuttaṃ hoti| vibhange

lābho patti sampatti phussanā sacchikiriyā upasampadā ti  
vuttaṃ|9 tassā pi evam ev'; attho daṭṭhabbo|

pakārajhānasamangī hutvā attabhāvassa iriyaṃ, vuttiṃ,  
pālanaṃ, yapanaṃ, yāpanaṃ, cāraṃ, vihāraṃ abhinip-  
phādeti| vuttaṃ h'; etaṃ vibhange: viharatī ti iriyati10,

### 4.p146 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p146"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On "with five factors abandoned": the abandoned factors are the five hindrances (nīvaraṇa): sensual desire (kāmacchanda), ill will (vyāpāda), sloth and torpor (thīnamiddha), restlessness and remorse (uddhacca-kukkucca) and doubt (vicikicchā). The jhāna does not arise while these are unabandoned, so they are called the factors of abandoning. Though other unwholesome states are also abandoned at the moment of jhāna, these five in particular are the obstructions to jhāna. Consciousness enticed by objects of many kinds is not concentrated on a unified object; overcome by sensual desire it does not follow the way to abandon the sense sphere. Struck by ill will at the object it does not continue without a break. Overcome by sloth and torpor it becomes unwieldy. Overtaken by restlessness and remorse it is unquiet and wanders about. Harmed by doubt it does not climb the way that leads to attaining the jhāna.

> yaṃ pana vuttaṃ:- pañcangavippahīnaṃ, pañcanga-  
samannāgatan ti3 tattha kāmacchando, vyāpādo, thīnamid-  
dhaṃ, uddhaccakukkuccaṃ, vicikicchā ti imesaṃ pañcan-

tabbā| na hi etesu appahīnesu jhānam uppajjati, ten'; ass'  
etāni pahānangānī ti vuccanti| kiñcāpi hi jhānakkhaṇe  
aññe pi akusalā dhamma pahīyanti, tathā pi etān'; eva vise-  
sena jhānantarāyakarāni| kāmacchandena hi nānāvisayap-  
palobhitaṃ cittaṃ na ekattārammaṇe samādhiyati, kāmac-  
chandābhibhūtaṃ vā taṃ na kāmadhātuppahānāya paṭi-  
padaṃ paṭipajjati, vyāpādena c'; ārammaṇe paṭihaññamā-  
naṃ na nirantaraṃ pavattati| thīnamiddhābhibhūtaṃ  
akammaññaṃ hoti, uddhaccakukuccaparetaṃ avūpasantam  
eva hutvā paribbhamati| vicikicchāya upahataṃ jhānā-  
dhigamasādhikaṃ paṭipadaṃ nārohati, iti visesena jhānan-  
tarāyakarattā etān'; eva pahānangānī ti vuttāni4|

### 4.p146 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p146-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On "possessed of five factors": since applied thought directs consciousness onto the object, sustained thought binds it on, and for consciousness thus prepared to be undistracted, rapture (from success of the endeavour) refreshes and happiness enhances, then one-pointedness, aided by these, steadies the mind with its associated states evenly and rightly on a single object. So the five factors are applied thought (vitakka), sustained thought (vicāra), rapture (pīti), happiness (sukha) and one-pointedness of mind (cittekaggatā); when these five have arisen the jhāna has arisen. It is not that some other jhāna has these five: as a four-limbed army, a five-piece band and the eightfold path are named by their limbs only, so the jhāna is called "five-limbed" by the mere factors. (Continues on p. 147.)

ti imesaṃ pañcannaṃ uppattivasena pañcangasamannāga-

terms: [vitakka](../terms/vitakka.md), [vicāra](../terms/vicara.md), [pīti](../terms/piti.md), [sukha](../terms/sukha.md), [ekaggatā](../terms/ekaggata.md) · concepts: [The first jhāna](../concepts/first-jhana.md)

### 4.p147 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p147"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The five factors are present in access too, but there they are stronger than ordinary consciousness; here (in the first jhāna) they are stronger even than in access and reach the character of the fine-material sphere. Applied thought arises directing consciousness on the object in a very purified way; sustained thought pondering the object exceedingly; rapture and happiness pervading the whole body; as the discourse says, "there is nothing in his whole body that is not pervaded by the rapture and happiness born of seclusion." One-pointedness, though not named in the wording "with applied and sustained thought", is a factor, since the Vibhaṅga says "jhāna is applied thought, sustained thought, rapture, happiness, one-pointedness of mind": the intention of the Blessed One's summary is shown by his own analysis.

> vasen'; eva pañcangikan ti vā pañcangasamannāgatan ti vā  
vuccatī ti veditabbaṃ| etāni ca pañcangāni, kiñcāpi upa-  
cārakkhaṇe pi atthi, atha kho upacāre pakaticittato balava-  
tarāni| idha pana upacārato pi balavatarāni rūpāvacara-  
lakkhaṇappattāni| ettha hi vitakko suvisuddhena1 ākārena  
ārammaṇe cittaṃ abhiniropayamāno upajjati| vicāro ati-  
viya ārammaṇaṃ anumajjamāno, pītisukhaṃ sabbāvantam  
pi kāyaṃ pharamānaṃ| ten'; ev'; āha: nāssa kiñci sabbāvato  
kāyassa vivekajena pītisukhena apphuṭaṃ hotī ti|2

### 4.p147 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-4-p147-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On "good in three ways, endowed with ten marks": the jhāna is good in beginning, middle and end, and the ten marks are the marks of those three. The Pāḷi says: the purification of the way (paṭipadā-visuddhi) is the beginning of the first jhāna; the strengthening by onlooking equanimity (upekkhānubrūhaṇā) is the middle; gladdening (sampahaṃsanā) is the end. The beginning has three marks: the consciousness is purified of the obstruction to it; being purified, it enters on the middle sign of calm; having entered, it leaps forward there. So the first jhāna is good in the beginning and possessed of these three marks. (The middle and end are given on p. 148.)

### 5 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-5"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The remaining kasiṇas are developed like the earth kasiṇa, each on its own device (water in a vessel, a fire seen through a hole in a screen, wind moving leaves, coloured disks or flowers, light cast through a hole on a wall, space seen through a hole); they are the basis for the corresponding supernormal powers.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: all · types: practice_

### 6 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-6"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

The foulness subject is grasped in eleven ways: the learning sign arises through looking at the corpse with open eyes; applying the mind to it, the counterpart sign arises; there he reaches absorption, and standing in absorption and developing insight he realizes arahantship.

> ekādasavidhena pana nimittaggāhaṃ sampādento kammaṭṭhānaṃ upanibandhati: tassa hi cakkhūni ummīletvā olokanapaccayā uggahanimittaṃ uppajjati, tasmiṃ mānasaṃ cārentassa paṭibhāganimittaṃ uppajjati; tattha mānasaṃ cārento appanaṃ pāpuṇāti; appanāya ṭhatvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhento arahattaṃ sacchikaroti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: intermediate · types: practice, death-dying_

terms: [asubha](../terms/asubha.md) · concepts: [The three signs (nimitta)](../concepts/three-nimittas.md) · practices: [Contemplation of the bloated corpse (uddhumātaka)](../practices/asubha-bloated.md), [Contemplation of the skeleton corpse (aṭṭhika)](../practices/asubha-skeleton.md)

### 6 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-6-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The ten foulnesses are ten kinds of corpse — bloated, livid, festering, cut up, gnawed, scattered, hacked and scattered, bleeding, worm-infested and a skeleton — each counteracting a particular kind of lust; one should go to them with care (informing the senior monk, noting the route and surroundings), and a body of the opposite sex is not suitable for a beginner.

> uddhumātakaṃ, vinīlakaṃ, vipubbakaṃ, vicchiddakaṃ, vikkhāyitakaṃ, vikkhittakaṃ, hatavikkhittakaṃ, lohitakaṃ, puḷuvakaṃ, aṭṭhikan ti dasasu aviññāṇaka asubhesu

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: intermediate · types: practice, death-dying_

### 7 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-7"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

Recollection of the Buddha recollects his special qualities ('he is accomplished, fully enlightened, perfect in knowledge and conduct …'); because the qualities are many and profound it reaches only access; its benefits include reverence, fullness of faith, mindfulness, understanding and merit, fearlessness, and a body that becomes worthy of veneration like a shrine room.

_level: conventional · standpoint: devotional · path: devotion, meditation · stage: all · types: practice_

terms: [anussati](../terms/anussati.md) · practices: [Recollection of the Buddha](../practices/buddhanussati.md)

### 7 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-7-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The recollections of the Dhamma, the Saṅgha, virtue, generosity and deities recollect their special qualities; in the recollection of deities one recollects one's own faith, virtue, learning, generosity and understanding with the deities as witnesses. They suit the faithful and lead to access.

_level: conventional · standpoint: devotional · path: devotion, meditation · stage: all · types: practice_

practices: [Recollection of the Dhamma](../practices/dhammanussati.md), [Recollection of the Saṅgha](../practices/sanghanussati.md), [Recollection of one's virtue](../practices/silanussati.md), [Recollection of one's generosity](../practices/caganussati.md), [Recollection of the deities](../practices/devatanussati.md)

### 8 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Mindfulness of death: if merely attending 'death will take place' does not bring access, one recollects death in eight ways — as a murderer standing by, as the ruin of success, by comparison with the great, as the body shared with many (creatures), as the frailty of life, as signless (unpredictable), as the limitedness of the life-span, and as the shortness of the moment.

> vadhakapaccupaṭṭhānato, sampattivipattito, upasaṃharaṇato, kāyabahusādhāraṇato, āyudubbalato, animittato, addhānaparicchedato, khaṇaparittato ti

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: all · types: practice, death-dying_

terms: [maraṇassati](../terms/maranassati.md) · practices: [Mindfulness of death (maraṇassati)](../practices/maranassati.md)

### 8 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The bhikkhu devoted to mindfulness of death is diligent, disenchanted with all kinds of existence, free from hoarding, familiar with the perception of impermanence, and dies undeluded and without fear; if he does not attain the deathless here, he is bound for a happy destiny.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: all · types: death-dying, practice_

practices: [Mindfulness of death (maraṇassati)](../practices/maranassati.md)

### 8 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-3"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

Mindfulness occupied with the body contemplates the thirty-two parts (head hair, body hair, nails, teeth, skin …) — first by verbal recitation, then mental recitation, by colour, shape, direction, location and delimitation — as repulsive; it leads to the first jhāna and suits the greedy temperament.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: all · types: practice, body-layers_

terms: [kāyagatāsati](../terms/kayagatasati.md) · practices: [Mindfulness directed to the body (kāyagatāsati): summary only](../practices/kayagatasati.md)

### 8 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-4"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Mindfulness of breathing is attended to by stages: counting, connection (following), contact, fixing, observing, turning away, purification and looking back on these.

> tatrāyaṃ manasikāravidhi:- gaṇanā, anubandhanā, phusanā, ṭhapanā, sallakkhaṇā, vivaṭṭanā, pārisuddhi, tesañ ca paṭipassanā ti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: all · types: practice_

terms: [ānāpānassati](../terms/anapanasati.md) · practices: [Mindfulness of breathing (ānāpānassati)](../practices/anapanasati.md)

_Superseded by [8.p278/2](visuddhimagga.md#tea-visuddhimagga-8-p278-2)_

### 8 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-5"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

The counterpart sign of the breath does not appear alike to all: to some it appears like a star, a round gem or a pearl; to others as a rough cotton seed or a peg of heartwood; to others like a long braid string, a wreath of flowers or a puff of smoke; to others like a stretched cobweb, a film of cloud, a lotus, a chariot wheel, the moon's or the sun's disk — because it is produced by perception; some teachers said it appears like a tuft of cotton or a draught of air.

> idaṃ hi kassaci tārakarūpaṃ viya, maṇiguḷikā viya, muttāguḷikā viya ca, kassaci kharasamphassaṃ hutvā kappāsaṭṭhi viya, dārusārasūci viya ca, kassaci dīghapāmaṅgasuttaṃ viya, kusumadāmaṃ viya, dhūmasikhā viya ca, kassaci vitthataṃ makkaṭakasuttaṃ …

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: powers-experiences, practice_

terms: [paṭibhāga-nimitta](../terms/patibhaga-nimitta.md) · concepts: [The three signs (nimitta)](../concepts/three-nimittas.md) · practices: [Mindfulness of breathing (ānāpānassati)](../practices/anapanasati.md)

### 8 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-6"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Mindfulness of breathing, developed through its sixteen bases, can carry the meditator through insight and the four paths to arahantship, where he reaches the nineteen kinds of reviewing knowledge and becomes worthy of the highest offerings.

> yathākkamena cattāro ariyamagge pāpuṇitvā, arahattaphale patiṭṭhāya ekūnavīsati bhedassa paccavekkhaṇā-ñāṇassa pariyantaṃ patto, sadevakassa lokassa aggadakkhiṇeyyo hoti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: practice, karma-liberation_

concepts: [Reviewing knowledge (paccavekkhaṇa-ñāṇa)](../concepts/reviewing-knowledge.md) · practices: [Mindfulness of breathing (ānāpānassati)](../practices/anapanasati.md)

### 8 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-7"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

A further benefit of mindfulness of breathing: for one who has developed it, even the final in-breaths and out-breaths at death cease known, not unknown.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: death-dying, practice_

practices: [Mindfulness of breathing (ānāpānassati)](../practices/anapanasati.md)

### 8 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-8"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

The recollection of peace recollects the qualities of nibbāna as the fading away that is the disillusionment of vanity, the elimination of thirst, the abolition of reliance, the termination of the round, the destruction of craving, fading, cessation, nibbāna.

> virāgo ti na rāgābhāvamattam eva, atha kho yadidaṃ mada-nimmadano … nibbānan ti yo so madanimmadano ti ādīni nāmāni asaṅkhatadhammo labhati, so virāgo ti paccetabbo.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: all · types: ultimate, practice_

### 8.p266 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p266"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high · _restricted: summary only_

The close of the exposition on mindfulness occupied with the body. A bhikkhu devoted to it masters discontent and delight and fear and dread, endures cold and heat and the like, gains the four jhānas by the colours of the hair and the rest, and penetrates the six direct-knowledges; the verse says the wise should be diligent in this many-benefited mindfulness occupied with the body. This is the outline of the detailed account of it.

### 8.p266 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p266-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The section on mindfulness of breathing (ānāpānasati) opens with the Buddha's praise, quoted from the discourse: "this concentration of mindfulness of breathing, monks, when developed and made much of, is peaceful and sublime, unadulterated, a pleasant abiding, and makes evil unwholesome states that have arisen disappear and subside at once." He then asks how it is developed and made much of, and answers: a bhikkhu gone to the forest, to the root of a tree, or to an empty place sits down, folds his legs crosswise, sets his body erect and establishes mindfulness in front of him (the text continues on p. 267).

### 8.p267 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p267"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The formula of the sixteen bases (vatthu) is given. Mindful he breathes in, mindful he breathes out; breathing in long he knows "I breathe in long", breathing out long he knows "I breathe out long"; likewise short; he trains "I shall breathe in, breathe out, experiencing the whole body" and "calming the bodily formation"; and so on through rapture (pīti), happiness (sukha), the mental formation (cittasankhāra), calming the mental formation, experiencing the mind, gladdening the mind, concentrating the mind, liberating the mind, contemplating impermanence, dispassion, cessation and relinquishment; the text gives these as the sixteen-based subject of mindfulness of breathing.

### 8.p267 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p267-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The method of developing it is now reached; since it is best told following the commentary on the text, the commentary comes first. "How developed, monks, is the concentration of mindfulness of breathing?" — "how" is the wish to expand on the many ways of development; "developed" is produced or increased; "concentration of mindfulness of breathing" is concentration conjoined with the mindfulness that embraces the breath, or concentration in mindfulness of breathing; "made much of" is done again and again.

### 8.p268 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p268"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

"Peaceful and sublime": this concentration is peaceful (santo) and sublime (paṇīto) both by object and by factor, unlike the foulness subject which is peaceful and sublime only by penetration but is neither by object, since its object is gross and repulsive; it is never in any way unpeaceful or unsublime and gives no satiety. "Unadulterated and a pleasant abiding": it is unmixed, needing neither preparatory work nor access for its peacefulness, being peaceful and sublime by its own nature from the first attention; some say "unadulterated" is "not sprinkled, sweet by itself". It brings bodily and mental pleasure at each moment of attainment. "Arisen, arisen": those not yet suppressed. "Evil": inferior. "Unwholesome states": those born of unskilfulness. "At once": in a moment, it suppresses them; "subsides": calms them well, or, being of the penetrating sort, it in due course cuts them off with the growth of the noble path. The commentator gives the brief sense: by what means and in what manner, by what method is the concentration of mindfulness of breathing developed and made much of so that it is peaceful and sublime?

### 8.p268 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p268-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On "here, monks, a monk": "here" points to the dispensation in which the person who can bring about the concentration of mindfulness of breathing in all its ways relies, and excludes other dispensations, as in the saying "only here, monks, is the ascetic; others are empty of ascetics". "Gone to the forest ... or to an empty place": this shows the choice of a dwelling fit for developing this concentration. The mind of such a monk has long wandered among objects such as forms and does not wish to climb onto the object of mindfulness of breathing; like a cart yoked to a vicious ox it runs off the road.

### 8.p269 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p269"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The simile continued: as a cowherd wishing to tame a calf reared on the milk of a vicious cow takes it from the cow, plants a big post at one side and ties it to it, so that after struggling this way and that it cannot flee and settles down or lies down by the post, the monk wishing to tame his wrongly-disposed mind, long nourished on the taste of forms and other objects, takes it away from those objects and brings it into a forest, to a tree root or an empty place, and ties it with the rope of mindfulness to the post of the in- and out-breath. Then, unable to get its former objects and unable to cut the rope of mindfulness and flee, it settles down and lies down by that very object by way of access and absorption. The ancients said: as one ties the calf to the post to tame it, so should one tie one's own mind firmly to the object with mindfulness. In this way the dwelling is fit for development.

### 8.p269 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p269-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

A second reason for the forest: this subject of mindfulness of breathing, which stands at the head of the subjects, is the base for the distinctions attained by the fully awakened Buddhas, the paccekabuddhas and the Buddha's disciples and for their pleasant abiding here and now; it is not easy to develop without leaving the village's edge, which is crowded with the sounds of women, men, elephants, horses and the like, sound being a thorn to jhāna; in a forest with no village, it is easy for the meditator to take the subject, produce the four jhānas of breathing, make that the base, comprehend the formations and reach the highest fruit, arahatship. So, showing a dwelling fit for it, the Blessed One said "gone to the forest" and so on. The Blessed One is like an expert in sites (continued on p. 270).

### 8.p270 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p270"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The simile continued: as a site-expert examines the ground for a city and says "build the city here", and, when the city is safely finished, is honoured by the royal house, so the Buddha examines a fit dwelling for the meditator and says "practise the subject here"; when the meditator, practising, reaches arahatship, people honour the Buddha: "he is truly the Fully Awakened, the Blessed One". Also this monk is compared to a leopard: as a great leopard hides in thickets, woods or mountain thickets and seizes forest buffaloes, deer and pigs, so the monk who practises in the forest and elsewhere gradually seizes the paths of stream-entry, once-return, non-return and arahatship and the noble fruits. The ancients said: the leopard hides and seizes deer; so the Buddha's son, devoted, an insight-meditator, enters the forest and seizes the supreme fruit.

### 8.p270 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p270-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Showing the forest lodging as a ground fit for the speed of his exertion, the Blessed One said "gone to the forest". "Forest" is defined: outside the boundary stone, all this is forest; a forest lodging is one at least five hundred bow-lengths (from the village); so "gone to the forest" means gone to any forest of these stated marks where the happiness of seclusion is found. "Gone to the root of a tree" means gone near a tree. "Gone to an empty place" means gone to an empty, secluded spot; apart from forest and tree-root, one who has gone to any of the remaining seven kinds of lodging may be said to have "gone to an empty place".

### 8.p271 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p271"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Having pointed to a lodging that suits the three seasons and the temperament of the (bodily) elements, the Blessed One then said "sits down", indicating a posture that is peaceful and inclined neither to sluggishness nor to restlessness. To show that the sitting is firm, that the breathing goes easily, and how to fix the object, he said "folding his legs crosswise". "Cross-legged" (pallanka) is sitting with the thighs locked all round; "folding" is binding. "Having set the body erect" is holding the upper body straight, the eighteen vertebrae end to end; for one who sits so, skin, flesh and sinews do not bend, and so the feelings that would arise at each moment from their bending do not arise; the mind is well concentrated, the subject does not fall away and it grows and flourishes.

> evam assa ututtayānukulaṃ dhātucariyānukulañ ca ānā-  
pānasati-bhāvanānurūpaṃ senāsanaṃ upadisitvā alīnānud-

ti āha| ath'; assa nisajjāya daḷhabhāvaṃ assāsapassāsānaṃ  
pavattanasukhataṃ ārammaṇapariggahupāyañ ca dassento  
pallankaṃ ābhujitvā ti ādim āha|  
     tattha pallankan ti samantato ūrubaddhāsanaṃ|  
     ābhujitvā ti bandhitvā|

### 8.p271 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p271-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

"Establishing mindfulness in front": setting mindfulness toward the meditation subject; or, as the Paṭisambhidā has it, pari = embracing, mukha = outlet, sati = establishing, so mindfulness made to embrace and lead out. "He breathes in mindful, breathes out mindful": the monk, seated so and having established mindfulness, not letting go of it, breathes in and out with mindfulness alone, acting mindfully. The text cites the Paṭisambhidā's analysis of this: he is "one who acts mindfully" in thirty-two ways, since by way of the long in-breath, the long out-breath and so on through the sixteen steps, in-breath and out-breath, mindfulness is established in one who knows the concentrated, undistracted state of mind.

### 8.p272 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p272"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

"Long" and "short". The Vinaya commentary says assāsa is the wind going out and passāsa the wind coming in, while the discourse commentaries give the reverse order. For all beings born from the womb, when leaving the mother's womb, the inner wind first goes out and then the outer wind, carrying fine dust, goes in and, striking the palate, ceases. The long and short are known by duration (addhāna): as water or sand spread over a space is called long water or long sand, or short, so breaths in an elephant's or a snake's body fill the long extent of that body slowly and go out slowly and are called long; in a dog or hare they fill the short extent quickly and go out quickly and are called short. Among humans some breathe in and out long by time like elephants and snakes, some short like dogs and hares; the breaths that take a long time are long, those that take a little are short.

> tayanto| assāso ti bahi nikkhamanavāto| passāso ti anto  
pavisanavāto ti vinayaṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttaṃ suttan-  
taṭṭhakathāsu pana uppaṭipāṭiyā āgataṃ| tattha sabbe-  
sam pi gabbhaseyyakānaṃ mātukucchito nikkhamanakāle  
paṭhamaṃ abbhantaravāto bahi nikkhamati, pacchā bāhira-  
vāto sukhumarajaṃ gahetvā abbhantaraṃ pavisanto tāluṃ  
āhacca nibbāyati:- evaṃ tāva assāsapassāsā veditabbā|

### 8.p272 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p272-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

This monk, breathing in and out long, knows "I breathe in long, I breathe out long" in nine ways; for one who knows so, the development of the establishment of mindfulness by contemplating the body is accomplished in one way. The Paṭisambhidā is quoted: he breathes the long in-breath, long out-breath, long in- and out-breath as extent (addhāna); zeal (chanda) arises; through zeal he breathes still subtler long breaths; gladness (pāmojja) arises (the quotation continues on p. 273).

### 8.p273 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p273"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The Paṭisambhidā continued: through gladness he breathes still subtler long breaths; then the mind turns away from the long in- and out-breath and equanimity is established. By these nine ways the long in- and out-breaths are the body as the ground of mindfulness (upaṭṭhāna), mindfulness and the knowledge that contemplates; the body is the ground, not the mindfulness; the mindfulness is both the ground and mindfulness; by that mindfulness and that knowledge one contemplates that body: this is called developing the establishment of mindfulness by contemplating the body in the body.

assasati pi passasati pi, pāmojjavasena tato sukhumataraṃ

### 8.p273 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p273-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence moderate

The same holds for "short", with the difference that "short in-breath, short out-breath" is by measure of a little (ittara) instead of extent. So the monk knows long and short by extent and by measure, breathing in and out; a verse says: long and short is the in-breath and likewise the out-breath: four kinds occur at the tip of the nose of the monk.

### 8.p273 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p273-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

"Experiencing the whole body": making known and evident the beginning, middle and end of the whole "in-breath body" and "out-breath body" he trains "I shall breathe in" and "I shall breathe out", breathing in and out with a mind associated with knowledge. One monk finds only the beginning clear in the in-breath or out-breath body spread out in fine dust, not the middle and end, and can grasp only the beginning and is troubled at the middle and end; another finds only the middle clear, another only the end; one finds all clear and is not troubled anywhere (the passage continues on p. 274).

sikkhatī ti vuccati| ekassa hi bhikkhuno cuṇṇavicuṇṇa-  
visaṭe assāsakāye passāsakāye vā ādipākaṭo hoti, na majjha-  
pariyosānaṃ| so ādim eva pariggahetuṃ sakkoti, majjha-

pariyosānaṃ| ekassa pariyosānaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti, na adī-  
majjhaṃ; so pariyosānaṃ yeva pariggahetuṃ sakkoti,

### 8.p274 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p274"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The other monk finds all of it clear and can grasp all, and is troubled nowhere; the Blessed One, showing that the meditator should be such, says "experiencing the whole body". "He trains" means he strives so: the restraint in one who is so is the training in higher virtue, the concentration is the training in higher mind, the understanding the training in higher understanding; in that object, with that mindfulness, with that attention he trains, practises, develops and makes much of these three. In the earlier steps he only has to breathe in and out and nothing else to do, so the text is put in the present ("he knows 'I breathe in'"); from here on effort is needed in producing knowledge and the rest, so the text is put in the future ("I shall breathe in, experiencing the whole body").

bhūtassa saṃvaro: ayam ettha adhisīlasikkhā| yo tathā-

paññā, ayaṃ adhipaññāsikkhā ti imā tisso sikkhāyo tasmiṃ  
ārammaṇe, tāya satiyā, tena manasikārena-sikkhati, āse-

### 8.p274 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p274-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

"Calming the bodily formation": he trains breathing in and out while calming, stilling, stopping and appeasing the gross bodily formation (the in- and out-breath). Gross and subtle, and calming: before the meditator has grasped the object, body and mind are disturbed and gross; when body and mind are gross and unappeased the breaths are gross, grow stronger and the nose is not enough, so he stands breathing in and out through the mouth. When body and mind are grasped they become calm and stilled and the breaths become subtle, so that it becomes a matter of checking "are they there or not?", like the breaths of a man who has run or come down a mountain or set down a heavy load from his head.

> passambhayaṃ kāyasankhāraṃ assasissāmī ti |||pe|||  
passasissāmī ti sikkhatī ti2 oḷārikaṃ kāyasamkhāraṃ passam-  
bhento paṭippassambhento nirodhento vūpasamento3 assa-  
sissāmi passasissāmī ti sikkhati|  
     tatra evaṃ oḷārikasukhumatā ca passaddhi ca veditabbā|  
imassa hi bhikkhuno pubbe apariggahitakāle kāyo ca cittañ  
ca sadarathā honti oḷārikā| kāyacittānaṃ oḷārikatte avū-  
pasante assāsapassāsā pi oḷārikā honti, balavatarā hutvā  
pavattanti, nāsikā nappahoti, mukhena assasanto pi pas-  
sasanto pi tiṭṭhati| yadā pan'; assa kāyo pi cittam pi parig-  
gahitā honti, tadā te santā honti vūpasantā| tesu vūpa-  
santesu assāsapassāsā sukhumā hutvā pavattanti: atthi nu  
kho natthī ti vicetabbatākārappattā honti| seyyathā pi puri-  
sassa dhāvitvā pabbatā vā orohitvā mahābhāraṃ vā sīsato

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, body-layers_

practices: [Mindfulness of breathing (ānāpānassati)](../practices/anapanasati.md)

### 8.p275 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p275"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence moderate

The simile continued: when he has rested, bathed, drunk and lies in cool shade with a wet cloth on his chest, the breaths become subtle and doubtful whether they exist. So too for the meditator: before grasping, there is no attending, adverting or reviewing "I calm the coarser and coarser bodily formation"; after grasping there is, so the formation becomes subtler. The ancients said: when body and mind are disturbed it goes on excessively; when the body is undisturbed it goes on subtly. The reciters differ on how the formation becomes subtle through the stages of jhāna: the Dīgha and Saṃyutta reciters say it is gross in grasping, subtle in the access of the first jhāna, gross there, subtle in the first jhāna, and so on up to the fourth jhāna, in which it is very subtle and reaches non-occurrence; the Majjhima reciters say gross in the first jhāna and subtle in the access of the second, and so on, each higher access subtler. By all agreement the formation occurring before grasping is calmed on grasping, that in each access calmed in the jhāna next above. In insight the formation is gross when unlimited by grasping, subtle when it grasps the great elements, and so on through form, mind, conditions, and the seeing of mind-and-matter with its conditions, to insight with characteristics as object, gross in weak insight and subtle in strong.

assa te assāsapassāsā sukhumā honti: atthi nu kho natthī ti

### 8.p276 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p276"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence moderate

The Paṭisambhidā's explanation with objection and answer: the bodily formations are the in- and out-breaths, states of the body bound up with the body; calming, stopping and stilling them, he trains. Objection: if he trains in stilling all bodily movement (bending, swaying, trembling and so on), then there would be no apprehension of the wind, no knowing of the in- and out-breaths, no mindfulness of breathing and no concentration of mindfulness of breathing, and the wise would not enter or emerge from this attainment; the answer: on the contrary, in the calming the wind is known and the wise do enter and emerge. The comparison follows: when a bell is struck, first gross sounds are heard, and because the sign of the gross sounds was well grasped, well attended and well noted, after the gross sound has ceased subtle sounds arise with the sign well grasped (continued on p. 277).

### 8.p277 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p277"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence moderate

Bell simile concluded: as gross sounds are followed by subtle sounds and then by the sign of subtle sound as object, so first gross breaths occur, then, the sign of the gross breaths being well grasped, subtle ones, and after the subtle breaths cease, the mind, having the sign of the subtle breath as object, does not go astray. So the wind is apprehended, and the wise enter and emerge from the attainment. Thus the breaths are the body as ground, mindfulness and knowledge contemplating, and it is called developing the establishment of mindfulness by contemplating the body in the body. This is the word-by-word commentary on the first tetrad, spoken by way of contemplating the body.

> pacchā sukhumasaddanimittārammaṇatā1 pi cittaṃ pavattati;

kānaṃ assāsapassāsānaṃ nimittaṃ suggahitattā2 sumanasika-

pacchā sukhumakā assāsapassāsā pavattanti, sukhumānaṃ  
assāsapassāsānam pi nimittaṃ suggahitattā2 sumanasikatattā  
sūpadhāritattā, niruddhe pi sukhumake assāsapassāse, atha  
pacchā sukhuma-assāsapassāsanimittārammaṇatā3 pi cittaṃ na

hoti, assāsapassāsānañ ca pabhāvanā hoti, ānāpānasatiyā ca

### 8.p277 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p277-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Since in this text the first tetrad is given as the subject for a beginner (ādikammika), while the other three tetrads are given for one who has already attained jhāna, by way of contemplating feelings, mind and mental objects, the clansman who wishes, having developed this subject, to reach arahatship together with the analytical knowledges through insight founded on the fourfold jhāna of breathing, should, having done the preliminary tasks such as purifying virtue, learn the five-link subject (pañcasandhika) from a teacher of the sort described. The five links are learning (uggaha), questioning (paripucchā), establishing (upaṭṭhāna), absorption (appanā) and the characteristic (lakkhaṇa): the learning of the subject, the questioning about it, its establishing, its absorption, and its characteristic.

### 8.p278 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p278"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The five links explained: the characteristic is the discerning of the subject's own nature ("this subject has this characteristic"). One who learns the five-link subject neither wearies himself nor troubles his teacher; so, having had a little taught and recited it for a long time, having learned the subject in five links, he lives, whether near the teacher or elsewhere, in a fit lodging as described, having cut the minor impediments, done the meal, dispelled the drowsiness of food, sat comfortably, and gladdened his mind by recollecting the virtues of the Triple Gem; not forgetting a single word of the teacher's teaching, he attends to this subject of mindfulness of breathing.

### 8.p278 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p278-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The method of attention (manasikāra) has eight terms: counting (gaṇanā), connecting (anubandhanā), touching (phusanā), fixing (ṭhapanā), noting (sallakkhaṇā), turning away (vivaṭṭanā), purification (pārisuddhi), and looking back on these (paṭipassanā). Counting is counting itself; connecting is following; touching is the place touched; fixing is absorption; noting is insight; turning away is the path; purification is the fruit; looking back is reviewing. Of these, the beginner first attends by counting: he must not count fewer than five nor more than ten, and must not make a gap in between. If he stops below five, consciousness struggles in a confined space, like a herd of cattle shut in a cramped pen; if he goes above ten, consciousness comes to depend on the counting; if he breaks the count, the mind wavers ("has my subject reached its peak or not?"). So he avoids these faults. He first counts slowly, like a measurer of grain, who fills the measure, says "one", and pours it out, and when he sees some rubbish while filling again says "one, one" as he throws it away; and so for two, two and the rest; so the meditator takes whatever breath is evident and says "one, one" (continued on p. 279).

> tatrāyaṃ manasikāravidhi:- gaṇanā, anubandhanā, phu-  
sanā, ṭhapanā, sallakkhaṇā, vivaṭṭanā, pārisuddhi, tesañ ca  
paṭipassanā ti|1 tattha gaṇanā ti gaṇanā yeva| anubandhanā  
ti anuvahanā| phusanā ti phuṭṭhaṭṭhānaṃ| ṭhapanā ti  
appanā| sallakkhaṇā ti vipassanā| vivaṭṭanā ti maggo|

khaṇā|

upari na netabbaṃ, antarā khaṇḍaṃ na dassetabbaṃ| pañ-

vipphandati, sambādhe vaje sanniruddhagogaṇo viya da-

me kammaṭṭhānaṃ, no ti cittaṃ vikampati; tasmā ete dose  
vajjetvā gaṇetabbaṃ| gaṇentena ca paṭhamaṃ dandhaga-

### 8.p279 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p279"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Counting continued. Up to "ten, ten" he counts, having noted the breath as each occurs. When the going out and coming in breaths become evident to him as he counts so, he should give up the slow counting like the grain-measurer and count quickly, in the herdsman's way: a skilful herdsman, with pebbles in the fold of his cloth and a stick in hand, goes to the pen at dawn, strikes the backs of the cattle, sits on the gate-post and counts each animal as it reaches the gate ("one, two") throwing a pebble each time; the cattle, distressed through the night in the cramped space, come out in bunches and he counts "three, four, five, ten" without stopping. So the meditator, when the breaths become evident and keep passing quickly, counts them in the herdsman's way, taking only those that reach the gate (the place touched), "one, two, three, four, five; one ... six, ... ten", quickly. For a subject bound by counting, consciousness becomes one-pointed by the power of counting, as a boat in a strong current is held by a rudder. When the subject seems to go on without break, then, not seizing the wind inside or outside, he counts as before with speed. If consciousness goes in with the inner wind, the inside seems full of wind, as if filled with fat; if it goes out with the outer wind, consciousness is scattered among many external objects; development succeeds for one who develops by setting mindfulness at the place touched.

### 8.p279 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p279-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

How long is it to be counted? Until, even without counting, mindfulness stands firm on the object of in- and out-breath (continued on p. 280).

> kīva ciraṃ pan'; etaṃ gaṇetabban ti? yāva vinā gaṇa-

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

practices: [Mindfulness of breathing (ānāpānassati)](../practices/anapanasati.md), [Counting the breath (gaṇanā), first attention of mindfulness of breathing](../practices/anapanasati-counting.md)

### 8.p280 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p280"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The purpose of counting is only to establish mindfulness on the object of in- and out-breath by cutting off thoughts scattered outside. Having attended by counting, he attends by connecting (anubandhanā): withdrawing the counting, he follows the in- and out-breaths continuously with mindfulness, but not by following their beginning, middle and end. For the outgoing wind the navel is the beginning, the heart the middle, the nose-tip the end; for the incoming wind the nose-tip is the beginning, the heart the middle, the navel the end; and when he follows it so, his consciousness, scattered, becomes agitated and unsteady, as the text says: when he follows the beginning, middle and end of the in-breath with mindfulness, body and mind become agitated, unsteady and shaky through consciousness scattered inwardly; and likewise for the out-breath through consciousness scattered outwardly. So one who attends by connecting should not attend by beginning, middle and end, but by touching (phusanā) and fixing (ṭhapanā). There is no attention by touching and fixing separate from counting and connecting as there is between those two; counting just at the place touched, he attends by counting and touching.

### 8.p280 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p280-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Counting withdrawn there, following the touched places with mindfulness and fixing the mind by way of absorption, he is said to attend by connecting, touching and fixing. This is to be understood by the simile of the lame man (paṅgula) and the doorkeeper found in the commentaries and by the simile of the saw in the Paṭisambhidā. The lame man's simile begins: as a lame man, having pushed the swing of a mother and child and sitting at the foot of the swing-post, sees the two ends and the middle of the plank as it comes and goes (continued on p. 281).

kīḷataṃ mātāputtānaṃ dolaṃ khipitvā, tatth'; eva dolā  
thambhamūle nisinno kamena āgacchantassa ca gacchantas-

### 8.p281 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p281"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The lame man's simile concluded: he sees both ends and the middle of the swing-plank without exerting himself to see them; so the monk, seated at the post of mindfulness, pushes the swing of the in- and out-breath and, sitting there at the sign with mindfulness, follows the beginning, middle and end of the breaths at the place they touch as they come and go, fixing the mind there, and sees without exerting himself to see them. The doorkeeper simile: a doorkeeper at a city gate does not examine the people inside and outside ("who are you? where from? where going? what in your hand?") for they are not his burden, but examines only those who reach the gate; so for the monk the wind that has gone in and gone out is not the burden, only that which has reached the gate is.

> sa ca dolāphalakassa ubho koṭiyo majjhañ ca passati, na ca  
ubho-koṭi-majjhānaṃ dassanatthaṃ vyāvaṭo hoti, evam eva  
bhikkhu sativasena upanibandhanatthambhamūle ṭhatvā

gacchanto, tattha ca cittaṃ ṭhapento passati, na ca tesaṃ  
dassanatthaṃ vyāvaṭo hoti:- ayaṃ pangulopamā|  
     ayaṃ pana dovārikūpamā:- seyyathāpi dovāriko naga-  
rassa anto ca bahi ca purise: ko tvaṃ? kuto vā āgato?  
kuhiṃ vā gacchasi? kiṃ vā te hatthe ti na vīmamsati,--na

ca bahi nikkhantavātā ca na bhārā honti, dvārappattā dvā-  
rappattā yeva bhārā ti ayaṃ dovārikūpamā|

### 8.p281 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-8-p281-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The saw simile is to be understood from the beginning. The text quotes: "The sign, the in-breath and the out-breath are not the object of a single consciousness; for one who does not know these three things development is not obtained. The sign, the in-breath and the out-breath are not the object of a single consciousness; for one who knows these three things development is obtained." How can these three not be the object of a single consciousness, yet not be unknown, the mind not become distracted, the effort be evident, the work be done and distinction be reached? As when a log is placed on level ground and a man saws it: his mindfulness is established by the saw-teeth where they touch the log, without his attending to the teeth as they come and go, yet they are not unknown to him as they come and go; the effort is evident, the work is done and distinction is reached. As the log is, so is the sign that serves as the anchor (of mindfulness); as the saw-teeth are, so are the in- and out-breaths (the passage continues on p. 282).

> kakacūpamā pana ādito paṭṭhāya evaṃ veditabbā|

          jānato va tayo dhamme bhāvanā upalabbhatī ti|  
     kathaṃ ime tayo dhammā ekacittassa ārammanā na honti,  
na c'; ime tayo dhammā aviditā honti, na ca cittaṃ vikkhepaṃ

### 9 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Loving-kindness should not be developed first towards a disliked person, a very dear friend, a neutral person or an enemy, nor specifically towards the opposite sex, and never towards the dead; first of all it is developed repeatedly towards oneself: 'May I be happy and free from suffering', or 'May I keep myself free from enmity, affliction and anxiety and live happily'.

> sabbapaṭhamaṃ pana: ahaṃ sukhito homi, niddukkho ti vā avero avyāpajjo anīgho sukhī attānaṃ pariharāmī ti vā evaṃ punappunaṃ attani yeva bhāvetabbā.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation, devotion · stage: beginner · types: practice, ethics_

terms: [mettā](../terms/metta.md) · practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md)

_Superseded by [9.p296](visuddhimagga.md#tea-visuddhimagga-9-p296)_

### 9 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

When the barriers are broken down — when loving-kindness is the same towards oneself, the dear, the neutral and the enemy (as tested by the simile of bandits demanding one of the group) — at that moment the sign and access are obtained; developing that sign, absorption follows.

> evaṃ sīmāsambhedasamakālam eva ca iminā bhikkhunā nimittañ ca upacārañ ca laddhaṃ hoti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: intermediate · types: practice_

terms: [sīmāsambheda](../terms/simasambheda.md) · practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md)

_Superseded by [9.p307](visuddhimagga.md#tea-visuddhimagga-9-p307)_

### 9 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-3"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

One whose resentment persists should admonish himself, recall the good qualities of the other, reflect that beings are owners of their kamma, recall the Bodhisatta's patience, reflect on the long round in which all have been one's relatives, on the benefits of loving-kindness, resolve the other into elements, and finally give a gift.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation, action · stage: beginner · types: practice, ethics_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md)

_Superseded by [9.p298](visuddhimagga.md#tea-visuddhimagga-9-p298)_

### 9 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-4"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Each divine abiding has a near and a far enemy: greed is the near enemy of loving-kindness and ill will its far enemy; home-based grief is the near enemy of compassion and cruelty the far; home-based joy the near enemy of gladness and aversion (boredom) the far; the home-based equanimity of unknowing the near enemy of equanimity, and greed and resentment the far.

> mettā-brahmavihārassa hi samīpacāro viya purisassa sapatto guṇadassanasabhāgatāya rāgo āsannapaccatthiko.

### 9.p295 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p295"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter IX opens with the divine abidings (brahmavihāra). Of the four (loving-kindness, compassion, gladness, equanimity), a beginner (ādikammika) who wishes to develop loving-kindness (mettā) should have cut the impediments and taken the subject; then, after the meal and after dispelling the food-drowsiness, seated comfortably in a secluded place on a well-prepared seat, he should first reflect on the danger (ādīnava) in hate (dosa) and the benefit (ānisaṃsa) of patience (khanti).

> anussatikammaṭṭhānānantaraṃ uddiṭṭhesu pana mettā-  
karuṇā-muditā-upekkhā ti imesu catūsu brahmavihāresu  
mettaṃ bhāvetukāmena tāva ādikammikena yogāvacarena  
upacchinnapaḷibodhena1 gahitakammaṭṭhānena bhattakic-  
caṃ katvā bhattasammadaṃ paṭivinodetvā vivitte padese  
supaññatte āsane sukhanisinnena ādito tāva dose ādīnavo  
khantiyañ ca ānisaṃso paccavekkhitabbo|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

### 9.p295 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p295-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Why begin with reflection. Hate is to be abandoned and patience to be attained by this development, and one cannot abandon what one has not seen the danger in, nor gain what one does not know the benefit of. So the danger in hate is to be seen by texts such as "the hating man, overcome by hate, with mind seized, may even kill a living being", and the benefit of patience by texts such as "patience is the highest austerity, endurance; the Buddhas call nibbāna supreme"; the verse on one whose strength is patience, "him I call a brahmin"; and "nothing is greater than patience".

> kasmā? imāya hi bhāvanāya doso pahātabbo, khanti  
adhigantabbā, na ca sakkā kiñci adiṭṭhādīnavaṃ pahātuṃ  
aviditānisaṃsaṃ vā adhigantuṃ; tasmā: duṭṭho kho, āvuso,  
dosena abhibhūto pariyādiṇṇacitto pāṇam pi hantī ti2 ādīnaṃ  
vasena dose ādīnavo daṭṭhabbo|  
          khanti paramaṃ tapo titikkhā nibbānaṃ paramaṃ va-  
danti buddhā3;  
          khantibalaṃ bālānīkaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi brāhmaṇaṃ4|  
         khantyā bhiyyo na vijjatī ti5  
ādīnaṃ vasena khantiyaṃ ānisaṃso veditabbo|

### 9.p295 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p295-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Then, to separate the mind from hate whose danger has been seen, and to join it with patience whose benefit has been known, the development of loving-kindness is to be begun. Beginning, one must first know the kinds of persons: toward which persons loving-kindness is not to be developed first, and toward which persons it is to be developed.

> ath'; evaṃ diṭṭhādīnavato dosato cittaṃ vivecanatthāya  
viditānisaṃsāya ca khantiyā saṃyojanatthāya mettābhā-  
vanā ārabhitabbā| ārabhantena ca ādito va puggalabhedo  
jānitabbo,6 imesu puggalesu mettā paṭhamaṃ [na7] bhāve-

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md)

### 9.p296 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p296"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Whom not to begin with. This loving-kindness is not to be developed first toward a disliked person, a very dear friend, a neutral person or an enemy; not specifically (odhiso) toward one of the opposite sex; and not at all toward one who is dead. Reasons: setting a disliked person in the place of a dear one is tiring; setting a very dear friend in the place of a neutral one is tiring, and when even a small pain comes to him one is as if weeping; a neutral person put in the place of a respected or dear one is tiring; recalling an enemy brings anger. Toward the opposite sex, desire (rāga) arises: a minister's son asked an elder toward whom to develop loving-kindness, was told "a dear person", took his own wife, and fought a wall-battle all night. Toward the dead one reaches neither absorption nor access: a young monk who developed it toward his teacher could not enter absorption until he found the "sign", learned that his teacher was dead, and took another person as object.

> ayaṃ hi mettā appiya-  
puggale atippiyasahāyake majjhatte verīpuggale ti imesu  
catūsu paṭhamaṃ na bhāvetabbā; lingavisabhāge odhiso  
na bhāvetabbā, kālankate na bhāvetabbā va| kiṅkāraṇā  
appiyādisu paṭhamaṃ na bhāvetabbā? appiyaṃ hi piyaṭ-  
ṭhāne ṭhapento kilamatiatippiyasahāyakaṃ majjhattaṭ-  
ṭhāne ṭhapento kilamati, appamattake pi c'; assa dukkhe  
uppanne ārodanākārapatto viya hoti| majjhattaṃ garuṭ-  
ṭhāne ca piyaṭṭhāne ca ṭhapento kilamati| veriṃ samanus-  
sarato2 kodho uppajjati, tasmā appiyādisu paṭhamaṃ na  
bhāvetabbā| lingavisabhāge pana tam eva ārabbha odhiso  
bhāventassa rāgo uppajjati| aññataro kira amaccaputto  
kulūpakattheraṃ pucchi: bhante, kassa mettā bhāvetabbā?  
ti| thero: piyapuggale ti āha| tassa attano bhariyā piyā  
hoti, so tassā mettaṃ bhāvento sabbarattiṃ bhittiyuddham  
akāsi| tasmā lingavisabhāge odhiso na bhāvetabbā| kālan-  
kate pana bhāvento neva appanaṃ, na upacāraṃ pāpu-  
ṇāti| aññataro kira daharabhikkhu ācariyaṃ ārabbha

nasamāpatti, na ca naṃ samāpajjituṃ sakkomi| kiṃ nu

āha| so gavesanto ācariyassa matabhāvaṃ ñatvā aññaṃ

### 9.p296 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p296-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

First of all, loving-kindness is to be developed toward oneself, repeatedly: "may I be happy and free from suffering", or "may I keep myself well, without hate, without ill will, without trouble, happy". There follows the question why, if so, the Vibhaṅga passage ("just as one would feel loving-kindness on seeing one dear, agreeable person, so he pervades all beings with loving-kindness") and the Paṭisambhidā passage ("may all beings be free from hate, ill will, trouble; may they keep themselves happy") do not mention oneself. (The answer follows on p. 297.)

> sabbapaṭhamaṃ pana: ahaṃ sukhito homi, niddukkho  
ti vā avero avyāpajjo anīgho sukhī attānaṃ pariharāmī ti vā  
evaṃ punappunaṃ attani yeva bhāvetabbā| evaṃ sante  
yaṃ vibhange vuttaṃ: kathañ ca, bhikkhave, bhikkhu  
mettāsahagatena cetasā ekaṃ disaṃ pharitvā viharati? seyya-  
thā pi nāma ekaṃ puggalaṃ piyaṃ manāpaṃ disvā mettā-  
yeyya, evaṃ eva sabbasatte3 mettāya pharatī ti;4 yañ ca paṭi-  
sambhidāyaṃ: katamehi pañcah'; ākārehi anodhiso pharaṇā  
mettā-cetovimutti? sabbe sattā averā [hontu], avyāpajjhā,  
anīghā, sukhī attānaṃ pariharantu| sabbe pāṇā, sabbe

### 9.p297 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p297"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The apparent conflict resolved. The texts on all beings do not mention oneself, so one might think developing toward oneself contradicts them. The text says it does not: those texts speak of absorption (appanā), while developing loving-kindness toward oneself is meant to make oneself the witness (sakkhibhāva). Even if for a hundred or a thousand years one developed loving-kindness toward oneself with "may I be happy", absorption would not arise from it.

> yañ ca mettāsutte: sukhino vā khemino hontu, sabbe sattā  
bhavantu sukhitattā ti2 ādivuttaṃ, taṃ virujjhati, na hi tattha  
attani bhāvanā vuttā ti ce, tañ ca na virujjhati| kasmā?  
taṃ hi appanāvasena vuttaṃ, idaṃ sakkhibhāvavasena|  
sace pi hi vassasataṃ vassasahassaṃ vā: ahaṃ sukhito ho-  
mī ti ādinā nayena attani mettaṃ bhāveti, nev'; assa appanā  
uppajjati|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md)

### 9.p297 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p297-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

What developing toward oneself does: for one who thinks "may I be happy", the thought arises, taking oneself as witness, "as I want happiness, dislike suffering, want to live and not to die, so do other beings", and a wish for the welfare and happiness of other beings arises. The Blessed One showed this method in the verse: "having gone in mind through all directions, one finds nothing dearer than oneself; so dear is the self to others, therefore one who wishes his own good should not harm another."

> ahaṃ sukhito homī ti bhāvayato pana: yathā ahaṃ sukha-  
kāmo dukkhapaṭikūlo jīvitukāmo amaritukāmo ca, evaṃ  
aññe pi sattā ti attānaṃ sakkhiṃ katvā aññasattesu hita-  
sukhakāmatā uppajjati| bhagavatā pi  
               sabbā disā anuparigamma cetasā,  
               nev'; ajjhagā piyataram attanā kvaci;  
               evaṃ piyo puthu attā paresaṃ,  
               tasmā na hiṃse paraṃ atthakāmo ti3

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, ethics_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md)

### 9.p297 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p297-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The order. So, for the sake of the witness-state, one first pervades oneself with loving-kindness, then, for the occurring of happiness, one develops it toward a person who is dear and agreeable, a teacher or one like a teacher, a preceptor or one like a preceptor, recalling his endearing words and the virtue and learning that make him dear and respected: "may this good man be happy and free from suffering". Absorption comes about easily in such a person. But the bhikkhu who wishes to break down the barriers (sīmasambheda) must not be content with that; next he develops it toward a very dear friend, from him toward a neutral person, and from the neutral toward an enemy. At each step he should make the mind soft and workable in one part before extending to the next. One who has no enemy, or who, being of great nature, has no perception of an enemy even when wronged, need not strive to produce one.

> vadatā ayaṃ nayo dassito, tasmā sakkhibhāvatthaṃ paṭha-  
maṃ attānaṃ mettāya pharitvā tad-anantaraṃ sukhappa-  
vattanatthaṃ yvāyaṃ piyo manāpo garubhāvanīyo ācariyo  
vā ācariyamatto vā, upajjhāyo vā upajjhāyamatto vā,  
tassa tāni piyavacanādīni piyamanāpattakāraṇāni sīlasutā-

puriso sukhī hotu niddukkho ti ādinā nayena mettā bhāve-

hāyake, atippiyasahāyakato majjhatte, majjhattato verīpug-  
gale mettā bhāvetabbā| bhāventena ca ekekasmiṃ koṭ-

### 9.p298 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p298"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

If resentment arises on directing the mind to the enemy (recalling the wrong he did), the meditator should, again and again, enter loving-kindness toward one of the earlier persons and, having emerged, again turn loving-kindness to the enemy in order to dispel the resentment. If, striving thus, it does not subside, he should strive to remove it repeatedly by recalling the Buddha's advice such as the simile of the saw, admonishing himself: "You angry man, has the Blessed One not said: even if bandits were to saw you limb from limb with a two-handled saw, whoever gave way to hate would not be doing my teaching?"

> va na kātabbo| yassa pana atthi, taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ:  
majjhattato verīpuggale mettā bhāvetabbā ti| sace pan'

raṇena paṭighaṃ uppajjati, athā 'nena purimapuggalesu  
yattha katthaci punappunaṃ mettaṃ samāpajjitvā vuṭṭha-  
hitvā punappunaṃ tam puggalaṃ mettāyantena paṭighaṃ  
vinodetabbaṃ| sace evam pi vāyamato na nibbāti, atha

### 9.p298 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p298-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The Buddha's verse: worse for him is he who returns anger for an angry man; not returning anger, one wins a battle hard to win; one acts for the good of both, oneself and the other, who, knowing the other angry, mindfully becomes calm. Then the seven things that please an enemy, addressed to a man or woman who becomes angry, begin: the enemy wishes for his foe "may he be ill-favoured"; but an angry man, overcome by anger, is ill-favoured even if well bathed, anointed, groomed and dressed in white; this is the first thing; the further wishes, abbreviated with ellipses in the text, are "may he meet suffering", "may he not have great benefit (pacurattha)", "may he not be wealthy", "may he not be famous", "may he have no friends".

naṃ āgacchati itthiṃ vā purisaṃ vā|

ti:- aho vatāyaṃ dukkhaṃ passeyyā ti! |||pe||| na pa-

### 9.p299 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p299"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The seventh thing: the enemy wishes "may he, at the breaking up of the body after death, not go to a good destination, heaven"; but an angry man does bad deeds in body, speech and mind and, after death, is reborn in a bad destination, hell. The angry man is like a torch burnt at both ends and smeared with dung in the middle, useless as timber either in village or forest. The meditator tells himself that, being angry, he is not doing the Blessed One's teaching, is worse than the one who returns anger and will not win the hard battle, and will himself do what pleases his enemies; he will be like the burnt torch. If the resentment subsides, well; if not, he should recall whatever quality in that person is calm and pure, which brings confidence when recalled, and by it dispel the anger.

> na kāyassa bhedā paraṃ maraṇā sugatiṃ saggaṃ lokaṃ upa-  
pajjeyyā ti| taṃ kissa hetu? na, bhikkhave, sapatto sapat-  
tassa sugatigamanena nandati| kodhano 'yaṃ, bhikkhave,  
purisapuggalo kodhābhibhūto kodhapareto kāyena duccaritaṃ  
carati, vācāya, manasā duccaritaṃ carati, so kāyena vācāya  
manasā duccaritaṃ caritvā kāyassa bhedā paraṃ maraṇā  
apāyaṃ duggatiṃ vinipātaṃ nirayaṃ upapajjati kodhābhi-  
bhūto ti1 ca: seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, chavālātaṃ ubhato padit-  
taṃ majjhe gūthagataṃ neva gāme kaṭṭhatthaṃ pharati, na  
araññe kaṭṭhaṭṭhaṃ pharati | | | tathūpamāhaṃ, bhikkhave,  
imaṃ [purisa-] puggalaṃ vadāmī tī ca,2 so dāni tvaṃ evaṃ  
kujjhanto na ceva bhagavato sāsanakaro bhavissasi, paṭi-  
kujjhanto ca kuddhapurisato pi pāpiyo hutvā na dujjayaṃ  
sangāmaṃ jessasi| sapattakaraṇe ca dhamme attā va attano  
karissasi| chavālātūpamo ca bhavissasī ti| tass'; evaṃ  
ghaṭayato vāyamato sace taṃ paṭighaṃ vūpasammati, icc'  
etaṃ kusalaṃ| no ce vūpasammati, atha yo yo dhammo  
tassa puggalassa vūpasanto hoti parisuddho, anussariya-  
māno pasādaṃ āvahati, taṃ taṃ anussaritvā āghāto paṭi-  
vinetabbo|

### 9.p299 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p299-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Recalling the good in the person who has wronged him. In one person only the bodily conduct is calm, and this is known to all from his many duties well done; his verbal and mental conduct are not calm; ignoring those, only the calm bodily conduct should be recalled. In another only the verbal conduct is calm, known from his skill at greetings, pleasant speech and sweet voice in teaching the Dhamma; ignoring the rest, recall that. In another only the mental conduct is calm (continued on p. 300).

> ekaccassa hi kāyasamācāro va upasanto hoti| upasanta-

santā honti, tassa te acintetvā kāyasamācāravūpasamo yeva  
anussaritabbo| ekaccassa vacīsamācāro va upasanto hoti,  
upasantabhāvo c'; assa sabbajanena ñāyati| so hi pakatiyā  
ca paṭisanthārakusalo hoti sakhilo sukhasambhāso sammo-  
dako uttānamukho pubbabhāsī madhurena sarena dham-  
maṃ osāreti, parimaṇḍalehi padavyañjanehi dhammaka-  
thaṃ katheti| kāyasamācāra-manosamācārā pana avūpa-  
santā honti, tassa te acintetvā vacīsamācāravūpasamo yeva  
anussaritabbo| ekaccassa manosamācāro va upasanto hoti,  
upasantabhāvo c'; assa cetiyavandanādisu sabbajanassa pā-  
kaṭo hoti| yo hi avūpasantacitto hoti, so cetiyaṃ vā bo-  
dhiṃ vā there vā vandamāno na sakkaccaṃ vandati, dham-

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p300 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p300"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Mental conduct as the calm quality: in one person only the mental conduct is calm, and this shows in all when he pays homage at a shrine and so on. One whose mind is not calm pays homage to the shrine, the Bodhi tree or the elders carelessly, and sits in the Dhamma hall distracted or nodding; the calm-minded pays homage attentively and listens to the Dhamma with a purposeful mind, showing confidence by body or speech. If in a person none of the three is calm, compassion (kāruñña) should be set up toward him: "though he now walks the world of men, in a few days he will be filling the eight great hells and the sixteen extra hells"; for compassion too dispels resentment. If all three are calm, he may recall any of them, since loving-kindness toward such a person is not difficult. For the fuller meaning the Āghātapaṭivinaya-sutta in the Book of Fives is to be expanded, which gives five ways by which a resentment that has arisen in a bhikkhu is to be entirely removed.

> masavaṇamaṇḍape1 vikkhittacitto vā pacalāyanto vā nisī-  
dati; upasantacitto pana okappetvā sakkaccaṃ2 vandati,  
ohitasoto aṭṭhiṅkatvā kāyena vā vācāya vā cittappasādaṃ  
karonto dhammaṃ suṇāti| iti ekaccassa manosamācāro va  
upasanto hoti, kāyavacīsamācārā avūpasantā honti, tassa  
te acintetvā manosamācāravūpasamo yeva anussaritabbo|

hoti, tasmiṃ puggale: kiñcāpi esa idāni manussaloke carati  
atha kho katipāhassa accayena aṭṭha mahāniraya-soḷasa-us-  
sadanirayaparipūrako bhavissatī ti kāruññaṃ ṭhapetabbaṃ|  
kāruññam pi hi paṭicca āghāto vūpasammati| ekaccassa  
tayo pi 'me dhammā vūpasantā honti, tassa naṃ yaṃ ic-  
chati, taṃ taṃ anussaritabbaṃ, tādise hi puggale na duk-  
karā hoti mettābhāvanā ti| imassa ca atth'; assa āvibhāvat-  
thaṃ: pañc 'ime āvuso āghātapaṭivinayā, yattha bhikkhuno  
uppanno āghāto sabbaso paṭivinodetabbo ti3
