practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p300 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p300-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

If resentment still arises, the meditator should admonish himself with verses. In sum: if an enemy has done you harm in his own domain, why do you want to do harm in your own mind's domain? Having left your kin who helped you much, with a weeping face, why do you not give up anger, the great cause of harm, your enemy? Anger cuts the root of the virtues you guard; who is as foolish as you to pet it? If another has done a wrong deed and you are angry at that, do you yourself want to do the same? If another has done what is disagreeable to you wishing to hurt you, why fulfil his wish by making hate arise in yourself? Whether or not you will make him suffer by your anger, you afflict yourself now with the pain of anger. If enemies have taken the harmful road of anger, why are you following them in anger? Cut the very fault in you because of which your enemy did what displeases you: why torment yourself for no reason?

> sace pan'; assa evam pi vāyamato āghāto uppajjati yeva,  
athā 'nena evam attā ovaditabbo:-  
          attano visaye dukkhaṃ katan te yadi verinā,  
          kiṃ tassā visaye dukkhaṃ sacitte kattum icchasi?  
          bahūpakāraṃ hitvāna ñātivaggaṃ rudaṃ mukhaṃ  
          mahānatthakaraṃ kodhaṃ sapattaṃ na jahāsi kiṃ?  
          yāni rakkhasi sīlāni, tesaṃ mūlanikantanaṃ  
          kodhaṃ nām'; upaḷālesi4-ko tayā sadiso jaḷo?  
          kataṃ anariyaṃ kammaṃ parena iti kujjhasi:  
          kin5 nu tvaṃ tādisaṃ yeva so6 sayaṃ kattum icchasi?  
          dosetukāmo yadi taṃ, amanāpaṃ paro kari:  
          dosuppādena tass'; eva kiṃ pūresi manorathaṃ?  
          dukkhaṃ tassa ca nāma tvaṃ, kuddho kāhasi vā na vā,  
          attānaṃ pan'; idān'; eva kodhadukkhena bādhasi?  
          kodhaṃ vā ahitaṃ maggaṃ arūḷhā yadi verino,  
          kasmā tuvam pi kujjhanto tesaṃ yevā 'nusikkhasi?  
          yaṃ dosaṃ tava nissāya sattunā appiyaṃ kataṃ,  
          tam eva dosaṃ chindassu kim aṭṭhāne vihaññasi?

### 9.p301 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p301"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The last verses: since states are momentary, the aggregates by which the disagreeable thing was done to you have ceased: with whom are you now angry? He who does harm to another does it to someone; without that other he could not; and you yourself are a cause of suffering, so why are you angry with him?

> khaṇikattā ca dhammānaṃ, yehi khandhehi te kataṃ  
          amanāpaṃ niruddhā te kassa dānī 'dha kujjhasi?  
          dukkhaṃ karoti yo yassa, taṃ vinā kassa so kare;  
          sayam pi dukkhahetu tvam iti kiṃ tassa kujjhasī ti?

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p301 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p301-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

If resentment does not subside even so, the meditator should reflect on the ownership of kamma, his own and the other's. His own: "What will you achieve by being angry with him? Does not this kamma, rooted in hate, lead to your own harm? You are the owner of your kamma, heir to your kamma, born of kamma, kin to kamma, with kamma as refuge; whatever kamma you do you will inherit. This kamma of yours is not able to bring about awakening, private awakening, the disciple's stage, Brahmā-hood, Sakka-hood, universal kingship, or any such attainment; it leads to losing the dispensation and to being a scrap-eater and to various sufferings of hell and the like; in doing it you are like a man who seizes glowing coals or dung with both hands to strike another, burning and befouling yourself first." The other's kamma is to be reflected on the same way: he too is owner of his kamma; what he does will bring harm to himself, like a man throwing dust against the wind.

> sace pan'; assa evaṃ attānaṃ ovadato pi paṭighaṃ neva  
vūpasammati, athā 'nena attano ca parassa ca kammassa  
katā paccavekkhitabbā| tattha attano tāva ekaṃ pacca-  
vekkhitabbā: ambho! tvaṃ tassa kuddho kiṃ karissasi?  
nanu tav'; eva c'; etaṃ dosanidānaṃ kammaṃ anatthāya  
saṃvattissati? kammassako hi tvaṃ, kammadāyādo, kamma-  
yoni, kammabandhu kammappaṭisaraṇo,1 yaṃ kammaṃ  
karissasi tassa dāyādo bhavissasi, idañ ca te kammaṃ neva

na brahmattasakkattacakkavattipadesarājādi-sampattīnaṃ  
aññataraṃ sampattiṃ sādhetuṃ samatthaṃ, atha kho sā-  
sanato cāvetvā vighāsādādibhāvassa ceva nerayikādiduk-  
khavisesānañ ca te saṃvattanikam idaṃ kammaṃ, so tvam  
idaṃ karonto ubhohi hatthehi vītaccike2 vā angāre gūthaṃ  
vā gahetvā paraṃ paharitukāmo puriso viya attānam eva  
paṭhamaṃ dahasi ceva duggandhañ ca karosī ti| evaṃ attano  
kammassa kataṃ paccavekkhitvā parassa pi evaṃ paccavek-  
khitabbā:- so pi tava kujjhitvā kiṃ karissati? nanu etass'  
ev'; etaṃ anatthāya saṃvattissati? kammassako hi ayam  
āyasmā kammadāyādo |||pe3|||? yaṃ kammaṃ karis-  
sati, tassa dāyādo bhavissati? idañ c'; assa kammaṃ neva

na brahmattasakkattacakkavattipadesarājādisampattīnaṃ

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md), [Ill will (byāpāda)](../obstacles/byapada.md)

### 9.p302 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p302"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The Buddha's verse: who offends an innocent, pure, blameless man, that evil comes back on that very fool like fine dust thrown against the wind. If even so the resentment does not subside, the meditator should recall the Teacher's earlier practice of virtue: "Monk, before his awakening, as a bodhisatta fulfilling the perfections for four incalculables and a hundred thousand eons, your Teacher did not allow his mind to hate even his murderers and enemies." In the Sīlava-jātaka, when a wicked minister who had committed an offence concerning his queen (attano deviyā paduṭṭhena) brought a rival king to take his kingdom of three hundred yojanas, he did not allow the ministers who rose up to defend him even to touch a weapon; and when he was buried with a thousand ministers up to the neck in a charnel ground he made no hatred; after being saved through the digging of jackals and, by a spirit's power, seeing his enemy asleep in the royal bedchamber, he felt no anger, exchanged oaths and made him a friend, saying: "a man should hope, the wise do not despair; I see myself as I wished."

> tam eva bālaṃ pacceti pāpaṃ

chupituṃ na adāsi|4 puna saddhiṃ amaccasahassena āma-

katvā paṭiladdhajīvito yakkhānubhāvena attano sirigab-

akatvā va aññamaññaṃ sapathaṃ katvā taṃ mittaṭṭhāne  
ṭhapayitvā āha:-

### 9.p302 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p302-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The Buddha's earlier patience continued. In the Khantivādī-jātaka, when the foolish king of Kāsi asked "what do you teach, ascetic?" and was told "I teach patience", he had him beaten with thorny whips and his hands and feet cut off, and the bodhisatta showed not even a flicker of anger. The text notes it is no wonder that one gone forth as an old man should do so; in the Cūḷadhammapāla-jātaka the infant prince Dhammapāla, though his hands and feet were cut off on the order of his father, the mighty king, while his mother wailed, showed no anger (continued on p. 303).

> khantivādī-jātake5 dummedhena kāsi-raññā:- kiṃ vādī  
tvaṃ, samaṇā ti puṭṭho: khantivādī nāmā 'han ti vutte sakaṇ-  
ṭakāhi kasāhi tāḷetvā hatthapādesu chijjamānesu kopa-  
mattam pi na akāsi6|  
     anacchariyañ c'; etaṃ, yaṃ mahallako pabbajūpagato  
evaṃ kareyya, cūḷadhammapālajātake7 pana uttānaseyyako  
pi samāno:-  
          candanarasānulittā bāhā chijjanti dhammapālassa,  
          dāyādassa pathavyā, pāṇā me, deva, rujjhantī ti|  
evaṃ vippalapamānāya mātuyā pitarā mahāpatāpena nāma  
raññā vaṃsakaḷiresu viya catūsu hatthapādesu chedāpitesu,

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p303 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p303"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Cūḷadhammapāla concluded: when the king then ordered "cut off his head", the child, thinking "now is the time to hold back your mind; toward the father who ordered the cutting, the man who cuts, the wailing mother, and yourself, be of equal mind", firmly resolved and showed no hint of hate. The text then says that this is no wonder in a human; even as the elephant Chaddanta, pierced in the navel by a poisoned arrow, he did not hate the hunter who had wronged him without cause.

> tāvatā pi santuṭṭhiṃ anāpajjitvā: sīsam assa chindathā  
ti āṇatte: ayaṃ 'dāni te cittasanniggaṇhaṇakālo,1 idāni,  
ambho! dhammapālasīsacchedāṇāpake pitari, sīsacchedake  
purise, paridevamānāya mātari, attani cā ti imesu catūsu  
samacitto hotī ti dalhaṃ samādānam adhiṭṭhāya paduṭṭhā-  
kāramattam pi na akāsi|

### 9.p303 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p303-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The Chaddanta elephant, pierced with a big arrow, asked the hunter with an unhating mind why he had been shot; told the hunter had been sent by the king's chief queen for his tusks, he fulfilled her wish by cutting off his own tusks, bright with six-coloured rays, and gave them. As a great monkey rescued from a mountain pit by a man who then thought to kill and eat him, he did not hate the man, and, though his head was split by a thrown stone, looked at him with tears and said "do not do such a thing; you are one of long life and ought to restrain others", felt no hate, and led him to safety. The story of the serpent king Bhūridatta, who had undertaken the uposatha factors, begins at the foot of the page.

chetvā adāsi|

acintetvā tam eva purisaṃ khemantabhūmiṃ sampāpesi|  
     bhūridatto nāma nāgarājā hutvā uposathangāni adhiṭ-

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p304 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p304"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Bhūridatta, lying on an anthill observing the uposatha, was smeared over his whole body with a drug like the fire at the world's end and carried in a basket around the whole of Jambudīpa as a plaything, yet showed no ill will toward the brahmin; the text quotes his verse that, though thrown in the basket and crushed, he did not get angry from fear of breaking his virtue. Campeyya the serpent king, tormented by a snake-charmer, produced no ill will; and Saṅkhapāla the serpent king, pierced by sharp spears, dragged with a rope through his nose by sixteen hunters' sons, able to reduce them to ashes by a look, did not even open his eye in anger. Each is said to have quoted his own verse of patience as observance of virtue.

> ṭhāya vammikamuddhanisayamāno kappuṭṭhānaggisadisena  
osadhena sakalasarīre siñcayamāno pi, peḷāya pakkhipitvā  
sakala-jambudīpe kīḷapiyamāno pi tasmiṃ brāhmaṇe mano-  
padosamattam pi na akāsi| yath'; āha:-  
          peḷāya pakkhipante pi maddante pi ca pāṇinā,  
          alampāne na kuppāmi sīlakhaṇḍabhayā mamā ti|  
campeyyo pi nāgarājā hutvā1 ahituṇḍikena viheṭhiyamāno  
manopadosamattam pi na uppādesi| yath'; āha:-  
          tadā pi maṃ dhammacāriṃ upavuttaṃ uposathaṃ  
          ahituṇḍiko gahetvāna rājadvāram pi kīḷati|  
          yam so vaṇṇaṃ cintayati nīlaṃ pītañ ca lohitaṃ,  
          tassa cittānuvattanto homi cintitasannibho|  
          thalaṃ kareyyaṃ udakaṃ, udakam pi thalaṃ kare;  
          yadī 'haṃ tassa kuppeyyaṃ khaṇena chārikaṃ kare|  
          yadi cittavasī hessaṃ,2 parihāyissāmi sīlato,  
          sīlena parihīnassa uttamattho na sijjhatī ti|  
     sankhapālanāgarājā hutvā tikhiṇāhi sattīhi aṭṭhasu ṭhā-  
nesu ovijjhitvā pahāramukhehi sakaṇṭakālatāyo3 pavesetvā  
nāsāya daḷhaṃ rajjuṃ pakkhipitvā soḷasahi bhojaputtehi  
kājenādāya vayhamāno dharaṇītale ghaṃsiyamānasarīro4  
mahantaṃ dukkhaṃ paccanubhonto kujjhitvā olokitamat-  
ten'; eva sabbe bhojaputte bhasmaṃ kātuṃ samattho pi sa-  
māno cakkhuṃ ummīletvā paduṭṭhākāramattam pi na akāsi|  
yath'; āha:  
          cātuddasiṃ pañcadasiṃ, aḷāra! uposathaṃ niccam upa-  
vasāmi;  
          ath'; āgamuṃ soḷasa bhojaputtā rajjuṃ gahetvāna daḷhañ  
ca pāsaṃ|  
          chetvāna nāsaṃ atikassa rajjuṃ, nayiṃsu maṃ sampa-  
riggayha luddā;  
          etādisaṃ dukkham ahaṃ titikkhaṃ uposathaṃ appaṭiko-  
payanto ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, ethics, narrative_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p305 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p305"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The Buddha did not do only these; he did many other wonders as in the Jātaka of the elephant who supported his mother. So it is very improper and unbecoming, when one points to the Teacher who attained omniscience and whose virtue of patience has no equal in the world with its gods, to let a hateful thought arise.

> na kevalañ ca etān'; eva, aññāni pi mātuposakajātakā-  
disu1 anekāni acchariyāni akāsi| tassa te idāni sabbaññu-  
taṃ pattaṃ sadevake loke2 kenaci appaṭisamakhantigu-  
ṇaṃ taṃ bhagavantaṃ satthāraṃ apadisato paṭighacittaṃ  
nāma uppādetuṃ ativiya ayuttaṃ appatirūpan ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, ethics_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p305 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p305-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

If the resentment still does not subside for one long enslaved by defilements, the meditator should reflect on the beginningless round: "it is not easy to find a being who has not been mother, father, brother, sister, son or daughter" (SN 15). So toward that person he should think: "in the past he was my mother, carried me ten months in the womb, removed without disgust urine, dung and spittle as if sandalwood, and reared me carrying me on her hip; as father he traded on the road at the risk of his life, entered a battle, crossed the sea for my sake; as brother, sister, son, daughter he did this and that for me; it is not right for me to hate him."

> sace pan'; assa evaṃ satthu pubbacaritaguṇaṃ pacca-  
vekkhato pi dīgharattaṃ kilesānaṃ dāsavyaṃ upagatassa  
neva taṃ paṭighaṃ vūpasammati, athā 'nena anamataggi-  
yāni paccavekkhitabbāni| tatra hi vuttaṃ:- na so, bhik-  
khave, satto sulabharūpo, yo na mātā bhūtapubbo, yo na pitā  
bhūtapubbo, yo na bhātā, yo na bhaginī, yo na putto, yo na  
dhītā bhūtapubbo ti|3 tasmā tasmiṃ puggale evaṃ cittaṃ  
uppādetabbaṃ:- ayaṃ kira me atīte mātā hutvā dasamāse  
kucchiyā pariharitvā muttakarīsakheḷasinghāṇikādīni hari-  
candanaṃ viya ajigucchamānā4 apanetvā, ure nacchāpentī,5  
ankena pariharamānā4 posesi; pitā hutvā ajapathasanku-  
pathādīni gantvā vāṇijjaṃ payojayamāno mayham atthāya  
jīvitam pi pariccajitvā ubhato vyūḷhe sangāme pavisitvā  
nāvāya mahāsamuddaṃ pakkhanditvā aññāni ca dukka-  
rāni karitvā: puttake posessāmī ti tehi tehi upāyehi dha-  
naṃ saṃharitvā mam posesi; bhātā bhaginī putto dhītā ca  
hutvā [pi6], idañ c'; idañ ca upakāraṃ akāsī ti tatra me nap-  
patirūpaṃ manaṃ padūsetun ti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, karma-liberation, ethics_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p305 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p305-3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

If even so the mind cannot be calmed, the meditator should reflect on the benefits of loving-kindness (mettānisaṃsa): "Monk, has the Blessed One not said that eleven benefits are to be expected when the liberation of mind by loving-kindness is practised, developed, made much of, made a vehicle and a basis, established, consolidated and well undertaken? One sleeps happily; wakes happily; sees no bad dream; is dear to human beings; is dear to non-human beings; devas guard him; fire, poison and weapons do not affect him; the mind is quickly concentrated; the complexion is serene; he dies unconfused; and, if he penetrates no higher, he goes to the Brahmā world." (The list ends at the head of p. 306.)

> sace pana evam pi cittaṃ nibbāpetuṃ na sakkoti yeva,  
athā 'nena evaṃ mettānisaṃsā paccavekkhitabbā:- ambho  
pabbajita! nanu vuttaṃ bhagavatā:- mettāya kho, bhik-  
khave, cetovimuttiyā āsevitāya bhāvitāya bahulīkatāya yanī-  
katāya vatthukatāya anuṭṭhitāya paricitāya susamāraddhāya  
ekādas'; ānisaṃsā pāṭikankhā| katame ekādasa? sukhaṃ  
supati, sukhaṃ paṭibujjhati, na pāpakaṃ supinaṃ passati,  
manussānaṃ piyo hoti, amasnussānaṃ piyo hoti, devatā  
rakkhanti, nāssa aggi vā visaṃ vā satthaṃ vā kamati,  
tuvaṭaṃ7 cittaṃ samadhiyati, mukhavaṇṇo pasīdati, asam-  
mūḷho kālaṃ karoti, uttarim appaṭivijjhanto brahmalokūpago

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, karma-liberation_

concepts: [The benefits of loving-kindness](../concepts/benefits-of-metta.md) · practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p306 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p306"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

With the eleventh benefit ended, the text closes the reflection: "if you do not calm this mind you will be outside these benefits." If even so the mind cannot be calmed, the meditator should analyse into the elements (dhātuvinibbhoga): "with whom are you angry, in being angry with this person? With his head hair, body hair, nails... urine? Or with the earth element in the hair and the rest, or the water, fire or air element? Or of the five aggregates, twelve sense bases and eighteen elements by which he is called 'this venerable so-and-so', are you angry with the form aggregate, or feeling, perception, formations or consciousness? With the eye base, the form base ... the mind base, the mental-object base? With the eye element ... the mind-consciousness element?" For one who does this analysis, anger has no place to stand, like a mustard seed on the tip of an awl or a picture in the air.

> hotī ti|1 sace tvaṃ idaṃ cittaṃ na nibbāpessasi, imehi  
ānisaṃsehi paribāhiro bhavissasī ti|  
     evam pi nibbāpetuṃ asakkontena pana dhātuvinibbhogo  
kātabbo:- kathaṃ, ambho pabbajita! tvaṃ pana etassa  
kujjhamāno kassa kujjhasi, kiṃ kesānaṃ kujjhasi, udāhu  
lomānaṃ, nakhānaṃ | | | muttassa kujjhasi, athavā pana  
kesādisu pathavīdhātuyā kujjhasi, āpodhātuyā, tejodhātuyā,  
vāyodhātuyā kujjhasi, ye vā pañcakkhandhe, dvādasāyata-  
nāni, aṭṭhārasa dhātuyo upādāya ayam āyasmā itthannāmo  
ti vuccati, tesu kiṃ rūpakkhandhassa kujjhasi, udāhu ve-  
danā-, saññā-, sankhāra-, viññāṇakkhandhassa kujjhasi,  
kiṃ vā cakkhāyatanassa kujjhasi, kiṃ rūpāyatanassa kuj-  
jhasi |||pe||| kiṃ manāyatanassa kujjhasi, kiṃ dhammā-  
yatanassa kujjhasi, kiṃ vā cakkhudhātuyā kujjhasi, kiṃ  
rūpadhātuyā, kiṃ cakkhuviññāṇadhātuyā |||pe||| kiṃ  
manodhātuyā, kiṃ dhammadhātuyā, kiṃ manoviññāṇadhā-  
tuyā ti| evaṃ hi dhātuvinibbhogaṃ karoto āragge sāsa-  
passa viya ākāse cittakammassa viya ca kodhassa patiṭṭhā-

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p306 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p306-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence moderate

If the meditator cannot do the analysis into elements, he should practise sharing gifts (dānasaṃvibhāga): give what is his own to the other and accept what is the other's; if the other has a broken livelihood and requisites unfit for use, only his own is to be given. By doing this his resentment toward that person is surely calmed; and the other person's anger too, even if it has followed him from a past birth, subsides at that very moment, as did that of the great elder at Cittalapabbata monastery who received the bowl given by the alms-food elder whose lodging had three times been taken from him, with the words "venerable sir, this bowl worth eight kahāpaṇas was given me by my mother, the lay-woman, as a lawful gain: let the great lay-woman gain merit." The text says such is the power of giving, and quotes a verse: giving tames the untamed, giving accomplishes everything; by giving and kind speech people rise up and bow.

> dhātuvinibbhogaṃ pana kātuṃ asakkontena dānasaṃ-  
vibhāgo kātabbo, attano santakaṃ parassa dātabbaṃ,  
parassa santakaṃ attanā gahetabbaṃ| sace pana paro  
bhinnājīvo hoti aparibhogārahaparikkhāro, attano santa-  
kam eva dātabbaṃ| tass'; evaṃ karoto ekanten'; eva tasmiṃ  
puggale āghāto vūpasammati| itarassa ca atītajātito paṭṭhāya  
anubandho pi kodho taṃ khaṇañ yeva vūpasammati, citta-  
lapabbatavihāre tikkhattuṃ vuṭṭhāpitasenāsanena2 piṇḍa-  
pātikattherena3: ayaṃ, bhante, aṭṭha-kahāpaṇagghanako  
patto mama mātarā upāsikāya dinno dhammiyalābho mahā-  
upāsikāya puññalābhaṃ karothā ti vatvā dinnaṃ pattam  
laddhamahātherassa viya| evaṃ mahānubhāvaṃ h'; etaṃ  
dānaṃ nāma| vuttam pi c'; etaṃ:-  
          adantadamanaṃ dānaṃ, dānaṃ sabbattha sādhakaṃ;  
          dānena piyavācāya, unnamanti namanti cā ti4|

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: beginner · types: practice, ethics, narrative_

practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md), [Recollection of one's generosity](../practices/caganussati.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p307 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p307"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

When resentment toward the enemy has subsided, the mind occurs with loving-kindness toward him as toward the dear, the very dear friend and the neutral. Then, by developing loving-kindness again and again toward himself, the dear person, the neutral person and the enemy, and making the mind equal toward these four, he should break down the barriers (sīmasambheda). The mark: suppose he sits in one place with these three (dear, neutral, enemy) and himself as the fourth, and bandits come and say "give us one bhikkhu" for a blood-offering; if he thinks "let them take so-and-so", the barrier is not broken; even if he thinks "take me, not these three", it is not broken, because he wishes the good of one and not of the others. When among the four he sees no one to give the bandits and his mind is equal toward himself and the three, the barrier is broken. The ancients' verse: so long as he sees a difference among himself, the dear, the neutral and the hostile, though his mind is of good will toward beings, he is not called skilled in loving-kindness, one who gains it at will (nikāmalābhī); when the four barriers are broken down for the bhikkhu, he pervades the whole world with its gods with loving-kindness equally, and he whose barrier is no longer known is greatly better than the former. At the moment the barrier is broken the meditator gains the sign and access; then, developing that sign, he reaches absorption with little trouble in the way said for the earth kasiṇa: the first jhāna accompanied by loving-kindness (with five factors abandoned, five possessed, good in three ways, ten marks) and, continuing, the second and third in the fourfold count (the second, third and fourth in the fivefold) (continued on p. 308).

> tass'; evaṃ verīpuggale vūpasantapaṭighassa yathā piyā-  
tippiyasahāyakamajjhattesu, evaṃ tasmim pi mettāvasena  
cittaṃ pavattati|  
     athā 'nena punapnaṃ mettāyantena attani piyapuggale  
majjhatte verīpuggale ti catūsu janesu samacittataṃ sampā-  
dentena sīmasambhedo kātabbo| tass'; idaṃ lakkhaṇaṃ:-  
sace imasmiṃ puggale piya-majjhatta-verīhi saddhiṃ atta-  
catutthe ekasmiṃ padese nisinne corā āgantvā:- bhante,  
ekaṃ bhikkhuṃ amhākaṃ dethā ti vatvā:- kiṃ kāraṇā ti  
vutt'; etaṃ: māretvā gaḷalohitaṃ gahetvā balikaraṇatthā-  
vā ti vadeyyuṃ,1 tatra c'; eso bhikkhu: asukaṃ vā asukaṃ  
vā gaṇhantū ti cinteyya, akato va hoti sīmasambhedo| sace  
pi:- maṃ gaṇhantu, mā ime tayo ti pi cinteyya, akato va  
hoti sīmasambhedo| kasmā? yassa yassa hi gahaṇam  
icchati, tassa tassa ahitesī hoti, itaresaṃ yeva hitesī hoti-  
yadā pana catunnaṃ janānam antare ekaṃ corānaṃ dātab-  
baṃ na passati attani ca tesu ca tīsu janesu samam eva  
cittaṃ pavatteti, kato hoti sīmasambhedo| ten āhu  
porāṇā:-  
          attani hitamajjhatte ahite ca catubbidhe  
          yadā passati nānattaṃ hitacitto va pāṇinaṃ,  
          na nikāmalābhī mettāya kusalī ti pavuccati  
          yadā catasso sīmāyo sambhinnā honti bhikkhuno|  
          samaṃ pharati mettāya, sabbaṃ lokaṃ sadevakaṃ,  
          mahāviseso purimena yassa sīmā na ñāyatī ti2|  
     evaṃ sīmāsambhedasamakālam3 eva ca iminā bhikkhunā  
nimittañ ca upacārañ ca laddhaṃ hoti| sīmāsambhede3 pana  
kate tam eva nimittaṃ āsevanto bhāvento bahulīkaronto  
appakasiren'; eva pathavī-kasiṇe vuttanayen'; eva appanaṃ  
pāpuṇāti| ettāvatā 'nena adhigataṃ hoti pañcangavippa-  
hīnaṃ pañcangasamannāgataṃ tividhakalyāṇaṃ dasalak-  
khaṇasampannaṃ paṭhamajjhānaṃ mettāsahagataṃ| adhi-  
gate ca tasmiṃ tad eva nimittaṃ āsevanto bhāvento bahu-  
līkaronto anupubbena catukkanaye dutiyatatiyajjhānāni,

concepts: [The first jhāna](../concepts/first-jhana.md), [The four divine abidings (brahmavihāra)](../concepts/four-brahmaviharas.md) · practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md) · obstacles: [Resentment and aversion (paṭigha, āghāta)](../obstacles/patigha.md)

### 9.p308 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p308"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The end of the barrier-breaking account and the start of the direction-pervading formula: the jhānas from the same sign: he reaches the second and third in the fourfold count (the second, third and fourth in the fivefold). Then, "by any of the first jhāna and the rest he abides pervading one direction with a mind accompanied by loving-kindness; likewise the second, the third, the fourth; thus above, below, across, everywhere and to all as to himself, he abides pervading the whole world with a mind accompanied by loving-kindness, abundant, exalted, measureless, without hate, without ill will."

> pañcakanaye dutiyatatiyacatutthajjhānāni ca pāpuṇāti| so  
hi paṭhamajjhānādīnaṃ aññataravasena mettāsahagatena  
cetasā ekaṃ disaṃ pharitvā viharati| tathā dutiyaṃ, tathā  
tatiyaṃ, tathā catutthaṃ| iti uddham adho tiriyaṃ sabbadhi  
sabbattatāya sabbāvantaṃ lokaṃ mettāsahagatena cetasā vi-  
pulena mahaggatena appamāṇena averena avyāpajjena pharit-  
vā viharati|1

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: practice_

concepts: [The four divine abidings (brahmavihāra)](../concepts/four-brahmaviharas.md) · practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md), [Development of the four divine abidings (brahmavihāra)](../practices/brahmavihara-bhavana.md)

### 9.p308 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p308-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence moderate

Glosses on the formula. "Accompanied by loving-kindness" means endowed with loving-kindness; "with the mind" is consciousness; "one direction" is said by way of pervading the beings included in one direction, taking first the beings delimited (in that direction); "having pervaded" is having touched (with it) and made it the object; "abides" is to keep going a posture of abiding sustained by the divine abiding. "Likewise the second" means after pervading any one direction, in the same way the second, third and fourth. "Above" is in the same way as "the earlier direction"; "below" and "across" are downward and the intermediate directions; so the mind accompanied by loving-kindness, like a horse in a horse-ring, goes and returns in all directions. This much shows the pervading of each direction, delimited (odhiso). "Everywhere" and the rest show the undelimited (anodhiso): "everywhere" is everywhere; "to all as to himself" is with the state of oneself in all the classes (the low, middling, high, friend, enemy, neutral) not making the distinction "this is another being"; or, "with the whole part of the mind", not distracted outward in the least.

> ettha ca mettāsahagatenā ti mettāya samannāgatena|  
     cetasā ti cittena|  
     ekaṃ disan ti ekam ekissā disāya paṭhamapariggahitaṃ  
sattam upādāya ekadisāpariyāpannasattapharaṇavasena2 vut-  
taṃ|  
     pharitvā ti phusitvā ārammaṇaṃ katvā|  
     viharatī ti brahmavihārādhiṭṭhitaṃ iriyāpathavihāraṃ  
pavatteti|  
     tathā dutiyan ti yathā puratthimādisu disāsu yaṃ kiñci  
ekaṃ disaṃ pharitvā viharati, tath'; eva tadanantaraṃ  
dutiyaṃ tatiyaṃ catutthañ cā ti attho|  
     iti uddhan ti eten'; eva nayena purimaṃ disan ti vuttaṃ  
hoti|  
     adho tiriyan ti adhodisam pi tiriyaṃ disam pi| evam evaṃ3  
tattha ca adho ti heṭṭhā| tiriyan ti anudisāsu| evaṃ sabba-  
disāsu assamaṇḍale assam iva mettāsahagataṃ cittaṃ  
sāreti pi paccāsāreti pī ti| ettāvatā ekamekaṃ4 disaṃ parig-  
gahetvā odhiso mettāpharaṇaṃ dassitaṃ|  
     sabbadhī ti ādi pana anodhiso dassanatthaṃ vuttaṃ|  
     tattha sabbadhī ti sabbattha|  
     sabbattatāyā ti sabbesu hīnamajjhimukkaṭṭhamittasapat-  
tamajjhattādippabhedesu attatāya| ayaṃ parasatto ti  
vibhāvaṃ akatvā attasamatāyā ti vuttaṃ hoti| athavā  
sabbattatāyā ti sabbena cittabhāgena5 īsakam pi bahi avik-  
khipamāno ti vuttaṃ hoti|

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: practice_

concepts: [The four divine abidings (brahmavihāra)](../concepts/four-brahmaviharas.md) · practices: [Loving-kindness (mettā-bhāvanā)](../practices/metta-bhavana.md)

### 9.p318 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p318"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Word-explanations and the marks of the four divine abidings. Loving-kindness (mettā) is friendliness; compassion (karuṇā) is what makes the hearts of the good tremble when others suffer, or destroys others' suffering, or is extended over the suffering; gladness (muditā) is gladness in others' good or the mere being glad; equanimity (upekkhā) is looking on with the giving up of the effort of "may they be free from hate" and coming to neutrality. By mark, function, manifestation and proximate cause: loving-kindness has as mark the occurring in the aspect of welfare; as function, bringing welfare; as manifestation, removing resentment (āghāta); as proximate cause, seeing the agreeable side of beings. Its success is the stilling of ill will; its failure is the arising of affection (sineha).

> vā esā pavattī ti pi mettā| paradukkhe sati sādhūnaṃ hada  
yakampanaṃ karotī ti karuṇā| kiṇāti vā paradukkhaṃ,  
hiṃsati vināsetī ti karuṇā| kiriyati vā dukkhitesu phara-  
ṇāvasena pasāriyatī ti karuṇā| modanti tāya taṃ-samangino,  
sayaṃ vā modati, modanamattam eva vā tan ti muditā  
averā hontū ti ādivyāpārappahānena majjhattabhāvūpaga-  
manena ca upekkhatī ti upekkhā|  
     lakkhaṇādito pan'; ettha hitākārappavatti-lakkhaṇamettā,  
hitūpasaṃhārarasā, āghātavinayapaccupaṭṭhānā, sattānaṃ

### 9.p318 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p318-2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Compassion has as mark the occurring in the aspect of removing suffering; as function, not bearing others' suffering; as manifestation, non-cruelty; as proximate cause, seeing the helplessness of those overcome by suffering; its success is the stilling of cruelty, its failure the arising of sorrow. Gladness has as mark rejoicing; as function, non-envy; as manifestation, the removal of discontent; as proximate cause, seeing beings' success; success is the stilling of discontent, failure the arising of exhilaration (pahāsa). Equanimity has as mark the occurring in the aspect of neutrality toward beings; as function, seeing equality among beings; as manifestation, the stilling of resentment and favour; as proximate cause, seeing beings as owners of their kamma ("by whose wish will they be happy, or be freed from suffering, or not fall from the prosperity they have reached?"); success is the stilling of resentment and favour, failure the arising of the ignorant indifference of the household life. The common purpose of all four is pleasant abiding in insight and success in existence; the special purpose is the destruction of ill will and so on: loving-kindness for ill will, the others for cruelty, discontent and lust. The text quotes: "this is the escape from ill will, namely the liberation of mind by loving-kindness" and so for compassion (cruelty), gladness (discontent) and equanimity (lust). Each of the four has two enemies, near and far (continued on p. 319).

> dukkhāpanayanākārappavatti-lakkhaṇā karuṇā, paraduk-

h'; etaṃ, āvuso, rāgassa yadidaṃ upekkhā-cetovimuttī ti1|

### 9.p319 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-9-p319"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The near and far enemies (paccatthika). Lust (rāga) is the near enemy of loving-kindness, being like it in seeing virtues; it finds an opening quickly, so loving-kindness must be well guarded from it; ill will (vyāpāda) is the far enemy, like a man's enemy who lives hidden in mountain thickets, alike by being unlike, so one develops loving-kindness fearlessly from it; it is impossible for one who has loving-kindness to be angry. For compassion, the household-based (gehasita) displeasure that arises in one who regards non-attainment of desirable forms as non-attainment, or recalls past attainment lost and changed, is the near enemy, being like it in seeing what has gone wrong; cruelty is the far enemy. For gladness, household-based gladness at attainment is the near enemy; discontent (arati) is the far enemy. For equanimity, the household-based equanimity of the ignorant fool, who on seeing a form has an indifference that does not go beyond the form, is the near enemy, since it is like it in not considering fault and merit; lust and resentment are the far enemies.

### 10 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-10"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

The formless attainments are called imperturbable and peaceful liberations because the coarser factors are abandoned; Āḷāra Kālāma, in a formless attainment, neither saw nor heard five hundred carts passing close by.

> arūpasamāpattīnaṃ āneñjatā santavimokkhatā ca vuttā. āḷāro ca kāḷāmo arūpasamāpanno pañcamattāni sakaṭasatāni nissāya nissāya atikkamantāni neva addasa, na pana saddaṃ assosī ti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: practice, powers-experiences_

### 10 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-10-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The first formless state is reached by removing the kasiṇa (except the space kasiṇa) and attending to the space left, 'space is infinite'; the second takes the consciousness pervading that space as 'consciousness is infinite'; the third attends to the absence of that consciousness, 'there is nothing'; the fourth is so subtle that it can be called neither perception nor non-perception.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: practice, consciousness-mind_

### 11 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-11"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The perception of repulsiveness in nutriment reviews food in ten aspects (going for it, seeking, using, secretions, receptacle, what is uncooked, cooked, its fruit, outflow, smearing); it brings access and removes craving for flavours.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: all · types: practice, body-layers_

### 11 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-11-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

Defining the four elements — in brief for one of quick understanding, or by forty-two aspects for one of slower understanding — the meditator sees the body as mere earth, water, fire and air; he penetrates voidness and loses the perception of a being, and reaches access concentration.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation, knowledge · stage: intermediate · types: practice, body-layers_

### 11 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-11-3"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The benefits of developing concentration are a pleasant abiding here and now, a basis for insight, the direct knowledges, a distinguished existence (rebirth in the Brahma worlds), and the attainment of cessation.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: all · types: practice, karma-liberation_

### 12 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-12"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Supernormal power is of ten kinds: by resolve, by transformation, mind-made, by intervention of knowledge, by intervention of concentration, the noble power, born of kamma-result, of the meritorious, accomplished by lore, and success through right application.

> katamā dasa iddhiyo? adhiṭṭhānā iddhi, vikubbanā iddhi, manomayā iddhi, ñāṇavipphārā iddhi, samādhivipphārā iddhi, ariyā iddhi, kammavipākajā iddhi, puññavato iddhi, vijjāmayā iddhi …

### 12 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-12-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The kinds of supernormal power (being one, becoming many; appearing and vanishing; passing through walls; diving into earth; walking on water; travelling through the air; touching the sun and moon; wielding bodily mastery as far as the Brahma world) are developed from the fourth jhāna based on the kasiṇas, after taming the mind in fourteen ways.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: powers-experiences, practice_

### 13 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-13"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Six kinds of people recollect past lives: sectarians, ordinary disciples, great disciples, chief disciples, paccekabuddhas and Buddhas; the sectarians recall only forty eons because their understanding is weak, lacking the definition of mentality-materiality; the others recall progressively more, the Buddhas without limit.

> imaṃ hi pubbe nivāsaṃ cha janā anussaranti; titthiyā, pakatisāvakā, mahāsāvakā, aggasāvakā, paccekabuddhā, buddhā ti. tattha titthiyā cattālisaṃ yeva kappe anussaranti, na tato paraṃ. kasmā? dubbalapaññattā; tesaṃ hi nāmarūpaparicchedavirahitattā dubbalā paññā hoti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: powers-experiences, karma-liberation_

terms: [pubbenivāsānussati](../terms/pubbenivasanussati.md) · concepts: [The six direct knowledges](../concepts/six-abhinnas.md)

### 13 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-13-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

An eon has four incalculables — contraction, remaining contracted, expansion, remaining expanded; there are three contractions — by water, fire and wind — whose upper limits are the Ābhassara, Subhakiṇha and Vehapphala heavens; when the eon contracts by fire, everything below the Ābhassara is burnt.

> tattha tayo saṃvaṭṭā: āposaṃvaṭṭo, tejosaṃvaṭṭo, vāyosaṃvaṭṭo ti. tisso saṃvaṭṭasīmā:- abhassarā, subhakiṇhā, vehapphalā ti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: cosmic · path: general · stage: all · types: world-fate_

terms: [kappa](../terms/kappa.md) · concepts: [World-cycles (kappa) and their destructions](../concepts/world-cycles.md)

### 13 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-13-3"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

The divine eye, seeing beings passing away and reappearing, is a cause of purification of view: one who sees only the passing away holds annihilationism, one who sees only the reappearance holds the view of a new being's appearance; the Buddha's disciples see both and so transcend both views.

> yo hi cutimattam eva passati, na upapātaṃ, so ucchedadiṭṭhiṃ gaṇhāti. yo upapātamattam eva passati, na cutiṃ, so navasattapātubhāvadiṭṭhiṃ gaṇhāti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: powers-experiences, death-dying_

### 14 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-14"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Understanding is insight-knowledge associated with wholesome consciousness; it understands in a way distinct from perceiving and cognizing.

> kusalacittasampayuttaṃ vipassanāñāṇaṃ paññā. ken' aṭṭhena paññā ti pajānanaṭṭhena paññā.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: advanced · types: consciousness-mind_

`skeleton` · confidence high

The analytical knowledge of language is knowledge of the language of intrinsic nature, the Māgadhī, the root language of all beings: on hearing a term one knows whether it is the language of intrinsic nature or not.

> sabhāvaniruttiyā māgadhikāya sabbasattānaṃ mūlabhāsāya pabhedagataṃ ñāṇaṃ niruttipaṭisambhidā.

### 14 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-14-3"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

The aggregate of formations is shown as the mental factors associated with each kind of consciousness: e.g. with the first unwholesome consciousness rooted in greed arise thirteen factors named in the text and four 'or-whatever' factors, seventeen in all.

> lobhamūle paṭhamākusalasampayuttā tāva niyatā sarūpena āgatā terasa, ye-vā-pana-kā cattāro ti sattarasa.

### 14 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-14-4"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

Consciousness is analysed into eighty-nine kinds (twenty-one wholesome, twelve unwholesome, thirty-six resultant, twenty functional), and matter into the four primaries and twenty-four kinds of derived matter, including the heart-basis as the support of mind.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: advanced · types: consciousness-mind, body-layers_

### 14.p468 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-14-p468"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter XIV, on the aggregate of formations, continues with the unwholesome. In the first unwholesome consciousness rooted in greed there are seventeen: thirteen given in their own form (contact, volition, applied thought, sustained thought, rapture, energy, life faculty, concentration, shamelessness, fearlessness of wrongdoing, greed, delusion, wrong view) and four "whatever" factors (zeal, resolve, restlessness, attention). Shamelessness (ahirika) has as its mark absence of disgust at bodily misconduct and the like, or lack of conscience; fearlessness of wrongdoing (anottappa) has as its mark absence of dread of the same, or lack of fear. Greed (lobha) is that by which one is greedy, or which is greedy, or mere greed: its mark is grasping an object, like a monkey-lime; its function is sticking, like a piece of meat thrown into a hot pan; its manifestation is non-relinquishing, like the dye of lamp-black oil; its proximate cause is seeing enjoyment in things that fetter. Craving, growing as a river, is to be regarded as a fast-flowing river carrying one to the sea, taking one to the states of loss. Delusion (moha) has as its mark blindness of consciousness or lack of knowledge; as function, non-penetration or concealing the nature of the object; as manifestation, wrong practice or darkness; as proximate cause, unwise attention; it is the root of all that is unwholesome.

> akusalesu, lobhamūle paṭhamākusalasampayuttā tāva  
niyatā sarūpena āgatā terasa, ye-vā-pana-kā cattāro ti  
sattarasa|  
     tattha  
             phasso samādhi  
             cetanā ahirikaṃ  
             vitakko anottappaṃ  
             vicāro lobho  
             pīti moho  
             viriyaṃ micchādiṭṭhī  
             jīvitaṃ  
ti ime sarūpena āgatā terasa|  
             chando uddhaccaṃ  
             adhimokkho manasikāro  
ti ime ye-vā-pana-kā cattāro|

na ottappatī ti anottappaṃ| tesu ahirikaṃ kāyaduccaritādīhi  
ajigucchanalakkhaṇaṃ, alajjālakkhaṇaṃ vā, anottappaṃ  
teh'; eva asārajjalakkhaṇaṃ, anuttāsalakkhaṇaṃ vā| ayam  
ettha sankhepo| vitthāro pana hirottappānaṃ vuttapaṭi-  
pakkhavasena veditabbo|  
     lubbhanti tena, sayaṃ vā lubbhati, lubbhanamattam eva  
vā tan ti lobho| muyhanti tena, sayaṃ vā muyhati, muyha-  
namattam eva vā tan ti moho|  
     tesu lobho ārammaṇagahaṇalakkhaṇo, makkaṭālepo viya;  
abhisangaraso, tattakapāle khittaṃ maṃsapesi viya; aparic-  
cāgapaccupaṭṭhāno, telañjanarāgo viya; saṃyojanīyadham-  
mesu assādadassanapadaṭṭhāno| taṇhā nadībhāvena vaḍ-  
ḍhamāno, sīghasotā nadī iva mahāsamuddaṃ, apāyam eva  
gahetvā gacchatī ti daṭṭhabbo|  
     moho cittassa andhabhāvalakkhaṇo, aññāṇalakkhaṇo  
vā; asampaṭivedharaso, ārammaṇasabhāvacchādanaraso vā;  
asammāpaṭipattipaccupaṭṭhāno, andhakārapaccupaṭṭhāno vā;  
ayonisomanasikārapadaṭṭhāno, sabbākusalānaṃ mūlan ti  
daṭṭhabbo|  
     micchā passanti tāya, sayaṃ vā micchā passati, micchā-

terms: [ahirika](../terms/ahirika.md), [anottappa](../terms/anottappa.md) · obstacles: [Greed (lobha)](../obstacles/lobha.md), [Delusion (moha)](../obstacles/moha.md)

### 14.p469 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-14-p469"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Wrong view (micchādiṭṭhi) is that by which one sees wrongly, or which sees wrongly, or mere wrong seeing: its mark is unwise insistence; its function is clinging; its manifestation is wrong adherence; its proximate cause is the wish not to see the noble ones and the like; it is to be regarded as the most blameworthy. Restlessness (uddhacca) is agitated state: its mark is non-calming; its function is unsteadiness, like water stirred by wind; its manifestation is bewilderment, like a flag fluttering in the wind or like ashes thrown up by a blow on a stone; its proximate cause is unwise attention to the non-calming of the mind; it is to be regarded as distraction of consciousness. The other factors are as stated for the unwholesome. The second unwholesome consciousness is like the first, but prompted and with sloth-torpor indeterminate. Sloth (thīna) is the sinking, torpor (middha) the drowsing: sloth has as its mark lack of energy and as function dispelling energy, as manifestation sinking; torpor has as mark unworkableness, as function smothering, as manifestation drooping or drowsy sleep; for both the proximate cause is unwise attention to discontent, lazy stretching and the like. Conceit (māna), indeterminate in the third and fourth, has as mark haughtiness, as function self-promotion, as manifestation vain-glory, as proximate cause greed dissociated from view; it is to be regarded as madness. The fifth to eighth are given in the same manner without wrong view or with joy left out.

### 14.p470 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-14-p470"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

In the two hate-rooted consciousnesses, the first has eleven fixed factors (contact, volition, applied thought, sustained thought, energy, life faculty, concentration, shamelessness, fearlessness of wrongdoing, hate, delusion), four "whatever" factors, and three indeterminate (envy, avarice, remorse), eighteen in all. Hate (dosa) is that by which one hates or which hates or mere hating: its mark is ferocity, like a struck snake; its function is to spread, like a fall of poison, or to burn its own support, like a forest fire; its manifestation is fury, like a foe who has found his chance; its proximate cause is the grounds of resentment; it is to be regarded as urine mixed with poison. Envy (issā) is envying: its mark is jealousy of others' success; its function is dissatisfaction with it; its manifestation is turning away from it; its proximate cause is others' success; it is to be regarded as a fetter. Avarice (macchariya) is the state of the miser: its mark is hiding one's own success, gained or to be gained; its function is not bearing to share it with others; its manifestation is shrinking or a bitter mood; its proximate cause is one's own success; it is to be regarded as an ugly state of mind. Remorse (kukkucca) is the state of having done wrong: its mark is later regret; its function is sorrow over what has been done and not done; its manifestation is remorse; its proximate cause is what has been done and not done; it is to be regarded as slavery.

> pana dvīsu paṭhamasampayuttā tāva niyatā sarūpena āgatā

              adhimokkho manasikāro  
ti ime ye-vā-panakā cattāro|  
              issā kukkuccan

### 14.p471 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-14-p471"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence moderate

The delusion-rooted consciousnesses. With doubt: the eleven are contact, volition, applied thought, sustained thought, energy, life faculty, consciousness-steadiness (cittaṭṭhiti), shamelessness, fearlessness of wrongdoing, delusion and doubt, plus restlessness and attention, thirteen. "Consciousness-steadiness" is weak concentration: only the steadiness of occurrence. Doubt (vicikicchā) is the absence of cure: its mark is uncertainty; its function is wavering; its manifestation is indecision or taking hold of no one side; its proximate cause is unwise attention; it is to be regarded as an obstacle to practice. With restlessness, the factors are those of the doubt-consciousness without doubt, twelve; since doubt is absent, resolve arises, and with it thirteen and concentration is stronger. Then, among the indeterminate, the resultant consciousnesses are of two kinds, root-less and with roots. In the root-less, eye-consciousness resultant on the wholesome or unwholesome has four factors in their own form (contact, volition, life faculty, consciousness-steadiness) and one "whatever" (attention), five; ear, nose, tongue and body consciousness are the same; the mind-element has these plus applied thought, sustained thought and resolve, eight. The rooted resultants in the sense sphere are like the eight wholesome sense-sphere ones, except that compassion and gladness (among the indeterminate) are absent in resultants, since resultants of the sense sphere have a single limited object.

### 16 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-16"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

In the ultimate sense all the truths are void of an experiencer, a doer, one who is extinguished and a goer: 'For there is suffering, but none who suffers; doing exists although there is no doer; there is extinction but no extinguished person; although there is a path, there is no goer.'

> dukkham eva hi na koci dukkhito, kārako na kiriyā va vijjati, atthi nibbuti, na nibbuto pumā, maggam atthi, gamako na vijjatī ti.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: absolute · path: knowledge · stage: advanced · types: ultimate, karma-liberation_

terms: [anattā](../terms/anatta.md), [nibbāna](../terms/nibbana.md) · concepts: [Nibbāna in the Abhidhamma and commentaries](../concepts/nibbana-abhidhamma.md) · disputes: [Is nibbāna a true self (attā) or not-self?](../debates/nibbana-atta-or-anatta.md)

### 16 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-16-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

Against an opponent who holds nibbāna to be non-existent or mere destruction, the Visuddhimagga argues that nibbāna exists — it is apprehended by the right means (the path), it is not mere absence, not created, without beginning or end, one and unconditioned.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: polemical · path: knowledge · stage: advanced · types: ultimate, dispute_

terms: [nibbāna](../terms/nibbana.md), [asaṅkhata](../terms/asankhata.md) · concepts: [Nibbāna in the Abhidhamma and commentaries](../concepts/nibbana-abhidhamma.md)

### 17 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-17"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

The ancients said that four things are hard to see and hard to teach — truth, the being, rebirth-linking and the structure of conditions; so Buddhaghosa, about to comment on the structure of conditions, says he finds no footing, like one plunged into the ocean, and relies on the teaching's many methods and the unbroken way of the former teachers.

> saccaṃ satto paṭisandhi paccayākāram eva ca, duddasā caturo dhammā desetuñ ca sudukkarā ti … vattukāmo ahaṃ ajja paccayākāravaṇṇanaṃ patiṭṭhaṃ nādhigacchāmi ajjhogaḷho va sāgaraṃ.

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: knowledge · stage: advanced · types: karma-liberation, teacher-transmission_

concepts: [Dependent origination in the Abhidhamma and commentaries](../concepts/dependent-origination-abhidhamma.md)

### 17 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-17-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Dependent origination is a wheel with three links, four groups and twenty spokes (modes), turning without rest in three rounds: five past causes, five present results, five present causes, five future results; the rounds are of kamma (formations, becoming), defilement (ignorance, craving, clinging) and result.

> vīsati ākārāraṃ atīte hetavo pañca idāni phalapañcakaṃ idāni hetavo pañca āyatiṃ phalapañcakan ti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: causal · path: knowledge · stage: all · types: karma-liberation_

terms: [paṭiccasamuppāda](../terms/paticcasamuppada.md), [vaṭṭa](../terms/vatta.md) · concepts: [Dependent origination in the Abhidhamma and commentaries](../concepts/dependent-origination-abhidhamma.md) · disputes: [Does dependent origination describe rebirth across three lives, or a process in present experience?](../debates/dependent-origination-three-lives-or-present.md)

### 17 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-17-3"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Because formations and the rest occur through ignorance and the other causes, the wheel of becoming is devoid of a maker — such as a Brahmā imagined as the creator of the round — and of an experiencer — a self imagined as the one who feels pleasure and pain.

> brahmā mahābrahmā seṭṭho sajitā ti evaṃ parikappitena brahmādinā vā saṃsārassa kārakena … attanā vā sukhadukkhānaṃ vedakena rahitaṃ, iti kārakavedakarahitan ti veditabbaṃ.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: polemical · path: knowledge · stage: advanced · types: karma-liberation, dispute_

terms: [anattā](../terms/anatta.md) · disputes: [Is there a self (ātman, puruṣa, jīva) — an enduring conscious subject distinct from body and changing cognitions — or only a stream of impersonal factors designated 'person'?](../debates/is-there-a-self.md), [Is there an Īśvara — a Lord who makes the world and dispenses the fruits of action? Is he proved by reason, known only from scripture, a special puruṣa for meditation, or not to be accepted at all?](../debates/isvara.md)

### 17 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-17-4"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

At the time of death, the kamma, a sign of the kamma, or a sign of the destination (such as the flames of hell, or a mother's womb, or celestial mansions) presents itself at the mind door; after the last cognitive series the death consciousness arises, and on its cessation the rebirth-linking consciousness arises taking that same object.

> maraṇamañce nipannassa yathūpacitaṃ pāpakammaṃ vā kammanimittaṃ vā manodvāre āpātham āgacchati, taṃ ārabbha uppannāya tadārammaṇapariyosānāya javanavīthiyā anantaraṃ bhavaṅgavisayaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā cuticittaṃ uppajjati.

_level: conventional · standpoint: causal · path: general · stage: all · types: death-dying, karma-liberation_

terms: [kamma-nimitta](../terms/kamma-nimitta.md), [gati-nimitta](../terms/gati-nimitta.md), [cuti-citta](../terms/cuti-citta.md), [paṭisandhi](../terms/patisandhi.md) · concepts: [Death and rebirth-linking in the Abhidhamma](../concepts/death-and-rebirth-linking.md)

### 17.p569 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-17-p569"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

On the fourfold clinging (upādāna) in the dependent-origination treatment of "feeling as condition for craving": clinging to sensual pleasures (kāmūpādāna), clinging to views (diṭṭhūpādāna), clinging to rites and observances (sīlabbatūpādāna) and clinging to a doctrine of self (attavādūpādāna). Their meanings: the first clings to sensual objects, or is itself sense-desire and clinging; "clinging" (upādāna) is firm grasping, the prefix upa- meaning firm as in upāyāsa; view is itself clinging, or clings to a view (as in "the self and the world are eternal", where a later view clings to an earlier); rites and observances (such as cow-virtue, cow-duty) are clinging in that one adheres to them as "purity thus"; clinging to a doctrine of self is clinging by which they say or take "self". The Dhammasaṅgaṇī's summary is quoted: clinging to sensual pleasures is "in sensual pleasures, sense-desire, sensual lust, sensual delight, craving, sensual affection, fever, swoon, fixation"; in short, the firmness of craving, the later craving grown firm through the earlier as strong support. Some say craving is the stretching out toward an object not yet reached, like a thief's hand groping in the dark, and clinging is the taking of the object reached, like grasping the goods; contentment with little is their opposite. The other three clingings are, in brief, just views; in detail, the firmness of the hundred and eight kinds of craving is clinging to sensual pleasures; the tenfold wrong view is clinging to views; the wrong grasp that purity comes from rites and observances is clinging to rites and observances (quotation given on p. 570).

> tatr'; āyam vibhāvanā:- kāmūpādānaṃ, diṭṭhūpādānam,  
sīlabbatūpādānaṃ, attavādūpādānan ti imāni tāv'; ettha  
cattāri upādānāni| tesaṃ ayam atthavibhāvo:- vatthu-

kāmamucchā kāmajjhosānaṃ: idaṃ vuccati kāmūpādānan

pādānan ti|2 sīlabbatehi suddhī ti parāmasaṃ pana sīlab-  
batūpādānaṃ| yath'; āha:- tattha katamaṃ sīlabbatūpā-

terms: [upādāna](../terms/upadana.md) · concepts: [The four kinds of clinging](../concepts/four-upadanas.md) · obstacles: [The four kinds of clinging (upādāna)](../obstacles/four-upadanas.md)

### 17.p570 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-17-p570"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Continuation. Clinging to rites and observances is quoted as the view "purity by virtue, by observance, by virtue-and-observance"; clinging to a doctrine of self is the twenty-fold view of a personal existence (sakkāyadiṭṭhi): "here the uninstructed ordinary person regards form as self" and so on. This is the summary of the states. On order: the order is threefold: of arising, of abandoning and of teaching. Since in a beginningless round there is no first arising of the defilements, the order of arising is not strictly stated, but in a manner of speaking, in one existence the clinging to a doctrine of self comes first, then adherence to eternalism or annihilationism: one who grasps "the self is eternal" takes up rites and observances for the purity of the self; one who grasps "it is annihilated", indifferent to another world, takes up sense pleasures; then views, rites and sense pleasures. In the order of abandoning, clinging to views and the rest are abandoned first, being to be cut by the stream-entry path, and clinging to sensual pleasures last, being to be cut by the arahat path. In the order of teaching, sense pleasures are taught first for their wide scope and evidence; then views; and of these two, rites and observances (coarse, seen in the behaviour of cows and dogs) before the doctrine of self (subtle).

### 18 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-18"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

To accomplish purification of view, one whose vehicle is calm emerges from any jhāna except the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception and discerns the jhāna factors and associated states by characteristic, function and so on, then their physical basis; one whose vehicle is pure insight discerns the four elements in brief or in detail.

> taṃ sampādetukāmena samathayānikena tāva, ṭhapetvā nevasaññā-nāsaññāyatanaṃ, avasesarūpā rūpāvacarajjhānānaṃ aññatarato vuṭṭhāya vitakkādīni jhānaṅgāni taṃ-sampayuttā ca dhammā lakkhaṇarasādivasena pariggahetabbā.

As 'chariot' is a mere term for axles, wheels, frame and pole assembled in a certain way, and when each part is examined there is no chariot in the ultimate sense, so there is only mentality-materiality, no being, no person.

### 19 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-19"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Purification by overcoming doubt: as a skilled physician seeks the origin of a disease, the meditator seeks the causes and conditions of mentality-materiality and, seeing that it occurred by conditions in the past and will in the future as it does now, abandons doubt about the three periods.

> etass' eva pana nāmarūpassa paccayapariggahaṇena tīsu addhāsu kaṅkhaṃ vitaritvā ṭhitaṃ ñāṇaṃ kaṅkhāvitaraṇavisuddhi nāma.

There is no god, no Brahmā, who makes this round of rebirths; only bare dhammas occur, because of the conjunction of causes and conditions.

> na h' ettha devo brahmā vā saṃsārass' atthi kārako, suddhadhammā pavattanti hetusambhārapaccayā ti.

### 20 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-20"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

The three full understandings are the full understanding as the known (defining mentality-materiality and its conditions), as investigation (comprehension by groups up to contemplation of rise and fall) and as abandoning; path-and-not-path knowledge arises during full understanding as investigation, so comprehension by groups is to be practised first.

### 20 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-20-2"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

In one who has begun insight ten imperfections of insight arise — not in a noble disciple who has reached penetration, nor in one who has given up his meditation subject or is idle, but in one who practises rightly: illumination, knowledge, rapture, tranquillity, bliss, resolution, exertion, assurance, equanimity and attachment.

> katame pana te dasa upakkilesā ti? obhāso, ñāṇaṃ, pīti, passaddhi, sukhaṃ, adhimokkho, paggaho, upaṭṭhānaṃ, upekkhā, nikantī ti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: powers-experiences, practice_

### 20 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-20-3"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence high

Taking these for the path — 'Surely such a thing never arose before; I have reached the path, reached fruition!' — he mistakes what is not the path for the path and leaves his basic meditation subject; the illumination and the rest are called imperfections as bases for imperfection, not as unwholesome, but attachment is both; by grasping with view, conceit and craving they become thirty.

> ettha ca obhāsādayo upakkilesavatthutāya upakkilesā ti vuttā, na akusalattā. nikanti pana upakkileso c' eva upakkilesavatthu ca. vatthuvasen' eva c' ete dasa; gāhavasena pana samatiṃsa honti.

_level: conventional · standpoint: experiential · path: meditation · stage: advanced · types: powers-experiences, practice_

### 20 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-20-4"></a>
`skeleton` · confidence moderate

The wise meditator, when the illumination and the rest arise, discerns them as impermanent, formed, dependently arisen, subject to fall, and knows that these are not the path; the insight knowledge free from imperfections that keeps to its course is the path — this is purification by knowledge and vision of what is and is not the path.

### 20.p633 <a id="tea-visuddhimagga-20-p633"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

The formations appear as ever new and short-lived, like a dewdrop at sunrise, a bubble on water, a line drawn on water, a mustard seed on an awl-tip, a flash of lightning, and as insubstantial and empty like illusion, mirage, dream, fire-wheel, city of spirits, foam and plantain. At this point the meditator has reached the knowledge of arising and passing away, the young insight. The ten imperfections of insight (vipassanūpakkilesa) now arise for one who has begun insight with this young insight; they do not arise in a noble disciple who has penetrated, nor in one who has wrongly practised, given up the subject or is lazy; they arise in a clansman who practises rightly and is earnest. They are: illumination (obhāsa), knowledge (ñāṇa), rapture (pīti), tranquillity (passaddhi), happiness (sukha), resolve (adhimokkha), exertion (paggaha), establishment of mindfulness (upaṭṭhāna), equanimity (upekkhā) and delight (nikanti). The text quotes: "how is the mind seized by restlessness about the Dhamma? For one attending to impermanence, illumination arises; he adverts to it as 'illumination is the Dhamma'; then comes distraction, restlessness; by that restlessness the mind is seized, and he does not know as it is the establishment (of the states) as impermanent, as suffering, as not self." Likewise with knowledge, rapture and the rest, up to delight.

nikanti dhammo ti nikanti āvajjati| tato vikkhepo uddhaccaṃ|  
tena uddhaccena viggahitamānaso aniccato upaṭṭhānaṃ yathā-
